System Architecture: The proposed methodology for the research is shown below

Figure 1. Architecture overview of emotion detection and transforming emotions through playing music

The architecture overview is shown above in figure1. The input of face taken from camera as video frame and is passes to pre-processing model to remove noise, cropped the face and convert image to grayscale and image is resize to 48x48 pixel. The output of pre-processing is given as input to CNN which will further process the image and classify emotions as positive emotions and negative emotions. For this work two negative emotion known as angry and sad and two positive emotion known as happy and neutral. Once negative emotion is detected the system will play music to transform the mood or emotion of person from negative emotion to positive emotion. Once found that emotion is positive then music automatically stops. To train the CNN model, FER-2013 dataset and divided the dataset into testing and training dataset. Histogram of emotions are plotted and intensity is measured for each emotion.

Figure 2. Block diagram of emotion detection and transforming emotions through playing music.

The different modules of the architecture for figure 2 is explained below:

Dataset

For training the model Facial Expression Recognition (FER-2013) dataset is used. It is Kaggle facial expression recognition consisting of 48x48 pixel grayscale images of face. It is a public dataset which can be accessed by everyone. For this research, some Indian faces are also considered for analysing facial expressions and to identify emotions. The dataset contain images for various expressions like neutral, happy, sad, surprise, fear, anger, disgust. To split model, cross validation method known as k-fold cross validation are used on all dataset of images. Cross validation is used to avoid overfitting and underfitting of dataset. For checking quality of model this validation is used. It is a resampling method which is used to evaluate CNN model on dataset. Dataset is splitted into testing and training. Here in k-fold cross validation dataset will be splitted in k number of folds or subsets and then training is performed on all the subsets and one subset(k-1) is used for training model. Here for this model k =10 is used i.e. 9 part of images is used to train data and 1 part of images is used to test data. Cross validation is calculated using Scikit-learn where SciPy python library is used on dataset. Data visualization of dataset for positive emotion and negative emotion are represented below in figure 3 and 4.

Figure 3. Visualization of a number of positive emotions dataset
Figure 4. Visualization of a number of negative emotions dataset
  • • Capture Video Frame Camera is used for video capturing; the facial expression for nth frame is given for pre-processing.
  • • Pre-processing is an important step required in this work where from the captured image only the region containing the face is cropped using Haar feature which helps in cropping facial region. Once the cropped face is achieved it is in the RGB format which has size of (255*255*255) 3 channel for a single pixel. This is difficult to process so the size is reduced by converting the image to grey scale further reducing each pixel to 255 single channel. Obtained Image is ready to be processed but it further needs to match the size of image from which it must be matched so, for that the image is resized to the size of trained image size i.e. 48x48. Further it is processed with CNN.

Convolutional Neural Network

A Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) consists of multiple layered of neural network with a special architecture to identify complex features in data. It is commonly applied to process image data for recognition and classifying image features.

A convolutional neural network consists of input, output layer, and many hidden layer. The hidden layers which is part of CNN constitutes of a number series of convolutional layers which will convolve with a multiplication or other dot product. RELU layer is the activation function, and this is following by another convolutions called pooling layers, fully connected layers are called hidden layers as these inputs and outputs are obscure by the activation function and last convolution layer. The last convolution contains back propagation so that weight the end product can be more accurate.

Working of CNN

All the input images need to pass through multiple convolution layers consisting of Pooling, filters, fully connected layers and then softmax function is applied so that object can be classified within probability ranging within 0 and 1. The figure below represents complete flow of CNN to demonstrate how an input image is processed and classified the objects based on values. Similar to human brain detecting features to identify objects, CNN are able to classify images by identifying features. CNN diagram is represented in figure 5.

Figure 5. CNN with many convolutional layers
Source credit for figure 5: https://towardsdatascience.com/a-comprehensive-guide-to-convolutional-neural-networks-the-eli5-way-3bd2b1164a53

CNN is mainly composed of three layers:

  • • Input layer
  • • Hidden layer
  • • Output layer

Input layer: It will be having width, height and depth for an input image. It represents pixels of matrix.

Hidden layer: It is present between input layer and output layer, here artificial neurons takes pair of weighted inputs and gives an output using an activation function. A CNN consists of many hidden layers and this leads to increase in accuracy in results. Hidden layer consist of layers like convolution, RELU, pooling used for feature learning and for classification fully connected and SOFTMAX layers.

  • For feature learning the layers are defined below:

Convolutional layer: It is building block of CNN and it performs dot product between two matrices where first matrix consists of pair of learnable parameter called kernel and second matrix is limited portion of the receptive field. It is a combined integration of two functions that shows us how one function modifies the other. The input image is represented as 48x48 matrix form with kernel size 3x3 and stride 1. The convolutional layer applies filter to an input image and create output feature maps. Stride is the amount by which the kernel is moved over the image. The output of first convolutional layer i.e. feature map is calculated as given equation 1. The feature map is shown in figure 6 and image processed in convolutional layers are shown in figure 7.

nxn * fxf = n-f+1 x n-f+1 (1)

Here n is 48x48 matrix representation of image as an input, f is 3x3 filter or kernel size.

Thus the output is reduced to size 46x46 and this is feature map.

Figure 6. Processing of image in convolutional layer to get feature map
Figure 7. Image output after convolutional layer

ReLU: ReLU is known as Rectified Linear Unit for a non-linear operation. ReLU layer is applied as an element wise activation function maximum(0,x), that changes negative values into zero. Here there is no change in volume size and hyper parameters are not present. Here no kernel size is needed.

The output of ReLU is calculated using the given formula equation 2

ƒ(x) = maximum(0,x) (2)

Here x is given to neuron as input

The input matrix of an image is passes to ReLU layer and will replace all the negative values of matrix with zeros value as shown below in figure 8.

Figure 8. ReLU operation

Pooling layer: When the image is very big in size, we should decrease the number of trainable parameters. Pooling layers is present between subsequent convolutional layers. It is done for decreasing the spatial dimension of the image and pooling is independent process so, depth of image is not changed. The usual type of pooling layer that will be applying on image matrix are called Max Pooling. We are calculating max pooling using the formula given below equation 3.

(3)

Where W is weight of matrix in cells of an input image, F is size of filter taken as 2x2 & S is Stride taken as 2.

The matrix shown below where max pooling is performed is shown below in figure 9.

Figure 9. Pooling operation

For classification of image in CNN fully connected and SOFTMAX layers are used.

Fully connected and SOFTMAX: All neuron of one layer is connected to other neuron in another layer. To classify the images flattened matrix needs to pass via a fully connected layer. It detects emotion and classifies it as positive or negative emotion. The endmost fully connected layers will use a Softmax activation function to classify features of an image taken as input into different classes depending on the given train dataset. The output is in the form of n dimensional matrix form. The softmax layers have same number of nodes as output layer contain.

Output layer: Hidden layer output is fed to softmax that converts the output of all class to probability score for all class.

Based on emotion detected as negative emotion such as sad, then automatically happy music will be played so that the person mood get transfer to positive emotions such as happy and music stops automatically once it is detected as happy mood. The detected emotion is shown below in figure 10.

Figure 10. Emotion classified as sad after processing through CNN

Tensor flow is an open source framework containing artificial intelligence library that has graphs showing data flow which is used for model building. This leads to development of complex neural networks constituting many layers. This is mostly used to classify, predict and create. When a person listen to happy or soft music then good hormone called Dopamine is released to make person mood pleasant and transforming mood from sad to happy mood. If soft music or classical music is played then person who is in anger mood will feel relax as his blood pressure will become normal and he will have neutral emotion. When angry the person blood pressure and heart rate increases fast due to high level of Adrenaline hormone and so listening to soft music will decrease the level of Adrenaline hormone and so heart rate and blood pressure becomes normal.

Classified emotions: CNN will classify the emotions as negative and positive emotions. For this research we have considered two negative emotion anger and sad and two positive emotion happy and neutral. Once negative emotion is detected then the histogram of negative emotion plotted. Soft music is played and then negative emotions transformed into positive emotions and histogram is plotted. The camera will be continuously monitor the face of person to detect any change in process and this process continue in cycle. For all emotions different bar chart is plotted.

Music dataset: The music dataset is created manually using brute force approach. It consists of happy music and relaxing music. Pygame is used as music library in python and it is responsible for controlling audio. The size of the given dataset is 2 Mb.

The algorithm used for proposed system is given below-

  • Step 1. Collected FER-2013 dataset from Kaggle and also included few Indian faces in the dataset containing 19083 images that are cropped and resized into 48x48 pixel of grayscale. Each face of image is labelled into 4 emotion classes such as anger, sad, happy and neutral. Using k-fold cross validation method, dataset is split into test and train dataset. Music dataset is created manually containing happy and soft music.
  • Step 2. Face images taken from video frame are pre-processed.
  • Step 3. Detecting face in given image.
  • Step 4. Image face is cropped and converted to grayscale images.
  • Step 5. The pipeline checks that all images that are fed into the input layer need to be of form (1, 48, 48) numpy array.
  • Step 6. The numpy array will be passing into the Convolution layer.
  • Step 7. Feature maps are generated by Convolution layer and the output of Convolution layer is of the form (nxn)*(f*f) = (n-f+1)x(n-f+1)
  • Step 8. After this the output is fed to ReLU layer so that it will replace all the negative values of image matrix as zeros. The output of ReLU layer is calculated using formula f(x)=max(0,x).

  • Step 9. Then feature map will be passed to next layer called Pooling for reducing the size of image using a common method called Max Pooling which use 2x2 filter on the feature map that hold only pixel having maximum value. The output of this layer is calculated.

  • Step 10. Neural network Forward propagation and Backward propagation was done while training the network on pixel values.
  • Step 11. The Softmax function gives a probability value for 4 emotion class. The model will classify the emotions as negative and positive based on given training dataset and model will predict the emotions as sad or angry.
  • Step 12. Music is played automatically if the emotion found as negative and music stops when emotion transformed to positive. For all the emotions bar chart and histogram is plotted.

Repeat the process from step 2 to step 11 to continuously monitor the change in emotions in human face. The Sequence diagram for the system is shown below in figure 11:

Figure 11. Emotion detection and transforming emotions through playing music sequence diagram

The code snippet is shown to calculate confusion matrix of a model:

def plot_confusion_matrix(cm, classes, title='Confusion matrix', cmap=plt.cm.Blues):
cm = cm.astype('float') / cm.sum(axis=1)[:, np.newaxis]
plt.figure(figsize=(10,10))
plt.imshow(cm, interpolation='nearest', cmap=cmap)
plt.title(title)
plt.colorbar()
tick_marks = np.arange(len(classes))
plt.xticks(tick_marks, classes, rotation=3)
plt.yticks(tick_marks, classes)
fmt = '.2f'
#mt = 'd'
thresh = cm.max() / 2.
for i, j in itertools.product(range(cm.shape[0]), range(cm.shape[1])):
plt.text(j, i, format(cm[i, j],fmt),
horizontalalignment=”center”,
color=”white” if cm[i, j] > thresh else “black”)
plt.ylabel('True label')
plt.xlabel('Predicted label')
plt.tight_layout()

The code snippet to detect the four emotions using CNN in a live video or a given image:

# dictionary which assigns each label an emotion (alphabetical order)
emotion_dict = {0: “angry”, 1: “sad”, 2: “happy”, 3: “neutral”}
# start the webcam feed
cap = cv2.VideoCapture(0)
count = 0
while True:
# Find haar cascade to draw bounding box around face
ret, frame = cap.read()
if not ret:
break
facecasc = cv2.CascadeClassifier('haarcascade_frontalface_default.xml')
count = count+1 #number of faces detected correctoly
gray = cv2.cvtColor(frame, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY)
faces = facecasc.detectMultiScale(gray,scaleFactor=1.3, minNeighbors=5) #minNeighbors “Howmuch area is taken to remove the noise
#a = 0
for (x, y, w, h) in faces:
cv2.rectangle(frame, (x, y-50), (x+w, y+h+10), (255, 0, 0), 2)
roi_gray = gray[y:y + h, x:x + w]
#Image is croppped from video and processed.
cropped_img = np.expand_dims(np.expand_dims(cv2.resize(roi_gray, (48, 48)), -1), 0)
prediction = model.predict(cropped_img)
probab_dis(prediction[0])

The designed system was trained and tested using Facial Expression Recognition (FER-2013) which is public dataset and it also contain private dataset of Indian faces and this is used for facial expression analysis. The dataset contain images for various expressions like neutral, happy, sad, surprise, fear, anger, disgust.

Figure 13. Example of images in FER-2013 dataset

Figure 13 shows images of dataset with different emotions like sad,angry,happy. For this project only two negative emotions angry and sad are considered and after playing music transformed to positive emotons like happy.

The total size of dataset is 38 mb.It contain 15104 images as training images and 3979 testing images.The images in dataset are pre-processed in various steps like cropping the faces,resize image to 48x48 pixel and convert it into grayscale images and remove noise from images.

To train network, cross validation method known as k-fold cross validation are used on all dataset of images with batch size 64 and 40 epochs.In training dataset there are 3995 angry images, 6279 happy images, neutral 2000 images and 4830 sad images. In testing dataset there are 958 angry images, 1774 happy images and 1247 sad images.

Cross validation is a resampling method which is used to evaluate CNN model on dataset.

In k-fold cross validation, certain value of k are choosen say k= 10 which becomes 10-fold cross validation.The value of k given divides test and train data of dataset.

Scikit-learn is a tool used to calculate cross validation and SciPy is python library used on dataset. Here I have taken k as 10 it means k-1 subset of images i.e. 9 part of images will be used to train data and 1 part of images to test data.

In this system, I have used camera as input where in live video face is detected and frame by frame it is given as input to CNN model. Image frame is pre-processed before passing to CNN model like face is cropped and resize to 48x48 and converted to grayscale images. CNN will processed the image of face by extracting the features and then classify the emotions as negative or positive emotions.

CONCLUSION

In this chapter, Convolutional neural network is used to identify emotion of human face by examining the facial expression of a person. Human face features are eyes, mouth, nose which will be detected and processed by Convolutional Neural Network. If the emotion detected as negative such as sad and anger than automatically music will be played to change the emotions of a person to positive emotions such as happy and neutral. Once emotion detected as positive, automatically music will be stopped. CNN is simple to implement and faster to train. It have good accuracy rate with test data. The dataset used to train CNN is FER- 2013. CNN image processing through all different layer is shown step by step. It also contain algorithm and code snippet for the architecture shown to process and classify the image using CNN. This chapter shows how music affects person emotions by releasing good hormone like Dopamine. There is numerous health benefits while listening to music like decrease in blood pressure, managing pain. The application of this chapter are it can be used as psychotherapy to treat depression, anxiety management, anger management, Alzheimer’s disease to retain memory, Pain management in cancer patients. In this chapter, histogram of positive and negative emotions is plotted. The graph is plotted for different performance measures.

The concepts explained in this chapter can be implemented in future with human brain signals, which can be measured by electroencephalogram (EEG) along with facial expression. Music will be play according to brain signal changes to detect emotions.

Chapter 4

Descriptive Coding, Pattern Coding, and Phonetic Transcription in Phenomenological Research

  • Seda Khadimally
    https://orcid.org/0000-0001-6374-3248
    North American University, USA

ABSTRACT

In the first cycle of this phenomenological investigation, where the researcher conducted an extensive coding process (i.e., descriptive coding), all of the previous codes were identified under textural descriptions. This first cycle of coding was completed with codes having been formed following an initial deconstruction phase of the raw data given by all participants. Next, a plethora of emerging themes was identified under structural descriptions. The next step comprised the second cycle of coding: pattern coding. Where the second type of coding in the coding phase was involved, the researcher found all participants’ common speech patterns by meta coding. The researcher applied the meta coding technique via IPA-based meta transcriptions. From a linguistic perspective, the researcher employed the type of data analysis that had not previously been exercised in the CALL field and in the realm of phenomenological study design: phonics in data analysis.

INTRODUCTION

According to Moustakas (1994), the organization and analysis of phenomenological data begins when the researcher studies the transcribed interviews and engages in the methods and procedures of phenomenal analysis. The author provided that these procedures involve horizonalizing the data, meaning that each horizon or statement (verbal or written) pertinent to the topic and question is equally valuable. Working on these horizonalized statements, the researcher can construct meanings or meaning units which he/she then clusters into common categories or themes. During this process, the researcher needs to reduce and abbreviate data or remove redundancies or repetitive statements that overlap. Next, with these clustered meanings and themes, textural descriptions of the participants’ lived experiences are developed.

Moustakas (1994) explicated that by use of these textural descriptions, the phenomenological researcher can then construct structural descriptions and incorporate textures and structures into units of meanings. Finally, the researcher constructs the essences of the phenomenon being studied. The data extensively coded and analyzed in this phenomenological study are presented to the reader, by use of the same approach to deriving categorical meanings and themes. These fundamental methods and procedures of phenomenological data analysis, coupled with additional techniques such as reduction, epoché, bridling (Vagle, 2014), and other methods of analyzing the coded data significantly assisted with the completion of this comprehensive data analysis.

Developing Codes and Themes of Codes

Phenomenologists analyze data through stories—or by recounting stories. Their data analysis is based on developing codes and themes based on the essences of the meanings derived from the descriptions individually told by each study participant. Thereby, phenomenologists extract thematic meanings elicited from these descriptions during the data analysis phase. According to Englander and Robinson (2009), with openness and by reflecting on an early memory himself/herself, the phenomenological interviewer can demonstrate empathetic abilities toward his/her interviewees. Such reflections could include a lived, persistent meaning of what is regarded as an early emotional memory (Englander, 2007). With a thorough data analysis, a phenomenological study provides researchers with an in-depth understanding of a current issue, problem, or a phenomenon within a specific social context.

Descriptive Coding and Pattern Coding

During the process of reporting the results and conclusions derived from all partaking respondents of this phenomenological study, which focuses on mobile technology-assisted English as a Second Language (ESL) learning and instruction, two coding techniques were employed: 1) Descriptive Coding and 2) Pattern Coding. These coding techniques were paramount in reporting and presenting all analyses based on the data derived both from the audio recordings and from the written Lived Experience Description (LED) protocols formulated by Van Manen (2001). These LED protocols were completed by each of the 12 study participants (i.e., adult ESL learners) during one-on-one interviews, wherein the questions aimed at an in-depth understanding of the essence of the meanings behind their descriptions related to their personal experiences of whether or not they felt having transformed into more autonomous and developed language learners with the assistance of mobile learning (m-Learning).

The written anecdotes in these LED protocols have significantly helped analyze, code, and interpret the data. The collected data allowed for not only what was hoped to be a set of valid and reliable findings, but also for a solid ground for broader directions for future research1 pertinent to the phenomenon that each of these 12 participants personally experienced. The data elicited both from the audio interview recordings and those derived from the written protocols that were additionally asked the participants to complete during the interviews led to a thorough analysis and interpretation process, which then substantively helped find the essence of the meanings behind each participant’s orally uttered and written statements in regards to the study’s problem and research questions.

Most importantly, the entire analysis process and eventually this particular presentation of findings related to the study could not have been possible without the implementation of a set of phenomenological data reporting techniques. As far as this research study’s data analyses processes were concerned, Van Manen’s (2007) reflective writing techniques stood amongst all. It was through these specific data analysis techniques that all of the coded, categorized, interpreted, and structured data are currently presented to the reader. A number of recommendations and directions for further exploration pertinent to this phenomenon will also be provided, with the expectation that both the validity and reliability of these reported findings can be consolidated by the followers of this phenomenological study. It is also the anticipation that alternative theoretical frameworks and best practices can be sought in order to further contribute to the technology-supported adult ESL learning and teaching literature.

BACKGROUND

Descriptive Phenomenological Data Analysis Method Applied

Husserl and Heidegger believed that the world is described as one specific line among many other worlds and that humans, as conscious beings, see it as one of truths (Sloan & Bowe, 2014). These two pioneers of this unique phenomenological method—but predominantly Husserl—first developed it in 1970, paving the way for what Giorgi (1986) later formulated under specific procedures of descriptive phenomenology. Thus, drawing on both the predecessors’ and contemporary descriptive phenomenologists’ works, particular procedures of descriptive phenomenological data analysis were employed. Application of the fundamental axioms of descriptive phenomenology, which is to rather focus on the essence of human experiences, or what humans experienced and how they experienced that phenomenon while they were in the state of intentional consciousness, led to the use of phenomenological purity. In this study, phenomenological purity was applied with pure phenomenological attitude towards the phenomenon, and any interpretation by the researcher was minimized, again, by the researcher (Lester, 1999), with the least number of perceptions, judgments, biases, or preconceived ideas about the phenomenon, using the epoché, or bracketing, method.

Following Husserl’s descriptive phenomenological method, other contemporary pioneers of descriptive phenomenology such as Amedeo Giorgi (1979, 1986, & 2009) and Moustakas (1994) have also developed on this method, by proposing alternate approaches to phenomenological data analysis. Since its inception in the 1970’s (Giorgi, Giorgi, & Morley, 2017), the phenomenological psychological data analysis method has paved the way for a more lucid picture of the consciousness of participants having lived the experiences of a particular phenomenon. It was this descriptive data analysis method that this phenomenological study heavily relied on. That is, amongst any other type of qualitative data analysis methods commonly used in phenomenological research studies, descriptive data analysis method was selected for this inquiry.

Rationale for Choice of Descriptive Data Coding and Analysis

There were two reasons that helped support this specific data analysis related selection: 1) the research problem supported by the main learning theory (andragogy) and the supporting learning theory (social constructivism), both laying the foundation for the overarching theme of this study (i.e., whether or not the study participants may have felt that they had developed and/or transformed into autonomous English learners; 2) ontological, rather than merely epistemological, implications of the study (i.e., again, adult ESL students’ possible developmental change or their transformation—in their identity—into self-directed, independent learners of English, which will further be explained). The latter, in particular, played a significant role in a way that it helped warrant the philosophical and psychological underpinnings of this phenomenological exploration, where both were inextricably intertwined and both helped justify the selection of the descriptive data analysis method based on the study participants’ personal accounts of their lived experiences pertinent to the phenomenon under study.

According to Giorgi et al. (2017), there is one simple criterion for a well-executed data analysis in a phenomenological research study, which is that the descriptions given by the participants are to be good enough, or adequate. Drawing on this very criterion, the analyses completed from the data collected from each of all 12 adult ESL students are expected to shed sufficient—or simply, good enough—light to a holistic and explicit understanding of the essence of the meanings from individual accounts of these students who commonly lived certain experiences regarding the phenomenon of their individual use of mobile technologies in their English language acquisition and/or development processes. Both the information obtained from audio recordings and participants’ written descriptions on the written LED protocols were analyzed into meaningful analysis units. It is crucial to note at this point that the data obtained from each of the 12 adult English language learners during the one-one-one interviews yielded the type of descriptions that were much fuller compared to those obtained from the written LED protocols, which validates what is suggested by Giorgi, Giorgi, and Morley (2017) on the degree of length, richness, authenticity, and personality of the spoken narratives in contrast with the written ones.

Giorgi et al. (2017) established that the true phenomenological nature of a research study stipulates that only first-person accounts be analyzed and that no third person opinions be included, the researcher being one of the latter. Otherwise, the process of data analysis might be significantly impacted as the data obtained might get distorted and the results skewed or are not generalized enough or transferable to other studies in the field, contrary to what was originally planned. Therefore, the best solution to employ throughout this study’s data analysis was the proper use of transcendental reduction and epoché (Husserl, 1931).

In order to bolster this argument for a successful descriptive phenomenological data analysis process, it was vital to draw on the axioms of Giorgi et al. (2017), who proposed that a researcher needs to try to describe the lived experiences based on the raw data he/she obtains—and do this extremely carefully—and once the thorough phenomenological philosophical analysis of these raw data begins, it needs to occur with the researcher’s use of the phenomenological-psychological reduction. To further support this argument during the data analysis stage, the authors claim that this type of reduction, or this special attitude of the researcher, or epoché, is only successful when the researcher is able to set aside all his/her previous knowledge or judgments in his/her consciousness and considers—and works with—merely the type of the data presented or given to his/her consciousnesses, instead of intuitions that are currently existing in his/her consciousness. In Giorgi, Giorgi, and Morley’s (2017) defense, these raw data presented to the researcher by each participant were then to be very carefully described and analyzed in their entirety.

Thus, in order to have a much clear portrayal of the reason why the aforementioned learning and developmental theories rule this study and the manner in which an individual’s consciousness to and of a certain being (or phenomenon) is shaped with a considerable alteration of his/her thinking, actions, or behaviors in the present or in the future, one could closely look into what Merleau-Ponty (1963) found with respect to the general alteration of behavior. According to Merleau-Ponty (1963), altered behavior appears or manifests itself in a multitude of a series of actions, also called manifold of appearances. Drawing on this prominent figure’s proposed concept, manifold of appearances explained under the descriptive data analysis method and that which is based on the descriptions of a specific school organization’s adult ESL learners selected for one-on-one interviews for this study can help the reader gain a much deeper and clearer understanding of the qualitatively altered and transformed thinking, consciousness, rationale, being, becoming, growth, and development—or the respondents’ new, modified version of their own selves as adults.

Procedures and Methods of Data Analysis Applied: The 7-Step Van Kaam Method

For the data analysis phase of this study, the applied main method was the 7-step Van Kaam (1966) method,2 which included the following steps: Horizonalizing, reduction into invariant constituents (Moustakas, 1994), individual textural descriptions, individual structural descriptions, and finally, formation of composite textural and structural descriptions via synthesis that led to the essence of meanings. According to Moustakas (1994), the organization and analysis of phenomenological data begins when the researcher studies the transcribed interviews and engages in the methods and procedures of phenomenal analysis.

The author provided that these procedures involve horizonalizing the data, meaning that each horizon or statement (verbal or written) pertinent to the topic and question was equally valuable. Working on these horizonalized statements, the researcher of this study constructed meanings, or meaning units, which she then clustered into common categories or themes. During this process, the researcher reduced and abbreviated data, and removed redundancies or repetitive statements that overlapped, which Moustakas (1994) called invariant constituents. Next, with these clustered meanings and themes, all of the 12 participants’ individual textural descriptions related to their lived experiences of the phenomenon were developed. Using these textural descriptions, the researcher then formed individual structural descriptions. Finally, both of these individual textural descriptions and individual structural descriptions were collected into a final, composite textural-structural descriptions category that allowed for an integration of both the textures and structures, leading to the construct of the essences of the meanings pertinent to the experienced phenomenon (Moustakas, 1994). Below is an illustration of this detailed analysis process applied throughout the data analysis phase of the study:

Figure 1. Noema-Noesis relationship during data analysis
Note. This illustration, drawn by the researcher herself (Seda Khadimally), helps identify the textural (noematic) and structural (noetic) dimensions of the phenomenon under exploration during data analysis.

The data thoroughly analyzed in this study were presented to the reader, by reference to Moustakas’ (1994) modified version of Van Kaam’s (1966) seven-step phenomenological data analysis method (see Figure 5 on the previous page). The following figure, which denotes the relationship between noema and noesis in pursuit of the essence of the meanings from the participants’ given lived experience descriptions about the studied phenomenon, assisted with using the similar Van Kaam data analysis approach to deriving categorical meanings and themes. Consequently, the data analysis process led to finding the essence of the most pivotal meanings. The methodology of how this data analysis method was applied in the study will be further discussed in expansive detail. In addition, Van Kaam’s (1966) 7-step, fundamental data analysis methods and procedures of phenomenological data analysis were coupled with additional techniques such as reduction, epoché, bridling (Vagle, 2014), and others, all of which significantly helped with the completion of this data analysis.

Throughout the data analysis stage of this phenomenological study, derivation of meanings based on both the noetic and noematic side of each international experience of the phenomenon lived by each of the study participants was an essential function of intentionality (Moustakas, 1994). Cilesiz (2010) explicated that the aim of a phenomenological researcher is to reach the essence of the individuals’ lived experience of the phenomenon as he/she ascertains and defines the phenomenon. In regards to the vitality of finding the universal essence of the meanings during a phenomenological data analysis, Van Manen (1990) posited the following:

The essence of a phenomenon is a universal which can be described through a study of the structure that governs the instances or particular manifestation of the essence of that phenomenon… A universal or essence may only be intuited or grasped through a study of the particulars or instances as they are encountered in lived experiences. (p. 10)

Finding the universal essences as referred to by Natanson (1973) was paramount for the salience of this study’s data analysis stage, because each of these essences derived from the meanings of the descriptions by all 12 adult ESL learners significantly helped represent the true pattern and the true nature—as well as the collective, consistent, and similar nature—of the phenomenon as it was individually lived and perceived in their international consciousness in the life world by all of these study participants. In other words, in an attempt to build a connection between the noema3 (the what, that which appears as such, the appearance of the phenomenon, or the perceived as such, Husserl, 1931) and the noesis (the how or the perfect self-evidence in the light of which the phenomenon appears, Husserl, 1931) of these lived experienced (Sloan & Bowe, 2014) related to this same phenomenon, meaningful data units were extrapolated. Next, these meaning units were clustered. Finally, these groups of meanings extricated and bridged together were categorized under general themes.

During the analyses of all data sets gathered for this study, the phenomenon as it was experienced by each student participant in his/her conscious state of mind, not the phenomenon as the subject itself, constituted the key role. In a formulaic and structured manner to describe how this noema-noesis relationship constituting the above discussed intentionality of consciousness (Husserl, 1931) was achieved in this study, the below procedures were used.

In order to conduct this phenomenological study’s data analysis, the analysis method used was a modified version of the 7-Step Modified Version of the Original (7-step) Van Kaam method:

  • 1. A concrete description of the phenomenon under inquiry was held via horizonalization (Moustakas, 1994). Through the horizonalization technique, each expression related to the lived experience from the transcripts was listed as a code, and reduction and elimination techniques were applied in order to determine all of the invariant constituents, with a determination of whether or not some of these expressions were necessary. So, the codes that were determined to be unnecessary were reduced and/or eliminated by cross-referencing them to one another (Moustakas, 1994). Next, these codes were clustered, and invariant constituents were thematized, which required grouping similar and/or identical codes into themes.
  • 2. The purest and most naïve form of the phenomenological attitude (Giorgi, 1985b) towards this very phenomenon—in order to avoid any potential biases or preconceived notions regarding it.
  • 3. All descriptions given by each of the 12 adult ESL students were read and reread in their entirety and holistically with the purpose of capturing an impression of the pertinent phenomenon as a whole and to identify it more deeply.
  • 4. These descriptions were reread and reidentified, which led to the construct of units of meanings that substantively helped gain a much in-depth understanding of various aspects, angular visions, or different parts or deeper perceptions, or dimensions of this whole.
  • 5. The next step was to construct individual textural descriptions of the experience. From these textural descriptions, individual structural descriptions were constructed, which allowed for a meaningful integration of both textures and structures (composite textural-structural descriptions), which, in turn, led to much clearer and more explicit understanding of the meanings and essences of the phenomenon (Moustakas, 1994). This continuous attempt to perceive the whole profound analysis of what was actually presented to the researcher by the participants and how it was presented allowed for a synthesis. This thorough synthesis enabled a clear understanding of the noema (the object) and noesis (the act of the perception of the object) correlation aimed at with this data analysis.
  • 6. Each of the meaning units was coded and clustered, and then, these groups of codes were reclustered into more explicit codes based on their recurrence, regularities, similarities, and consistencies, which then gave way to explicit unraveling of general themes—these general themes helped categorize the psychological and philosophical underpinnings of each of these recoded and regrouped, or reclustered meaning units in congruence with all 12 respondents’ lived experiences of the phenomenon.
  • 7. Finally, the general structure of all of these 12 adult ESL students’ descriptions of their individually lived experiences of the phenomenon in their state of consciousness was presented and articulated in a way that it led to a much deeper, more conspicuous and more distinct, explicit, overt, and clear understanding of both the noema (the what) and the noesis (the how) of this experienced phenomenon.

Gestalt Theory and its Relation to Phenomenological Data Analysis

The underlying principle of Gestalt theory are congruent with the research school of phenomenology in ways that these principles can lead a clear and comprehensive understanding of the basic human experience in regards to a certain phenomenon under investigation. To corroborate such a connection between the Gestaltian way of perceiving things in a research participant’s individual life world—from an experiential perspective—and the methodology of conducting data coding and analysis based on the principles of the phenomenological school of inquiry, Braungardt (2016) provided the following:

Gestalt theorists argue that when the perceptual system of the human mind forms a gestalt, the whole has a reality of its own, independent of the parts. [When] the Gestalt psychologist Kurt Koffka said, “the whole is other than the sum of the parts,” he did not mean that the whole is greater than the sum of its parts. The Gestalt principle is not a principle of addition; it is a theory about the independent existence of the units of meaning that we call a gestalt. From a strictly scientific point of view, this is hard to prove. But in terms of lived experience, we know that an entire visual field may appear to be clear or chaotic. Faces of some people may seem relaxed, others seem tense, tired, or absent-minded. A purely behavioral approach based on hard evidence and objectifiable traits has no language for such qualities, other than the verbal reports themselves. (para. 2)

Because the Gestalt phenomenon is not found in merely a single form in the human perception, but also manifests in memory, decision-making, learning, as well as in other areas of life, then, the characteristics of perceptions, qualities of perceptions include groupings, movements of objects, their shapes, colors, sounds, tones, stresses, rhythms, rhymes, melodies, and other perceptive qualities created by human consciousness (Braungardt, 2016). For this reason, subjects’ lived experiences can appear in varied representations. It was through these multiple forms of representations of the perceived experience that the researcher conducted her entire data analysis process.

Gestaltian Approach to Data Analysis: Things Manifesting via Data Visualization

During the data coding and analysis stages of this research study, it was pivotal to take on a Gestaltian look at things that manifested via visualization of data. The data analysis method employed in this phenomenological study can provide the reader with a panoramic and holistic understanding of the Gestaltian (Giorgi, Giorgi, & Morley, 2017) way of looking at things that manifest or are given—these things appearing are not necessarily the things that exist. It is these things that helped the researcher of this study to be able to extrapolate specific codes and themes of codes that she derived from deeper meanings, initially by deconstructing the content—or by breaking down the participants’ descriptions both from face-to-face interviews and from the participants’ written LED descriptions—and then by reconstructing them into single meaning units during the conduct of the analysis. This study’s epistemological and ontological premises are in line with how each participant perceived their individual way of experiencing the studied phenomenon (i.e., m-Learning in their English language learning and development as adult second language learmers).

Each individual perception, thought, and feeling stemmed from the manner in which they looked at things as parts and wholes and as abstracta and concreta in their life world. The parts and wholes of perception of a particular phenomenon under inquiry could solely be seen, heard, thought, tasted, touched, and felt by use of five sensory perceptions, which led to the perception of parts as a whole. This way of looking at things explained the underlying principle of the Gestaltian way of perceiving things in the life world.

In this context, it is critical to note that the ways in which all participants lived their individual experiences pertinent to the phenomenon needed to be analyzed initially through sounds (phonemes) and next through visuals. The first type of analysis, which was phonetic coding (via meta transcriptions), was conducted via a special IPA-based phonetic coding system (coding of sounds/vowels) and by use of a specific software program called NCH Software (WavePad Sound Editor software program) both in the First Cycle of Coding and in the Second Cycle of Coding. The latter type of data analysis, which was applied by data visualization, was conducted under Pattern Coding in the Second Cycle of Coding, following the initial comprehensive phonetic coding. Each of the themes of the phonetic codes identified was visualized by use of specific data visualization software called Wordle. Every visual image represented the feelings, emotions, thoughts, and viewpoints that the partaking adult ESL students expressed during the face-to-face interviews as they described their feelings and perceptions of their experiences related to the phenomenon.

Visualization of data led to a much clearer understanding of the phonetically coded data during analyses, because it allowed for visual representations of the audio data (obtained from participants’ audio recording from the face-to-face interviews) initially coded by use of sounds. In the field of linguistics, in phonetics in particular, these sounds are also called phonemes. This type of data visualization enabled a deeper grasp of what experience each participant lived and how they lived that experience, the two core questions that a phenomenologist should seek in phenomenological inquiry (Moustakas, 1994). Thus, it was of paramount importance to capture how the study participants individually perceived the parts and wholes of that lived experience. Thus, in order to fully understand how every participant saw, thought, felt, and perceived the phenomenon as they personally experienced it, the researcher completed a holistic data visualization process, by generating images of each phonetically coded statement.

First Cycle of Coding: Descriptive Coding through Textural and Structural Descriptions

Interview Q1: Please describe in detail a situation in which you lived the experience of using mobile technologies in your class.

Codes Identified through Thematic Analysis. Descriptive coding4 significantly assisted with summarizing the primary topic of the below excerpts (Saldaña, 2008). The following 11 codes were identified based on the first three student participants’ (adult ESL P1, P2, & P3) compiled responses to interview question 1:

Participant 1: Last huuhh....session...huuhhmm...we had like two or three tests in which the teacher uhh....give us the...the....the...the.....uahhhh....[pause]...we needed to use the video to answer the test, so we can pause the test...pause the video, sorry. Participant 2: Also in this [TOEFL Reading & Writing] class...huhhhh......we use mobile technology...ahhhh... in order to take the test of academic word list, and it was very useful because for example, in my case that, I cannot use the computer at home; I use the cell phone, and it was very didactic and easy...huuuhhh...because when I was in the car, I use it. It was good. For me, managing my time. Aaanndd, it was good [in a quiet tone].

Participant 3: Uhhmmm, also, in the speaking class, we use uuuhhh...we use our phone to record our speaking test, and then, we can listen again, then, we know our advantages and disadvantages that we can improve ourselves. So, that’s very helpful for us.

At the end of this excerpt, it is noteworthy to look at student participants’ self-recognition and realization (Vagle, 2014) based on their own individual learning and developmental experiences related to the phenomenon under question. This recognition appears to have stemmed from each participant’s recognition of the phenomenon under their intentional consciousness (Husserl, 1931), which solidifies the study’s transcendental premises. The type of coding here in this initial phase of the coding process helped describe and understand all three students’ recognition of the current situation (i.e., their lack of use of mobile technologies for classroom practices in the past, their realization and appreciation of their self-improvement in the present, their understanding of both the advantages and disadvantages of using mobile technologies in their learning processes, reinforcement of their newly acquired knowledge and English skills, recognition of the affordances of mobile tools in their recent learning experiences and potentially in future ones) regarding their personal learning experience, as well as their possible future action(s).

Second Cycle of Coding (Pattern Coding): Phonetic Coding Conducted via IPA-Based Meta Transcriptions

In the second cycle of coding, or pattern coding, the researcher employed meta transcriptions, which included spelling of sounds (phonemes) towards finding common speech patterns related to the lived phenomenon. In the First Cycle of the coding process, which was Descriptive Coding, all of the previous codes were identified under textural descriptions. This First Cycle of coding was completed with codes having been formed following an initial deconstruction phase of the raw data given by all participants. Next, a plethora of emerging themes was under identified structural descriptions. The next step comprised the Second Cycle of Coding: Pattern Coding. Where the second type of coding in the coding phase was involved, the researcher found all participants’ common speech patterns by meta coding. She applied the meta coding technique via IPA-based meta transcriptions.5 Being a linguist herself, therefore, from a linguistic perspective, the researcher employed the type of data analysis that had not previously been exercised in the CALL field and in the realm of phenomenological study design: Phonics in data analysis. This specific type of pattern coding, phonetic coding, is what makes this phenomenological exploration original, authentic, ingenious, and unique.

In order to decipher phonetic codes in this second phase of her analysis of the collected data, the researcher revisited and reread the table of descriptive coding schema (see Table 1) based on all interview transcripts combined. She had earlier structured both of these coding schemas throughout the descriptive coding phase. Next, she located the most frequently used, or the most consistent, recurring and repetitive textural descriptions provided by all of the 12 adult ESL student participants. She then analyzed the particular tones and stresses (high stress and low stress) that stood out in each of these recurring codes under each textural description. Her goal with extrapolating an array of these outstanding sounds was to find what each of these respondents felt as they lived their individual experiences pertinent to the phenomenon under her inquiry. In order to achieve this goal and complete this second cycle of the coding process (i.e., Pattern Coding), the researcher referred to the official and universal phonics alphabet system, including its related interactive charts and a unique coding system used by all linguists, including herself.

International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA) Association6 (1999) is an organization founded for phoneticians and that practice linguistics, which assists with interactive, ubiquitous, and most recent phonetic coding charts and systems as these practitioners engage in deciphering the meanings behind certain word accents, tones, and other linguistic elements of the language used by speakers. During the Second Cycle of Coding (Pattern Coding), the researcher utilized these phonetic charts to describe the purpose, intention, and meanings of the most commonly used oral remarks that her interviewees used during the interviews. The researcher’s phonetic coding process exclusively focused on particular vowels as phonemes only, rather than consonants, which yielded emergent stress, rhythm, tone, and rhetoric from within the partaking subjects’ oral statements. She derived these tones and high or low stress sounds, by especially referring to the word accent chart, diacritics chart, and suprasegmentals chart specifically designed by the IPA organization for the type of phonetic coding distinct and particular to the English language.

In reference to IPA and for a salient practice of data analysis through each phonetic code, the researcher utilized an extensive number of resources—including a vast range of code schemas and frameworks—as she examined each of the 12 adult ESL learners’ uttered speeches as they orally responded to all four interview questions during one-on-one interviews. Based on the similarities, regularities, and consistencies, and repetitions of the responses each of the 12 student participants, specific statements were selected and placed in the same coding category. The next step applied was to determine the tones, word accents, and styles of each of these similar—or even identical—statements uttered according to the stresses (high or low stress) rhythms, as well their intonations and enunciations.

This was only possible to decipher by identification of some of the outstanding words which were then broken down into their syllabi (i.e., syncategorematic elements) that next led to identifying particularly emergent vowels of the sounds. These vowels were analyzed in detail based on their phonetic features (see Figure 2 for Vowels - Tones and Word Accents Chart).

The phonetic analysis assisted with having a deeper grasp of the essence of the meanings derived from the (textural) descriptions that each respondent provided. In other words, by use of specific phonetic codes from within the IPA system, the researcher could understand in detail what each participant meant and intended to say in their conscious internationality, and why (purpose) they said the things they said (what→noema) in a certain manner (how→noesis). The subjects’ purpose and intent with their oral statements was pivotal for the researcher to discern, which is directly congruent with the Husserlian approach to phenomenological inquiry and analysis, which can be referred to as a philosophy of phenomena experienced in an intuitive manner.

Phonetic Data Analysis towards Deeper Learning of the Participants’ Conscious Intentionality and Perceptions of the Phenomenon Under Research

Sokolowksi (2000) later built his views upon this notion of conscious intentionality and expanded the to which an individual living an experience about a phenomenon intuits each concrete and abstract identity of things that manifest to him/her under certain circumstances and contexts either through empty or filled intentionality, and he/she realizes this based on his/her memory, perception, imagination, and anticipation. With the intent to develop on this concept of conscious intentionality where things manifest to subjects, the researcher of this phenomenological study conducted this analysis of sounds (i.e., phonemes), because she sought what her partaking study subjects felt, thought, perceived, and intended within their individual consciousness, circumstances, and contexts as they spoke to her during one-on-one conversations.

The researcher was aware that as she searched for essential features and meanings of things, she needed to move into somewhat a deeper inquiry rather than basic or original, which then led up to her digression from the original, basic, natural attitude, and thereby, resulted in a phenomenological attitude. From a philosophical viewpoint, which also perfectly aligns with the philosophical and psychological premises of this phenomenological research, as discussed earlier in this chapter, moving from the original, natural, and naïve phenomenological attitude to a more profound and philosophical attitude during the conduct of her data analysis would not only enrich her experiences along with those of her subjects—in an interdialogic manner—but also bring about a paradigmatic shift in her research-based, phenomenological attitude: from natural to transcendental (Sokolowski, 2000).

According to Sokolowski (2000), there is a clear distinction between natural attitude and phenomenological attitude as phenomenologists seek to understand what phenomenology really is. The researcher of this exploration contended that when phenomenological researchers engage in investigating meanings related to certain phenomena, they start off with an original approach associated with them. This attitude is considered as the very basic stance, or what Sokolowski (2000) called the default perspective or one in which humans originally are. Sokolowski (2000) analogized moving into the phenomenological attitude to becoming a philosopher, which validates the philosophical attempts made in this particular phenomenological data analysis process. In favor of this argument, Sokolowski (2000) stated that researchers look at things and describe them analytically from a phenomenological standpoint, and that all specific intentionalities correlate these intentionalities with the world beliefs, along with the world as its own correlative. Drawing on this assertion, the researcher adopted and implemented this particular shift from the natural attitude to phenomenological attitude, which then led her to hold a transcendental attitude. This transcendental attitude, in fact, was a far more comprehensive and radical attitude, as congruent with what Sokolowski (2000) suggested.

Meta Transcription

With this new phenomenological shift in her attitude to her own analysis, the researcher was then able to more comprehensively and deeply understand the thought—and (intentional) consciousness—process behind her subjects’ talks and the language they used as she selected certain answers from their responses related to their lived experiences of their personal use of mobile technologies in their English language learning and development—as well as in their personal development and possible transformation as self-directed, autonomous adult ESL learners. During this process, IPA significantly helped identify these phonemes, which were determined as particular representations of sounds that were labeled by phonic symbols (by use of the official IPA alphabet). In other words, throughout this type of phonetic coding process, each phonetic code identified was derived from certain sounds that phonetically stood out as they were derived from all 12 students’ oral statements during all of the face-to-face interviews. This section of the data analysis focuses on the participants’ textural descriptions where commonalities in the previously structured descriptive coding schema (i.e., Table 1) were found. Repetitive statements (descriptions) and recurring codes and themes of codes from within these descriptions were located and selected from within this chart.

By reference to IPA tones and word accents as denoted on this vowels chart, which each participant used, the researcher found what each respondent actually meant, perceived, and emotionally felt as they narrated what they experienced related to the pertinent phenomenon and how they experienced it. A compilation of phonetic transcriptions (meta transcriptions) led to the finding of deeper meanings that each participant attributed to their experience of utilizing mobile technologies in their ESL acquisition and development. In the SLA literature, this process is also called speech recognition. In summary, the meta transcription process during this second cycle of the coding stage allowed for understanding of what each of the subjects meant when they said one word, a few words, or longer phrases along certain syntax.

Meta Transcription Conducted by Phonetic Coding (by Use of Phonemes): The Essence of the Meanings Derived from Study Subjects’ Descriptions of Their Lived Experiences

In this phenomenological study, two types of transcriptions can be found: 1) Audio recordings’ transcriptions completed by the researcher, following 12 face-to-face interviews; 2) phonetic transcriptions (IPA-based phonetic coding) conducted by use of these first set of transcribed, written interview data. All of the following phonetic codes were derived from Figure 7 (IPA Phonetic codes chart for vowels in the English language). Each of these phonetic codes resulted from a process of finding, selecting, and clustering vowels in each syllable situated in a particular syntax. Phonetic coding was completed by use of the previously transcribed statements uttered by selected respondents during the interviews. Each vowel that was identified is called as phonemes. Phonemes are also called as units of sound. Thus, the researcher conducted a second type of transcription by identifying each of these sound units as she phonetically analyzed these vowels in an extremely meticulous and ardent manner.

The following phonetic codes, structured under the Second Cycle of Coding (Pattern Coding), were derived from all 12 respondents’ (12 adult ESL learners’) answers to all four open-ended interview questions. Extensive IPA-based phonetic analyses were conducted as a product of meta transcriptions, or through phonetic spelling of the identified sounds, or vowels. Meta transcriptions emerged as the conduct of phonetic transcriptions of—or beyond—the existing transcriptions of the audio recorded interviews that had earlier been transcribed one by one by the researcher and put into the written text form. In other words, meta transcriptions were completed as the second form of the first form of transcripts (the previously transcribed audio recordings obtained from all 12 interviews) that had already been listened to by the researcher and then transcribed into a written form by her. Finally, a common, unified speech pattern was created through an extrapolation of the most commonly used and the most repetitively uttered statements as part of the respondents’ 11 selected, descriptive (textural) codes under the First Cycle of Coding: Descriptive Coding Schema (see Table 1).

Findings Reported: In this pattern coding analysis, the detailed phonetic coding exercised directly overlapped with the syntactic, or linguistic, sign system, for the properly constructed syntax within this respondent’s statement assisted with a much clearer understanding of the linguistic sign system—the grammatical ordering of the chunks of an uttered or written statement (Sokolowski, 2000)—which was then amenable to delving into a salient phonetic analysis by the researcher. This phonetic analysis, therefore, not only provided the syncategorematic parts of the language during reporting (Sokolowski, 2000), but most importantly, it allowed for a deeper understanding of the idiosyncrasies, authenticity, nuances, uniqueness, and richness of the meanings and themes derived from this student’s uttered remarks as he/she lived his/her personal experience related to the phenomenon under inquiry, which was adult ESL learners’ use of mobile technology inside and/or outside of class in their English language acquisition and/or development processes. FUTURE RESEARCH DIRECTIONS

The findings of this research study can be found to echo how Vagle (2014) sees phenomenology when he stated that this type of qualitative study is a way of living with a certain way and philosophy in life; it is an encounter of how others live their own experiences in their own conscious intentionalities. Therefore, according to Vagle (2014), engaging in the practice of phenomenology is a craft in and of itself. This specific term, craft, coined to the practice of phenomenology is precisely what the researcher of this exploration undertook as she engaged in an attempt to solve her study’s problem, answer its three central questions, as well as ensure that each of the study’s elements aligned with its design: phenomenology. As she undertook crafting this phenomenological practice, she scrutinized every component of her inquiry through various vantage points, as expected from a phenomenologist who often seeks the essence of things on descriptive, fresh, psychological, and philosophical levels.

In that respect, the first direction and recommendation for future (novice) phenomenologists who may involve in crafting a comprehensive phenomenological exploration like this study is to regard phenomenology as a craft and hone it to the best of their ability and within their own experience, expertise, and practice. The second recommendation for future investigation in this field is that it is paramount that they take upon this onus with an inspiration of the fundamental nature of transcendental phenomenological research. This undertaking includes adopting and practicing the basic elements of descriptive data coding and analysis processes from start to finish. It is especially essential for future phenomenological researchers to keep in mind that descriptive data coding and analysis under the principles of phenomenological school of inquiry focus on the very fundamentals of being a human in a certain context or situation and perceiving that situation with being-ness under conscious intentionality, instead of being directed to an object—or of being-ness, rather than merely of doing or of-ness.

It is through this acknowledgment, understanding, and appreciation that they can then delve more deeply into the basic essence of the meanings behind the descriptions given to them by their human subjects, who know their own being-ness and their own becoming within their own paradigms of change or transformation at any level. In this way, as Vagle (2014) suggests, too, future phenomenological researchers can develop and craft a salient pattern of single themes—or thematic categories—that may help yield a comprehensive and meaningful apprehension of the very existentialist (ontological) and epistemological (knowing) layers of what their study participants really meant as they provided personal accounts of their own lived experiences about a specific phenomenon under inquiry.

CONCLUSION

It was through these originally devised components of transcendental phenomenology (Husserl, 1931) that the researcher of this phenomenological exploration kept in mind that phenomenology rests in encountering things as if for the first time and with the highest level of empathy, as, during her one-on-one interviews with the, she listened to things from them by fresh perceptions. She listened to them as if for the first time and as though her respondents’ given descriptions of their experiences were brand new every time she received a new individual account of that lived experience, or a personal narrative, or a description from them regarding the phenomenon under her study, which was whether or not utilizing mobile learning technologies in the English language learning and development process brought about a possible transformation in all 12 adult ESL learners both epistemologically and ontologically and whether or not they had perceived any changes in their English language learning or in their identity as adult learners and autonomously (adult learning, or andragogy) as they experienced this phenomenon with mobile technologies both inside and outside of the classroom context. Drawing on the components and the findings of this research study, future phenomenologists could listen to their own human subjects’ accounts of their personally lived experiences in such a fresh manner. And every time they do this, they could embrace their partaking respondents’ ideas, or when they listen to their stories in a face-to-face setting, they can successfully gather the pertinent data—and perceiving these data freshly, as if for the first time (Moustakas, 1994).

KEY TERMS AND DEFINITIONS

Andragogy: A term that refers to instruction for adults, or adult learning (Knowles, 1970), which focuses more on the process and less on the content being taught. In andragogical learning, strategies such as case studies, role playing, simulations, and self-evaluation are most useful. Instructors adopt a role of facilitator or resource rather than lecturer or grader. Andragogy makes the following assumptions about the design of learning: 1) adults need to know why they need to learn something; 2) adults need to learn experientially; 3) adults approach learning as problem-solving; and 4) adults learn best when the topic is of immediate value.

Bracketing: In phenomenological research, in the process of interpreting the meaning of the phenomenon from the participants’ perspective, the researcher can remove his/her biases by using this method. Bracketing is also called as suspending judgment.

Bridling: A data analysis technique in phenomenological inquiry.

Descriptive Data Coding and Analysis.: It is a phenomenological, psychological data coding and analysis method which allows for an in-depth understanding of a more lucid picture of the consciousness of participants having lived personal experiences related to a particular phenomenon.

Descriptive Phenomenology: It focuses on the essence of human experiences, or what humans experienced and how they experienced that phenomenon while they were in the state of intentional consciousness. This leads to phenomenological purity. In this study, phenomenological purity was applied with pure phenomenological attitude towards the phenomenon, and any interpretation by the researcher was minimized, again, by the researcher (Lester, 1999), with the least number of perceptions, judgments, biases, or preconceived ideas about the phenomenon, using the epoché, or bracketing, method.

Diacritics: Type of phonetic coding distinct and particular to the English language, as determined by the IPA organization.

Enunciation: The act of saying or pronouncing a word or expression in a clear, slow, and definitive manner.

Epistemological: A term that refers to knowing or knowledge of things or phenomena.

Epoché: Based on the participants’ lived experiences of that phenomena, phenomenologists use the bracketing technique, also called epoché (Sokolowski, 2000). Through this technique, they set aside their own experiences and/or assumptions regarding that phenomenon; they try to describe those experiences with a fresh perspective, treating those experiences as if for the first time.

ESL: An acronym for English as Second Language.

Horizonalizing: It is a data analysis technique in phenomenological research, wherein each horizon or statement (verbal or written) pertinent to the topic and question is equally valuable. Working on these horizonalized statements, the researcher can construct meanings or meaning units which he then clusters into common categories or themes. During this process, the researcher needs to reduce and abbreviate data or remove redundancies or repetitive statements that overlap. Next, with these clustered meanings and themes, textural descriptions of the participants’ lived experiences are developed.

Intonation: The rise and fall of one’s voice in speech.

IPA: Acronym for International Phonetic Alphabet.

LED Protocols: A term referred to as the Lived Experience Description protocols formulated by the prominent phenomenologist Van Manen (2001).

M-Learning: Abbreviation for mobile learning.

Noesis and Noema: In the Winter 2016 edition of The Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, the following is noted: In Ideas I (Book One, 1913) Husserl introduced two Greek words to capture his version of the Bolzanoan distinction: noesis and noema, from the Greek verb noéō (νοέω), meaning to perceive, think, intend, whence the noun nous or mind. The intentional process of consciousness is called noesis, while its ideal content is called noema. The noema of an act of consciousness Husserl characterized both as an ideal meaning and as “the object as intended.” Thus, the phenomenon, or object-as-it-appears, becomes the noema, or object-as-it-is-intended. The interpretations of Husserl’s theory of noema have been several and amount to different developments of Husserl’s basic theory of intentionality (Smith, 2016).

Ontological: A terms that refers to the presence of an entity or phenomenon from an existentialist viewpoint.

Pattern Coding: Special coding system that assists with finding commonalities, similarities, or recurring patterns in study participants’ oral or written statements.

Phonemes: In phonetics, they are defined as units of sound.

Social Constructivism: Social constructivism is a learning perspective founded on the assumption that learning is focused on learners and promotes their active participation as they manage to construct their own knowledge depending on their own reality. Vygotsky’s (1978) social constructivist theory stipulates that students acquire the ability to construct their own meanings by critical thinking.

Stress: Giving a syllable or word intensity during speech, by producing loudness and emphasis.

Suprasegmental: According to IPA, it is a distinct type of phonetic coding particular to English.

Syncategorematic Elements: Words dissected into their syllabi for their proper identification.

Van Kaam (1966) Method: For the conduct of phenomenological data analysis, one of the most prominent and frequently used data analysis methods that Moustakas (1994) proposes is the 7-step modified van Kaam process, which this study employed.
