KEY TERMS AND DEFINITIONS

Artificial Intelligence (AI): The artificial intelligence (e.g., AI) is the smart programs, machines capable of performing the tasks, that require human intelligence. Some of the examples of AI include text editors or auto correct, search and recommendation algorithms, chatbots, digital assistants, etc.

Causal Inference: The causal inference allows the researchers to draw causal conclusions based on the data by following different estimation strategies, assumptions.

Classifier: The classifier utilizes some training data to understand the relationship between the input data and the class. The classifier belongs to the supervised learning where the datasets are labeled.

Computer-Generated Imagery (CGI): The computer-generated imagery (e.g., CGI) is the images created by the applications of the computer graphics, computer simulated results, computer animations, etc.

Convolutional Neural Network (CNN): The convolutional neural network (e.g., CNN) is a DL algorithm that can analyze image data and differentiate different objects observed in the data.

Cyberattack: The cyberattack is any attempt to expose, alter, disable, destroy, steal, or gain information through unprivileged or unauthorized access to the computer, network systems.

Cybersecurity: The cybersecurity is the protection measures of the computer systems and networks from the cyberattack. The cyberattack includes the information disclosure, theft or damage to the hardware systems, the disruption or misdirection of the services associated with the system.

Deep Learning (DL): The deep learning (e.g., DL) is the subset of the machine learning that allows machines to solve complex problems from the multidimensional, complex datasets. Some examples of the DL include self-driving cars, natural language processing, visual recognition, fraud detection, etc.

Deep Learning Technologies: The deep learning technologies define the technology that follows the concept of DL. The deep neural network (e.g., DNN), recurrent neural network (e.g., RNN), convolutional neural network (e.g., CNN) are some examples of the DL technologies.

Deep Neural Network (DNN): The deep neural network (e.g., DNN) is an artificial neural network (e.g., ANN) with multiple layers between the input and output layers that allow to learn the features that optimally represent the given training data.

Digital Assistant: The digital assistant is a computer program that can assist the user by completing different tasks as answering the calls, performing basic tasks etc. The Amazon Echo and Google Home are popular examples of the digital assistant.

Image Analysis: The image analysis is a process of extracting meaningful information from the digital image. The process incorporates simple image analysis as reading bar coded tags or as sophisticated as identifying different objects from the image. The CNN is an example of image analysis.

Inferential Outcome: The inferential outcome refers to the inferential statistics that makes judgments of the probability that an observed difference between groups is a dependable one or one that might have happened by chance in this study.

Machine Learning (ML): The machine learning (e.g., ML) is the part of AI, that can learn and improve learning automatically using the data. Some of the real world examples of the ML include virtual personal assistants, video surveillance, malware filtering, etc.

Multidimensional Search: The searching operation is done in multiple dimensions rather than single one. The searching complexity increases as the no. of dimension increases. The dimensions can be correlated by the searching keywords.

Natural Language Processing (NLP): The NLP, a branch of the artificial intelligence (e.g., AI), can help the computer to understand, interpret and manipulate the human language by the RNN. Some common examples of the NLP include the email filters, smart assistants, language translation, predictive text, etc.

Recurrent Neural Network (RNN): The recurrent neural network (e.g., RNN) is an ANN that can recognize a data’s sequential characteristics and use patterns to predict the next likely scenario. It is generally used in the speech recognition and natural language processing (e.g., NLP).

Chapter 6

A Comprehensive Literature Review With a Systems Approach

  • Seda Khadimally
    https://orcid.org/0000-0001-6374-3248
    North American University, USA

ABSTRACT

The purpose of this literature review is to provide readers with comprehensive information on how adult learners can master their learning autonomously by use of social networking technology. With this extensive review, readers are expected to expand their horizon of the role of social networking tools in progressive higher educational organizations, including their contributions to learning, instruction, instructional design, and project management from a systems perspective with foci on two popular instructional systems design (ISD) models applied in educational processes. Some of the innovative applications of social networking within the project management-based online learning design (PMBOLD) field, in addition to a number of potential risks and challenges associated with incorporating social networking technology into learning and instruction will be additionally presented.

INTRODUCTION

According to Tomei and Morris (2013), “social networking is defined as a web-based service that allow individuals to construct a public profile within network parameters that permit the user to admit or restrict those with whom they mutually agree to share a connection” (p. 141). Applications such as blogs, social networks, content communities or virtual social worlds can all be placed under the umbrella of social media, which can be referred to as “a group of Internet-based applications that build on the ideological and technological foundations of Web 2.0” (Aymerich-Franch & Fedele, 2015, p. 54). College students tend to adopt social media applications both for entertainment and learning purposes (Cao, Aijan, & Hong, 2013). Dahlstrom (2012) asserted that “students use various social media applications and it has become an indispensable part of their everyday life for personal and learning purposes. Cao et al. (2013) provided that today’s learners have the unique educational opportunity to interweave mobile technologies and smartphones in their palms and at their command anytime and anywhere. Bicen and Cavus (2011) posited an array of social networking sites (SNSs) have recently come to existence with the advances in information and communication technologies (ICTs) (as cited in Benson & Morgan, 2014).

However, among other popular social networking tools (e.g., Twitter, Instragram, Pinterest, Tumblr, etc.), none of these SNSs has been as successful as Facebook, which is rather regarded as a technological tool for learning, instruction, and communication. In other words, Facebook has prevailed among all other social networking applications until today. From a systems perspective, Boyd and Ellison (2008) asserted that social networking tools allow users to do the following: 1) Construct a profile that is either semi-public or public in an online system, 2) create a user list based on the individuals’ connections, and 3) view other users’ profiles and shared friends, as well as their connections through the systems, in addition to those of their own (as cited in Benson & Morgan, 2014).

BACKGROUND

Ongoing debate among believers and skeptics of the pedagogical value of technology supported learning and instruction is not an indicative of a one-size-fits-all model in the 21st century classroom. While some school systems adopt and implement a completely face-to-face approach to getting students to interact with the content, with each other, and with their instructor, some others opt for fully online education to leverage students’ learning and teachers’ instructional practices. The debacle here does not stem from whether or not instructors should implement technology-based resources in their class-related activities. Paramount instructional decisions are made not based on the “usage level of technological resources, but the [proper] usage of technology in educational environment with convenient pedagogical approach” (Sezer, Yilmaz, & Yilmaz, 2013, p. 141). As such, in order to optimize effective learning and teaching with various web 2.0 technologies both inside and outside of the classroom, instructors need to consider several factors.

These design considerations involve instructors’ own design-related decisions in conjunction with the learning objective they determine, the educational context they are teaching in, their personal attitudes towards and perceptions about integration of technology-based resources into curricula, types of learner groups they are working with, these learners’ previous educational backgrounds, differences in their learning styles, preferences, study skills, and cognitive processing (Clark & Mayer, 2011) competencies. Learners’ current level of confidence and comfort in their use of these technologies, along with their preferences on their hardware and software use, administrative issues such budgeting and school administrators’ support on the use of technology-based resources in class, determiners of educational policies, involvement of parents and other stakeholders, as well as legal and ethical issues with intellectual property use—proper conduct of copyright laws—can be cited as additional considerations for design of effective instruction.

Given the current educational milieu where millennials (Tomei & Morris, 2013) rule their own learning processes with rather a student-centric approach, a thorough review needs to be conducted. The purpose of this paper is to provide the reader with such a comprehensive review, drawing on the current education in technology literature. With this review, the reader is expected to expand their horizon of the aforementioned considerations and issues pertinent to the incorporation of technology-based resources into learning/teaching. This extensive review and personal evaluations included at the end of the paper can particularly shed light to why, for whom, and how these technology-based resources are to be integrated into curriculum, rather than whether or not they should be. One specific technology was selected for the conduct of this literature review: Social networks. With this thorough review, it is the hope that the reader can widen their current knowledge of both the pedagogical value of this technology in congruence with deeper learning and the type of learning that is fully mastered by learners, in addition to some of the potential challenges it poses when integrated into educational activities at schools; namely, adult learning and instructional contexts.

Contribution of Social Media to Learning and Teaching Environments: Transformation from Teacher-Centered to Student-Oriented Learning and Instruction

Regarding the utility of social media on today’s students’ transformation into self-directed learners, it is possible to assert that as various mobile learning technologies came to existence, they have had a tremendous impact on students’ transforming into autonomous, independent learners. This role is evident in the emerging paradigm shift from yesterday’s traditional, teacher-centered learning which is now replaced by student-centered learning and/or learner-generated content. With respect to this, Kukulska-Hulme (2010) stated that social media act as a catalyst, enabling a “pedagogical refocus from teacher-directed content delivery to student-generated content and student-generated learning contexts” (p. 2).

In their recent study, Yildiz and Scharaldi (2015) found that “there is a growing number of research and support by academics” in this field (p. 259). The Digital Media and Learning Initiative funded by the McArthur Foundation released the report findings of the Digital Youth Research project back in 2008, in support of kids’ using digital media for informal learning. The research findings reported that “youth engage in peer-based, self-directed learning online” (Ito, 2008, Digital Youth Research, para. 9). Ito (2008) noted that social media lead to freedom of expression for younger learners and allow for “autonomy for youth that is less apparent in a classroom setting. Youth respect one another’s authority online and they are often more motivated to learn from peers than from adults. Their efforts are also largely self-directed” (para. 11) when compared to learning objectives predetermined by their instructor and in a classroom environment.

Andragogy (Adult Learning) Formulated by Malcolm Knowles (Early 1970s)

The idea with self-directed learning is that learners control and regulate most of their learning activities. Adults learn through a process during which they can regulate how they learn, where they learn, when they learn, as well as how fast they learn; that is, the pace of their learning. Brookfield (1986) explained that “the attainment of adulthood is concomitant on adults’ coming to perceive themselves as self-directing individuals” (as cited in Simonson et al., 2012, p. 51). According to Knowles (1980), andragogy includes the following four assumptions: 1) self-concept; 2) experience; 3) readiness to learn; and 4) orientation to learning. Democratic participation is another critical component of andragogy. Under Knowles’ self-directed learning approach, a shift to transformation starts once adult ESL learners successfully manage to transfer information from their previous experiences to their current knowledge (Murray, 2008).

Autonomous (i.e., self-directed learning) learning constitutes a vehicle for helping adult learners feel like they are an organic part of the mainstream education. If so, by using DE media and technologies, adult learners are very likely to actively participate in the society, and thereby, become active, social, and participative members of the social milieu. They prefer and manage to direct their own learning processes, rather than remaining as passive, marginalized learner groups. Murray (2008) called this phenomenon as a paradigm shift in the digital era.

Role of Digital Technologies in the Net Generation’s Autonomous Learning

The new norm in the current educational contexts is the type of pedagogy that is driven by technology (Chuang, 2013) not in advocacy for traditional teaching methods that refuse to adapt to new changes and maintain the status quo. Such transference is initiated with mobile devices as they become independent and autonomous as tech-savvy learners who are apt to understand and apply the given content with portable, digital technologies without the boundaries of time and space. Utilizing mobile technologies for deeper learning both inside and outside of the classroom is a process in its entirety that could lead adult students to gradually lead their own learning and thus become self-regulated learners (Ormrod, 2008). As such, it is feasible to assert that the practicality of learning autonomously via social networking technologies is highly conducive to Knowles’ theoretical approach to adults’ learning, andragogy. In terms of their ability to create learning contexts that are diverse, Fuegen (2012) argued that, with their huge potential, these technologies support learners’ socialization and thus can help create learning and instructional contexts conducive to open, interactive communications and to the types of dialogues that are powerful enough to defeat today’s transactional distance divide.

Glen (2000) contended that the type of students today is rather interested in interactive learning environments wherein they have access to multiple choices in terms of assignments they complete and various forms of feedback they receive, in addition to learning opportunities with which they can express themselves as self-directed learners utilizing various resources in order to create learning experiences that are individually meaningful for themselves. Today’s students want to be in charge of their own learning processes, performing tasks as hands-on and solving problems with an inquiry-based approach to their personal learning experiences. Besides the evolution of students in the new millennium, the evolution of technology, according to Gitonga and Murungi (2015), provides learning opportunities with innovative technologies that are interactive and effective, demonstrating diverse content areas that might be difficult for students. Lehtinen (2006) posited that students of the digital world are able to learn anytime and anywhere thanks to the advancements in technology, particularly on the Internet.

Mastery of Learning Gained with an Andragogical Approach to Learning and Instruction

When infused with the andragogical approach to learning towards effective design, development, implementation, and evaluation of instruction (Reiser & Dempsey, 2007), these digital technologies pose alternatives to students’ participation in social, economic, and political life in the U.S. Katzlinger and Herzog (2014) posited that instructors should organize the learning environment that fosters and supports self-regulated learning processes for their students. Knowles (1984) stated that because adults are self-directed, type of instruction delivered to them should allow them to discover things and gain knowledge on their own, without reliance on people who should provide them with guidance only when necessary or when they make mistakes. Instructors should organize the learning environment that fosters and supports self-regulated learning processes for their students.

Under the provision of andragogy applied to DE, information and communication technologies (ICTs) are designed for learning, promoting, and fostering a learning culture of innovations. ICTs utilized by adult learners remotely and at different times provide them with significant experiential research that supports innovation and independent learning. These online, DL technologies lead adult ESL learners to socially connect and interact with others in the global context, in addition to electronically engage not only in the content provided to them, but also to information others are learning, building, and disseminating. With respect to this, Ally, McGreal, Schafer, Tin, and Cheung (2007) maintained that m-Learning is a new field wherein learning is delivered to the right person, at the right time, and in the right place, by use of mobile computing devices, which leaves scholars in the field with the discussion that it is very likely to become an integral, organic part of self-directed learning and continuous education for adult learners.

Social Networks and Adult Learners’ Mobility in their Learning Processes

Social networks have considerable potential for adult learners and are cost-effective because they not only support the type of learning that is socially networked, but also the type of knowledge that is constructed by learners recreating the content themselves. As such, instructors should consider the vitality of creating meaningful and relevant learning experiences for their adult learners. Current literature on adult learners’ use of social networks in their learning processes informs that social media tools allow them to continue to autonomously learn the world outside of their classroom first through modeling their teachers and their peers, and finally, by themselves with support of mobile technologies. Mazman and Usluel (2009) found that social media enable adult learners to collaboratively interact with their lecturers and engage with other students on a common platform where they all share the same interests. When learning of adult students is under focus, these adult learners ultimately end up mastering an array of competencies in the English language. If so, then instructional designers and instructors should take into account the huge impact of handheld devices on adult learners as they use these tools to socialize and learn via online, social platforms.

Adults’ learning is best facilitated in an environment where students socially network, collaboratively engage in problem solving, and construct knowledge at their own pace (Newby, Lehman, Rushell, & Stepich, 2010). With respect to the collaborative knowledge construction theories, Yang (2012) further found that students learn more conveniently in a mobile learning (m-Learning) environment, for it enables cooperative learning. Students’ use of social networks is promoted by this type of learning, which explains the degree to which adult learners’ use of mobile technologies and social networks comingle. Drawing from the current literature, it is thus feasible to conclude that adult students’ learning experiences with mobile technologies both inside and outside of the classroom environment are optimized.

With the overlap of social networks and mobile technologies, instructors should additionally attend to the most recent pedagogical design frameworks as they create their lesson plans for this learner group. One of the most outstanding m-Learning frameworks that can facilitate a deeper understanding of how social networking and m-Learning comingle is Park’s (2011) four-type pedagogical framework. The framework involves the following four categories: 1) high transactional distance and socialized mobile learning activity, 2) high transactional distance and individualized mobile learning activity, 3) low transactional distance and socialized mobile learning activity, and 4) low transactional distance and individualized learning activity. It is noteworthy that the third category (i.e., low transactional distance and socialized mobile learning). This specific domain of Park’s (2011) m-Learning related framework can have pedagogical implications for learners socializing and learning through SNSs. This third category clearly demonstrates the aforementioned overlap between social networking and learning with mobile tools. The inference in this context should be that as learners get on SNSs by use of mobile technologies for the purpose of socializing, networking, and most importantly, for learning, they become closer to one another and the barriers of geographic distances are almost removed. This (i.e., m-learning—social networking collaboration) could be a good pedagogical analysis for instructional designers to consider at the onset of their instructional design process involving social networks and adult learners.

Another consideration by instructional designers and instructors should be positive communication and dialogue as students and instructors engage in informal learning and teaching activities by mutual use of social media. When creation of an informal learning community is considered by teaching agents who are to design effective instruction, they will also recall Keegan’s (1995) intersubjectivity element embedded in his Equivalency Theory (as cited in Simonson, Smaldino, Albright, & Zvacek, 2012). Intersubjectivity comes to existence between teachers and learners as they recreate an informal learning environment in an artificial manner (Simonson et al., 2012)—or recreate the reality. It is through these socially networked, recreated, online, informal learning communities that instructional designers or teachers can possibly observe the level of unstructured and yet continuous, dialogic interplay between instructors and their adult learners.

An Alternative Learning Technology: Massive Open Online Courses (MOOCs)

MOOCs currently offer alternative educational opportunities on the part of instructional designers, instructors, learners, and educational leaders, in general, MOOCs use strategies similar to those used by social networking sites in order to “connect the masses but with the added benefits of subject matter experts, to facilitate the content, and to coordinate a vast array of free, online materials” (Chen, Barnett, & Stephens, 2013, p. 3). According to Carr and Duderstadt (2012), “some of the areas in which MOOCs have been cited as most beneficial include increased options for accessibility, increased potential for student engagement, and expanded lifelong learning opportunities” (as cited in Chen et al., 2013, p. 4). Other than enhanced accessibility, MOOCs have additional advantages for learners from across the world, such as flexibility and the potential to “democratize higher education in some of the countries” (de Waard, 2011, as cited in Chen et al., 2013, p. 4) that are marginalized and disenfranchised both technology wise and in terms of other resources. For example, according to Leber (2013), “as online education platforms like Coursera, edX, and Udacity burst onto the scene over the past year, backers have talked up their potential to democratize higher education in the countries that have had the least access” (para 1, as cited in Chen et al., 2013, pp. 4-5). Also, contrary to common belief, even younger learners can attend MOOCs, instead of solely adults (e.g., college students, working professionals, etc.).

Advantages

With the emergence of advanced educational technologies, MOOCs have become today’s nontraditional and rather informal modes of learning and teaching environments on demand. Thousands of educators from around the world share the same learning platform only to observe the significant levels of motivation and readiness for learning on the part of their K-12 students. Khan academy is an excellent example to these online learning platforms, where common core standards smoothly apply. Teachers from several states share their students’ educational success stories supported by the guidelines established within common core standards that Khan Academy adopts and implements as one of the most a contemporary and popular distant learning platform. Numerous educators today utilize Khan Academy, which helps make the CCSS come to life in their classrooms. Where lesson planning and preparation of students for real-life situations and scenarios are involved, many teachers refer to these platforms as useful vehicles for teaching with common core standards.

One of the primary goals of MOOCs is to “enhance student engagement [and improve] student outcomes” (Chen et al., 2013, p. 5). Trowler and Trowler (2010) posited that student engagement is the “investment of time, effort, and other relevant resources by both students and their institutions intended to optimize the student experience and enhance the learning outcomes and development of students, and the performance and reputation of the institution” (as cited in Chen et al., 2013, p. 5). MOOCs can help online or distance course instructors deliver that.

Recent research informs that MOOCs can help instructors understand the extent to which is it critical to “recognize the learning styles of students and adapt their teaching strategies accordingly” (Chen Barnett, & Stephens, 2013, p. 5). In the implementation process of MOOCs designed for a much larger number of students, these online course instructors should manage to understand the academic responsibilities of their students and those that they need to demonstrate themselves, along with their own instructional roles—as facilitators. In regards to the large audiences MOOCs are designed to teach, Carr (2012) pointed out that, until today, “more than a million people in the world have taken MOOCs” (Chen et al., 2013, p. 3). McAuley, Stewart, Siemens, and Cormier (2010) additionally provided that “from a pragmatic perspective, MOOCs provide access to large numbers of people who might otherwise be excluded for reasons ranging from time, to geographic location, to formal prerequisites, to financial hardship” (as cited in Chen et al., 2013, p. 6). As such, differentiating online instruction based on a solid analysis of and appreciation for a variety of students’ diverse learning styles is central to student engagement and the overall success of these courses. From a systems perspective, this is a crucial phenomenon to inquire further and in a systemic manner through ongoing, formative feedback, reviews, and promotion of life-ling learning opportunities to a wider range of learners across the globe.

Disadvantages

Some of the challenges currently associated with MOOCs cannot be disregarded. There is an ongoing controversy that surrounds these open, online courses, particularly where “individual instruction, student performance assessment, and long-term administration and oversight” (Chen, Barnett, & Stephens, 2013, p. 6) are involved. These issues need further address, investigation and log-term, sustainable solutions that can sound results within the free, open, online education literature. Some of the higher occupational institutions today reject to join these courses, for they believe that MOOCs contradict with their overarching mission and core academic philosophy.

Furthermore, regarding their free offerings and open format to millions of students from around the world, MOOCs can actually be significantly costly to institutions. In fact, nothing comes for free, especially where business profits are involved. To illustrate, Kolowich (2013b) maintained that “course development assistance through edX can reach upwards of $250,000 per course with an additional $50,000 fee each time the course is offered” (as cited in Chen et al., 2013, p. 7). Also, Chen et al. (2013) underscored that “for instructors who develop their own courses, human resource needs [to] include course development (typically 100 hours) and course management (8-10 hours per week) in addition to existing professorial duties” (p. 7).

Therefore, considering these challenges that MOOCs pose to educational leaders, potential values of MOOCs should be more closely explored. Technologically-driven educational values of MOOCs (i.e., students’ learning development, innovation, engagement, authenticity and meaning of content, motivation, interaction, project-based learning, collaboration, differentiated instructional design, delivery, and assessment, etc.) should be scrutinized with more caution. There is certainly a future for MOOCs; however, as scholars suggest, instructional designers, editors, and learners should not hold their breaths for a breakthrough in this area in the very near future. After all, MOOCs are still in their infancy stage since the offering of Siemens and Downes’ Connectivism and Connective Knowledge course in 2008 and the launch of Coursera in April of 2012 (Chen et al., 2013).

Impact of Social Networks on Deep Learning: Mastery of Learning Reviewed from a Systems Perspective

Without a thorough diagnosis of needs or what and why a new project is much needed in its initiation stage, the project might end up unsustainable or fail to meet the needs of the end users, customers, or its executive sponsors, as Lynch and Roecker (2007) noted. According to Lynch and Roecker (2007), a well-built project charter is prepared laying out the what and why of a project at its onset. A project charter would be highly instrumental as the project manager initiates the project along with the team members.

Systems thinking postulates that successful innovation or change within any educational context or cannot be achieved without including the inextricably linked and interdependent parts of that organization. Based on the premises of the systems approach in online learning design and delivery, finding long-term, sustainable solutions to current problems is essential. Could (2012) pointed out that a problem is a symptom of another problem, and a systems thinker needs to figure out how upstream he can go in order to identify a certain problem within his sphere.

The systems approach was first introduced with the Articulated Instructional Media (AIM) project funded by the Carnegie Corporation (1964 – 1968), and the project was directed by Charles Wedemeyer at the University of Wisconsin in Madison. AIM is regarded as the third generation of social, collaborative, and PMBOLD and delivery. The AIM project stands as the milestone in the history of PMBOLD. This third-generation sustainable learning project gave way to today’s social networking sites and MOOCs. Perceived to be today’s sustainable and green learning, design, and instructional applications, social media sites and MOOCs are accepted as the evolved versions of what is referred to as the genesis of PMBOLD.

In a systematic process, learners from around the world gain opportunities to learn anytime and anywhere as they utilize social networking applications to optimize their learning and personal development. From a systems thinking, all social media tools are pragmatic online technologies where collaborative learning, project and data set management, instructional design, delivery, assessment, and ongoing feedback are all sustainable and fostered. To illustrate, instructional designers and educators utilizing these tools can break instruction down into smaller components, engage in informal learning contexts with their learners and other faculty, and thus create collaborative, online learning environments outside of the classroom environment.

Social Networking Technology with a Systems Approach to Effective Learning and Instruction: Two Instructional Systems Design (ISD) Models towards Learners’ Mastery of Learning

Where systems thinking and systems theories or models are involved, social networks can largely benefit from the following two instructional systems design (ISD) models: 1) Dick and Carey Model formulated by Dick, Carey, and Carey (2009); and 2) Forward Model formulated by Visser and Sclundt-Bodien (2009).

Dick and Carey Instructional Systems (ISD) Design Model

In the process of creating and implementing a successful design and toward attaining the previously targeted learning outcomes, the model urges designers to develop an effective instructional strategy. Strategy development occurs based on the information obtained from the preceding five stages of the model. Data collected through formative evaluation (i.e., one-on-one, small-group, and field trial evaluation) (Reiser & Dempsey, 2007) in the second to last stage of the model lead to system wide evaluation prior to the actual delivery of the designed instruction.

This is an added value of the model, especially because mastery learning (Gagne, Wager, Golas, & Keller, 2005) is sought during educational activities. In the last stage of the entire evaluation process, the worthiness and validity of the test items and formerly set performance objectives are revised. That is, the effectiveness of instruction is reassessed, reexamined, and improved, if necessary, in order to achieve the desired goals, align with the formerly set benchmarks and standards of instruction, as well as reach each planned learning outcome. Regarding this final phase of revision, Dick, Carey, and Carey (2009) stressed that “revision is not a discrete event that occurs at the end of the ID process, but an ongoing process of using information to reassess assumptions and decisions” (p. 8). This statement provided by the authors would not fall contrary to the aforementioned iterative nature (Johnson, 2007) of the entire process.

This classroom-oriented instructional model also “reflects the fundamental design process used in many business, industry, government, and military training settings, as well as the influence of performance technology and the application of computers to instruction” (Gustafson & Branch, 2002, p. 62). Research indicates the effectiveness of the Dick & Carey ID model in the way that it aligns with and is greatly influenced by particular learning theories such as Robert Gagné’s (1985) Conditions of Learning and Fischer and Immordino-Yang’s Cognitive Information Processing (2008) theories (Dick, Carey, & Carey, 2009). According to Reiser and Dempsey (2007), besides the five types or domains of learning he discussed in his book, Gagné (1985) specifically gave a description of nine events of instruction. These are defined as teaching activities he considered critical as educators and instructional designers promote any type of learning outcomes they had targeted prior to the delivery of instruction. Similar events of instruction targeting particular academic gains are also manifested in the Dick and Carey ID model. These instructional events are independent—albeit interrelated— instructional components, which attribute fluidity to the model, particularly when designers prefer to start the process from any step of the sequence. Thus, because of its consistency and alignment with the elements of Gagné’s learning framework, as well as its congruence with brain-based learning approaches, one can find this rather classroom-based model quite powerful in educational environments. It is also noteworthy that the model includes the components of behaviorist, cognitivist, and constructivist approaches to learning.

Backward Instructional Systems Design (ISD) Model

This ISD model predominantly focuses on identifying student learning outcomes (SLOs) in the process of designing instruction. The fundamental principle of the backward design model is to initially focus on designing effective instruction based on identifying SLOs and then work backwards to develop course materials and instruction to help students achieve the desired outcomes. The model is described as a backward design with an “approach to designing curriculum or unit that begins with the end in mind and designs toward the end” (Childre, Sands, & Pope, 2009, p. 7).

In this respect, the model is unequivocally similar to the preceding ID model. As in the case of the Dick and Carey ISD model, gaining mastery of certain skills in the Backward Design Instructional Model is observed as a quite powerful learning technique employed during educational activities. Particularly the first two types of assessment within the Backward Design instructional model can be analogized to the Dick and Carey ISD model, which enables educators to employ effective and efficient performance-based assessments and criteria-referenced tests for their learners towards gaining deeper knowledge and mastering the content.

Advantages of Integrating Social Networks into Learning and Teaching

Creating autonomous, meaningful, interactive, and collaborative learning environments. A plethora online social networking sites (SNSs) has arisen due to today’s technological advances. Some of these are Facebook, Myspace, twitter, wikis, blogs, and virtual worlds (Bicen & Cavus, 2011, as cited in Benson & Morgan, 2014). Welcoming social media tools into the classroom environment is significant, for they are very powerful tools of learning. The members of the net generation (Glenn, 2000) recognize their own important role in helping “shape the future of this increasingly global, interconnected society” (Wesch, 2008, p. 7, as cited in Yildiz & Scharaldi, 2015, p. 259). Through SNSs, students can create new forms of expression and new rules for social behavior. They can share their creative works such as a video they produced, an online, interactive game they designed, or a SNS they customized based on their personal preferences. They can then receive feedback and response from their peers online which is often immediate, and includes breadth of information. Such information exchange greatly adds to these learners’ media skills and knowledge repository, particularly because this online, often asynchronous exchange provides them with the ability to learn, create, and share at their own pace and based on their own learning needs and personal learning styles. These learning gains through social networking explain that the social media and the digital world are very likely to lower barriers to students’ autonomous learning in the digital age.

Today, it is undoubtedly that social networks have demonstrated their ability to encourage collaborative and real-life skills through which learners can transfer their knowledge to real-world scenarios. Thanks to social networking technologies, students can find the opportunity to collaboratively engage in knowledge building communities with their cyber peers and their instructors, and thus feel like they are part of a virtual, yet a legitimate learning environment wherein they can easily socialize. According to Moore and Kearsley (2012), “for many students, this is a valued way of reducing their feeling of isolation” (p. 171). Social networks promote meaningful and much richer communication between learners and learners, learners and faculty, and learners and content. The last type of interaction (i.e., learner-content interaction) stands out among all three, because it allows for a new paradigm shift in today’s digital education: Learner-centered approach to learning versus yesterday’s obsolete teacher-focused education. Finally, group thinking and positive interpersonal interactions among learners, instructors, and the content can provide instructors with an invaluable pedagogical toolkit that facilitates their assessments of their students’ academic performances.

Disadvantages of Incorporating Social Networks into Education

Risks social networking tools pose to learners’ security and privacy. Use of social media networks in learning and instructional design and delivery does not solely comprise positive aspects. Besides their perceived benefits, affordances, and opportunities, learning and instructional practices implemented via social media sites also pose perceived risks and challenges (Cao, Aijan, & Hong, 2013). Cao, Aijan, and Hong (2013) found in their recent work that “external pressure, perceived usefulness, perceived risk and task–technology compatibility is all important in explaining social media use decision” (p. 591). Negative implications of social media use in learning and teaching should be understood especially from an ethical perspective, when the personal and public domains overlap on the same platform.

In another recent research investigating this issue, Aymerich-Franch and Fedele (2014) contended that “as any other revolution in the human history, the incorporation of social media in the different social spheres comes with reactions of both fear and enthusiasm” (p. 57). When “Facebook and Twitter are unsupervised and must be used by instructors” (Tomei & Morris, 2013, p. 89), potential pitfalls of integrating such open public online communities into learning and teaching practices should be fully understood by all of the stakeholders involved in the process of instructional design, delivery, implementation, and evaluation. Instructional design related concerns, in particular, should be brought up by educational leaders and instructional designers who strive to create and deliver sustainable, long-term, and risk-free instructional products for their specific learner populations or groups. Most importantly, these learning agents should raise awareness of the fact that designing secure, meaningful, motivational, and memorable (Allen & Sites, 2012) collaborative and interactive project-based learning environments.

Based on the findings of their recent study, Aymerich-Franch and Fedele (2014) indicated that besides their positive implications, social media tools such as Facebook, Twitter, Skype, weblogs, and other such SNSs also raise concerns on the part of the students. Rather than indicating how the faculty intend to use SNSs in their teaching or what function they perceive SNSs to have in their instructional design and delivery, the study results actually demonstrate the perceptions and attitudes of several college-level students towards their SNSs use. These students’ responses appear to have affected the findings of this inquiry in the way that it turns out that social media do, indeed, raise serious concerns and moral questions in the minds of these young users/learners. The large sample of the study includes university students who constantly utilize SNSs as part of their private sphere. According to Livingstone (2008), “despite the strong connection of this first generation of digital natives and new media, the increasing use of social media among teenagers and teens is also of concern of both public opinion and scholars” (Aymerich-Franch & Fedele, 2014, p. 56).

The findings of this research indicate that social media may impose risks for these young people, particularly on their privacy. Youth seem to have created a bedroom culture, which, in the digital learning landscape, represents these young learners’ private, leisure activities they hold separate from their school or family-related obligations. Simply put, they want to socialize by use of these social media tools, instead of using them primarily as a vessel for learning. Some studies even show that “students believe that certain social media such as Facebook are not designed for education but for socializing” (Hewitt & Forte, as cited in Aymerich-Franch & Fedele, 2014, p. 58). On these digital spaces, young learners tend to create for themselves identities that are both individual and group-based and what they experience here is a sense of intimacy and community (Aymerich-Franch & Fedele, 2014).

As paradoxical as it sounds and although they do not want public sphere to clash with their private sphere, young people seem to frequently use social media publicly. Because of this reason, the study results reveal that students often opt out of using them as part of their private lives. In other words, they do not want their personal boundaries to be crossed. In support of this argument, the study results inform that as they choose to rather learn in an informal manner through multiples modes of media that help enhance both their verbal and visual literacy (Clark & Mayer, 2011), they tend to exclude formal educational activities from their private sphere, an issue that raises further questions about the educational and instructional value of use of social networking sites.

Inherent Problems with Learning via Social Learning Networking Technologies in Relation to Identity Management and Privacy: Analogy to Focault's Panopticon Design

According to Benson and Morgan (2014), privacy refers to the “right of individuals to freely decide the aspects of their lives to remain in the private domain and those belonging to the public one” (p. 57). However, the more public these young learners go by using these social media tools, the more vulnerable their identities become to potential online predators who might stalk them, by picking them as their cyber victims. Also, while some students report that they positively perceive social media to be a potential vehicle for learning (e.g., watching faculty’s lectures, engaging in discussions with the faculty and peers, listening to topic-related podcasts, etc.), some others (almost other half of the respondents involved in the study) state that they have serious concerns as to whether social media networks might cause issues with identity management and privacy. Thus, students appear to reject to use social media they normally “use in the private arena for educational purposes in order to keep a separation between their social and academic life” (Hewitt & Forte, 2006; Selwyn, 2009, as cited in Aymerich-Franch & Fedele, 2014, p. 58).

Young users of these social media sites “may reveal information that may be used for marketing and commercial purposes, which, according to Barnes (2006), makes their private spheres open to superfluous online advertisements. An additional finding on the topic is that these social media sites may “allow for high levels of surveillance. Governmental agencies, college officials or parents can access networking sites while young users think of it as private spaces” (as cited in Aymerich-Franch & Fedele, 2014, p. 57), which, once again, precludes these young users from trusting these sites or perceiving them to be completely private. A good metaphor to use at this point can help describe such a surveillance mechanism, and that would be Focault’s Panopticon design (Barnes, 2006; Fuchs, 2010, as cited in Aymerich-Franch & Fedele, 2014, p. 58).

Social Networking as a Sustainable Application for Deeper Learning: Evaluations from a Systems Perspective

The technologies included in AIM included print study materials, programs broadcast by radio and television, recorded audiotapes guides and correspondence tutoring, kits for home experiments, local library resources, and telephone conferences. Also, what were articulated into the AIM program were basically student counseling and support, as well as discussions held in local study groups, and students’ use of laboratories at universities during periods of vacation.

The role of AIM was that it was the first pilot project simulating online and distance learning and teaching as a total system (Moore & Kearsley, 2012) with which social media are accepted to have apparent commonalities in the way students learn and instructors plan, manage, design, deliver, and evaluate their instructional products. AIM was a project that tested whether the theory that the roles and responsibilities of the instructor could be decentralized and whether teaching could be improved with a shift in teachers’ and learners’ roles. Similar to their foundations, social media applications also function based on the premises of promoting student-centered learning approach—and with very little guidance and assistance from the instructor—in both blended and online educational settings.

Educational Leadership Described in a Development Project Launched on Social Media

Recruitment strategies in the workplace. Today’s learning organizations and effective leaders have begun to ensure that their employees receive not only formal training, but also informal forms of education and professional development for the entire success of the workplace. For example, these organizations have begun to adapt to current demands of the global economies today, by fully adopting social media tools to several of their departments. Muyia and Nafukho (2015) underscored that “as social media penetrates our everyday lives, organizations and human resource development professionals are looking at how to leverage social media tools to enhance workplace learning and development” (p. 93).

There is no doubt that today’s leaders are searching for the top talent; that is, the type of diverse workers—or global citizens—that have technical expertise that calls for a vast array of digital skills and a high level of adaptability to both formal and informal learning settings, with quite a lot of cultural sensitivity and receptivity for constructive feedback. Workers today need to be able to respond to the wants and needs of the customers and consumers. Hunt (2010) drew attention to a Career Builder survey, which demonstrates that today’s employers increasingly use social media for recruitment purposes. (Hunt, 2010) also discussed that “at this point in time, social media neither can, nor should replace traditional recruiting methods; however, it is necessary for organizations to supplement their efforts online. Fueling traditional recruiting strategies with social media requires understanding how the different sites operate” (p. 38).

An Exemplary Social Media Learning Platform Initiative: Utilizing Facebook in the Classroom

Students’ and instructor’s social media use in this advanced level, adult, academic preparation English as a second language (ESL) course facilitates their positive communication and dialogue (see figures 1-3). In this informal and online learning context, Keegan’s (1995) intersubjectivity element embedded in his Equivalency Theory, which comes to existence between teachers and learners, is re-created in an artificial manner (Simonson et al., 2012). Furthermore, with this example of an informal learning community facilitated with Facebook used as a social networking tool, it is possible to observe the level of unstructured and yet continuous, dialogic interplay between the instructor of this course and her adult ESL students.

Such a dialogic process not only indicates that this is a rather informal learning context, but also demonstrates what Moore (1990) calls as positive and encouraging teaching behaviors for the entire learner group in this adult ESL academic preparation course. Most importantly, the fact that all of the assignments, tasks, class schedule, as well as class reminders are designed and delivered in a guided manner can lead one to appreciate the type of learning/teaching environment that is constructivist in nature. Given this scaffolding aspect of the course content that is designed, developed, and delivered under the instructor’s initial guidance, and based on the ongoing, positive, and immediate feedback she provides, one can observe the constructivist approach to learning adopted and implemented by the course instructor in this academic preparation ESL course.

Figure 1. This figure demonstrates a group of adult students’ online interactions by use of social media (i.e., Facebook) as an informal learning community. The educational context in which this social networking technology tool was utilized is an advanced level academic preparation ESL course at a higher educational organization.
Figure 2. Adult ESL students’ use of social media (i.e., Facebook) as an informal learning community
Figure 3. Adult learners’ use of social media (i.e., Facebook) as an informal, self-directed learning community

FUTURE RESEARCH DIRECTIONS

With this comprehensive literature review on the role and impact of social networking technology on adult learners’ mastery of their leaning through autonomy, ownership, and deeper learning, it is the expectation that instructional designers, educational technologists, and simply, educators seeking to design, develop, deliver, and assess innovative, authentic, engaging, motivational, and meaningful content (Allen & Sites, 2012) can greatly benefit from the comprehensive information provided in this review. Yet, further review needs to be conducted in regards to the role, benefits, and opportunities of this technology, as well as further trends, issues, and challenges that social media applications might pose on new design solutions and/or curricula rooted in the principles of PMBOLD and systems thinking. Special focus should be on particular design models tailored towards the specific learner group discussed in this review. Only then can the pedagogical, instructional, technological, and PMBOLD related implications for use of social networks in learning be better comprehended and appreciated.

CONCLUSION

For the purpose of this literature review, social networking technology was selected as a vehicle for presenting in-depth knowledge regarding both the best practices and some of the risks and challenges that this technology poses to the field of learning, blended and online curriculum design, and teaching. One of the most commonly recognized factors to consider in the process of creating collaborative, interactive, and project-based online learning environments that involve a convergence of pedagogy and technology is attending to the learning needs, differences, and preferences of today’s digital natives (Prensky, 2010). This instructional design principle holds true across all disciplines, offering pragmatic, long-term, and sustainable solutions in educational contexts supported with social media tools. From a systems thinking, this literature review presented the reader a panoramic view to the pros and cons of creating effective and fruitful learning environments for learners who master their own learning experiences through digital and networked learning.
