KEY TERMS AND DEFINITIONS

AIM: AIM was the first test of the idea of distance education as a total system (Moore & Kearsley, 2012). It tested the viability of the theory that the functions of the teacher could be decentralized, and teaching could be improved when those functions were assembled by a team of specialists and delivered through various media. The technologies included in AIM included printed study guides and correspondence tutoring, programs broadcast by radio and television, recorded audiotapes, telephone conferences, kits for home experiments, and local library resources. Also, what was articulated into the program was student support and counseling, discussions in local study groups, and use of university laboratories during vacation periods.

Autonomous Learning: Type of learning that is conducted in a self-directed or self-regulated manner. Often associated with older adolescents and adult learners.

Cognitive Processing: Taking in all of the information collected via motor brain functions and through sensory acts and operating these data (i.e., storing, recovering, transforming, and implementing) effectively and intelligently.

Instructional Design (ID): Also referred to as instructional systems design (ISD), ID is the systematic practice of design, development, and delivery of both physical and digital instructional resources towards efficient, effective, engaging, empowering, contextualized, and authentic learning and instruction.

Intersubjectivity: That which is shared by and existing between more than one conscious mind.

Millennials: Those who were born in this millennium (i.e., 2000).

MOOCs: Online, interactive, open-source courses with unlimited participation by attendees who have open access to these courses on the web.

Panopticon Design: A metaphor used to a surveillance mechanism pertaining to an individual’s right to claim their privacy.

PMBOLD: Acronym for Project Management-based Online Learning Design.

SLOs: Acronym for Student Learning Outcomes.

Chapter 7

Use of a Dashboard in a University English Course

  • Dennis Foung
    The University of British Columbia, Canada
  • Joanna Kwan
    https://orcid.org/0000-0001-5569-9179
    The Hong Kong Polytechnic University, Hong Kong

ABSTRACT

One of the common uses of artificial intelligence in higher education is learning dashboards, which aim at collecting and analyzing student information and providing feedback to students. Despite limited studies on dashboards in language learning, this chapter describes the design, development, and evaluation of a dashboard designed for a university English course, aiming to provide insights to learning designers, teachers, and researchers. The Course Diagnostic Report was developed after consulting with subject leaders, and it was piloted with students. The results of the pilot showed students were satisfied with this dashboard, showing average scores of the previous cohort of students and suggestions for improvement. Still, students requested better suggestions and sophisticated visualizations to understand their standing in the course. By describing the whole process of development and evaluation, this chapter provides insights on how future dashboards could be designed with better visualizations and the potential of this project for teachers with little knowledge of dashboards.

INTRODUCTION

The concept of Artificial Intelligence (AI) is not new in education. Similar to the growth of any emerging fields of studies, AI research in education has grown from conceptual studies to empirical studies with practical implications to learning and teaching in an educational context. Roll and Wylie (2016) analyzed papers published in a journal on AI in education for 40 years and found that the number of empirical studies grew from 5% in 1994 to 71% in 2014. Generally speaking, AI refers to the tasks performed by computers to simulate intelligent human behavior (Hwang et al., 2020). In the case of education, this can mean simulating the roles of teachers to give feedback, provide course information, or teach.

Dashboards are one of the applications of AI in education. According to Verbert et al. (2013), a learning dashboard can be described as an informatic application that collects personal information (e.g., behaviors, habits and interests) about people (i.e., students) and helps individuals to improve self-knowledge through analyses of the collected information. For example, the dashboard can collect information about students’ personalities and academic performance and provide them with recommendations on subject selection. In the context of higher education, various learning management systems (LMSs), such as Blackboard and Moodle, provide dashboards for students to help them understand their progress in the course. These dashboards are embedded as part of the course site in the LMS. While Aljohani et al. (2019) showed that engagement with these dashboards improves engagement with other course tools as well, these dashboards are context-free, and their success can be affected by how the teacher designs the course.

Due to the limitations of these dashboards, a university English program was motivated to design and develop a tailor-made learning board for students to better inform them of their progress and how to address their weaknesses. This paper reports the design, development and evaluation of the learning dashboard, the Course Diagnostic Report (CDR). The objectives of this paper are as follows:

  • 1. allow other learning designers, teachers and AI researchers to explore further possibilities in their contexts; and
  • 2. contribute to the growing knowledge on the development of learning dashboards.

First, the paper will describe the design process, including the stakeholders and their perspectives. This will be followed by a description of the development process, including how the features demanded by stakeholders were realized in the platform and their technological considerations. The paper will then describe how the CDR is being evaluated and how it is perceived by students and teachers. The paper will conclude with some lessons learned and recommendations for other AI researchers, practitioners and frontline teachers.

The paper will make a significant contribution to the field because the CDR is highly cost-effective, and a “homegrown” learning dashboard is not common in the language learning context.

LITERATURE REVIEW

Trend of Learning Dashboard Studies

Over the last two decades, the trends of data-tracing and using digital technologies in education have motivated tertiary institutions to consider ways of analyzing and visualizing the large amount of data collected from LMSs to aid learning and improve pedagogy (Thomas et al., 2017). A growing number of studies have been focusing on the increasingly common use of learning dashboards. Schwendimann et al. (2017) defined a learning dashboard as a combination and demonstration of single or multiple visualizations of various learning indicators about learners, processes and contexts. Kim et al.’s (2016) learning dashboard was developed to visualize factors like learners’ time and frequency of log-in and their frequency of using the dashboard. Usage frequency was found to be positively related to learners’ learning achievement. Sedrekyan et al. (2020) examined the relationship between dashboard design and the regulatory cognitive and behavioral mechanisms underlying the learning process. They reported a case study on using a learning analytics dashboard to promote learning regulation as a demonstration of their idea.

Other existing studies on dashboards (e.g., Elaachak et al., 2015; Mottus et al., 2015; Ramos-Soto et al., 2015), however, have focused on explaining and proofing concepts without reaching the aims of identifying appropriate information for stakeholders or effective methods of data presentation—let alone evaluating the impact of learning dashboards (Baker, 2007; Few, 2006; Schwendimann et al., 2017). Most of these studies concentrated on university settings, which provide a larger sampling scale and more flexibility for learning analytics (LA) research compared to school settings. However, these studies lacked specific visualizations for addressing learning and teaching patterns or a consensus on the usage frequency and patterns of learning dashboards (Schwendimann et al., 2017). Moreover, there have been very few LA studies in a Hong Kong higher education context (e.g., Choi et al., 2018; Foung, 2019; Wong & Li, 2019), where discussions about learning dashboards remain unpopular.

Learning Analytics and Dashboards in Language Learning

LA has started to play a significant role in the context of language learning. Wen and Song (2020) investigated the use of LA for collaborative language learning in classrooms through the integration of LA, learning design and teacher inquiry. Their study suggested that straightforward LA indicators and visualization could reflect the process of collaborative writing based on sound theories, which could inform learning design and teacher inquiry. Moreover, they found that it is critical for teachers to believe in the innovative teaching approach and be keen to collaborate with researchers and to focus on their professional development for LA to inform the other two factors effectively. Peeters et al. (2020) studied the self-regulated learning tactics of students during peer collaboration on academic writing tasks on social networking sites by applying LA methods. They concluded that students’ use of self-regulated learning tactics is positively correlated with their learning performance and that relevant support mechanisms are needed in computer-supported collaborative learning. Viberg et al. (2020) introduced the MALLAS framework, aiming to assist learning researchers and designers while supporting second language learners in their self-regulated learning in mobile contexts using LA.

There are few studies on the use of learning dashboards in a language learning context. An online dashboard was used to gather information about non-native English-speaking students’ engagement with Blackboard’s LMS in a graduate-level research writing course, which assisted teachers in their course design and personalization of feedback strategies to address students’ needs and abilities (Link & Li, 2015). Similarly—but more learner-oriented—dashboards were created to examine learning factors, including course outlines, intended usage of activities and learners’ actual online behaviors, and to inform online language learners about the indicators in their language-learning patterns that lead to successful or unsuccessful learning (Thomas et al., 2017). The TimeTracker App was designed to help second language learners who are adult migrant workers keep track of the amount of time spent studying the target language and its influence on their engagement and motivation in the continual study of the host language (Viberg et al., 2021).

For learning dashboards to be effective and successfully implemented in any context (not confined to language learning), it is crucial to consider the factors reflected in the studies mentioned above: stakeholders’ (i.e., teachers, professional staff and students) familiarity with the use of online applications, tools and dashboards; learning and teaching indicators and goals; and user-oriented customization of dashboard design and visualization. These factors provide the foundation for designing and developing the CDR for our university English program.

CONTEXT OF THE PROJECT

Language Background of Students

This research was conducted in an English-medium university in Hong Kong, in which nearly 96% of students speak Chinese/Cantonese as their first language (The Hong Kong Polytechnic University, 2020). That is, most students are English as a second language users/speakers, who have fulfilled the minimum English language requirement for university admission: Level 3 in the English Language subject of the Hong Kong Diploma of Secondary Education Examination (HKDSE) for local admission; or an overall band score of 6 on the International English Language Testing System (IELTS) or a score of 80 on the Internet-based or 550 on the paper-based Test of English as a Foreign Language (TOEFL) for international admission (The Hong Kong Polytechnic University, 2021). However, most of the students directly promoted from secondary schools are new to academic English in higher education.

Exposure of Students to Online Platforms

The university has been subscribing to the Blackboard Collaborate as the LMS, Microsoft Office 365 for internal staff and student mailing and daily work applications and Turnitin for assignment submission and plagiarism checking, to name a few. Upon entering the university, students are given access to the university portal, where all these online learning resources and applications can be found. Corresponding training seminars, guidelines and information technology support services are provided to students (ITSO, The Hong Kong Polytechnic University, 2021); thus, in general, they have the necessary knowledge for using the university portal and online learning resources.

Nonetheless, students’ usage of these online learning platforms is limited to obtaining brief information, such as course updates, teachers’ scanned handwritten/typed feedback on assignments and assessment results, downloading course materials, uploading assignments, completing compulsory online exercises and checking university emails. Students are not able to track their learning progress by course, semester or academic year on these online platforms. Problems often arise when students are not interested in using these platforms or resources frequently or have even abandoned these platforms, which results in missing important online course information and updates. In light of these issues, the CDR tool, aiming to act as an early alert system (Foung, 2019), would attract students’ attention by providing assessment results and suggestions for improvement while contributing new ideas to make fuller use of similar learning dashboards/platforms.

Details of English Courses

According to the University’s English language requirements, students must take two English for Academic Purposes (EAP) courses (among three of them) to learn the genre and gain fundamental academic writing and speaking skills. Both courses involve learning to use academic sources and the assessment requirements of writing academic essays and delivering academic oral presentations. The courses to be taken depend on students’ English exam results obtained in HKDSE. The CDR tool focused on these three courses, which are core English courses taken by three-quarters of students. To satisfy the intended learning outcomes of these courses, students are required to complete three assessments in each of these courses: two writing assignments and one speaking assignment. The genres of writing and speaking assignment depends on courses. For example, in one of the courses, the first two assignments were a 500-word problem-solution essay, an 800-word discursive essay; and this is followed by the academic presentation assessment. In all three courses, students are given an overall assessment grade and a grade for every writing component, including content, organization, language and referencing, or for every speaking component, including content, delivery, language and pronunciation/fluency. The CDR was developed based on the overall grade and components grades of the first writing assignment in each course. The mechanism of the CDR will be explained in detail in the next section.

DESIGN AND DEVELOPMENT OF COURSE DIAGNOSTIC REPORT

Development of Course Diagnostic Report

Due to the developments in AI and dashboards, a project team was established to develop a dashboard for an English for Academic Purpose program (three courses) in a Hong Kong university). This intelligent dashboard was named “Course Diagnostic Report” (CDR). This section describes the development and features of the CDR, along with key concerns/problems identified during the development process.

Planning and Development

The CDR project has gone through several main stages of development. After the project team was assembled, with a teaching faculty and a project assistant/programmer, the team brainstormed ideas for the project, consulted stakeholders, developed prototypes and evaluated their effectiveness. This project was supported by a project grant administered by the university.

During the brainstorming stage, a range of possible algorithms, development languages and hosting platforms were suggested. Regarding algorithms, it was suggested to mine past students’ data to develop a decision tree or some other sophisticated data mining methods to predict if students would do poorly in the course. In terms of development language and hosting platform, it was originally suggested that an application on a mobile device be developed in some designated programming language. There were also suggestions to develop separate student and teacher versions so that both parties could maximize the tool. These ideas were presented to five subject leaders who coordinate these three courses. Their ideas had to be carefully considered because their support in the use of these tools, despite having project funding, was crucial for implementing the pilot successfully.

During the consultation stage, the project team presented initial ideas to the subject leaders and sought their support. While the subject leaders, in general, supported the development of CDR, their concerns/interests were quite different from those of the project team. A detailed discussion of these meetings (as a discussion on innovation in higher education) was documented in another paper by Foung (2019). From a project operation perspective, Table 1 offers an overview of the differences between the project team and subject leaders that can be resolved.

Subject leaders believed that the CDR, as a project, would impact the workload of teachers, and that was an influential starting point. Accordingly, it was decided that there would only be one student platform that students could use with minimal assistance (so that teachers do not need to solve technical problems). Specifically, the CDR platform should be simple and require no installation; hence, simple HTML pages were considered most desirable. To allow the CDR itself to provide simple justification for the feedback given to students, simple averages were suggested instead of more complicated data mining methodologies. One very specific concern of subject leaders was the language/text to be presented in the CDR. The subject leaders did not want the text to cause any stress or anxiety for the students (or subsequently to teachers). Instead, the text presented to students should be motivating and show them clear ways to improve. Therefore, the project team worked closely with the students to develop the texts to be presented in the CDR.

Table 1. Differences in Perspectives between Stakeholders

Project Team Subject Leaders Resolution
Accuracy of prediction Easy to understand/justify Using simple averages
Teacher and student version Student version (for teacher workload) Student version
Platform compatibility Easy-to-use/simple implementation Simple HTML-based
-- Language to motivate Further consultation needed (prototype)

After the consultation stage, the project team was confident that the fundamental development principles could guide the development of the CDR. The part-time student programmer took charge of the development. The CDR consisted of several HTML pages supported with jQuery (i.e., with IF operation based on students’ input), and the student programmer developed one prototype for testing. One major challenge was that the student programmer was busy and the development requirement (i.e., page design and wording) needed to be appropriate. The student programmer was unable to make changes to the CDR after the initial draft. The project leader had a basic understanding of jQuery and HTML and was able to continue with the development process. Several prototypes were developed to obtain the subject leaders’ feedback, and further revisions were made to enhance readability or provide accurate resource links for the CDR. Both the project team and subject leaders were pleased with a final prototype for piloting (described in the Results section). The following section describes the features of this prototype.

Piloted Course Diagnostic Report

Operation

The CDR was designed to be easy to use, motivating and interpretable. After considering the above-mentioned design principles and numerous rounds of consultation with subject leaders, CDR prototypes were developed for piloting with students. The features of the CDR that were piloted with students are described below.

The tool was hosted as a web page accessible through a public URL. Students were expected to use the tool to understand their progress after completing the first assignment in their course. After clicking on the URL, students are presented with a landing page with a general introduction of the CDR and radio buttons for entering their grades from the first assignment (Figure 1). The first assignments of all courses included four component grades and one overall grade. For example, the first assignment for one of the courses was a problem-solution essay, and the grades to be entered for this assignment included Content Development, Organization, Language Use, Academic Conventions and the Overall Assignment grade. After entering their grades with the radio buttons, students are shown a “diagnostic report” page with the grades entered, general comments and actions students could take to do better on future assignments. After viewing this report online, students can enter their email address and have this report sent to their email.

Figure 1. CDR landing page

Features of the “Diagnostic Report” Page

This page includes three important elements (Figure 2). The first is the grades entered by the students. One obvious reason for this is to allow students to check the accuracy of the grades entered. Second, there is a general comment: “Above Average,” “Below Average” or “Bottom 5%.” The general comment is based on the student’s average score and the average score obtained by the previous cohort of students. For example, if the grade entered is better than the average obtained by the previous cohort of students, “Above Average” will be displayed. If the grade is worse than the average, “Below Average” will be displayed. If the grade is among the bottom 5% of students in the previous cohort, “Bottom 5%” will be displayed. This general comment allows students to understand their standing among students in the previous cohort. Third, there is a column on actions to be taken by the student. If students are above average, there will be a general comment asking them to keep enhancing their skills. If and only if students are below average or among the bottom 5% of students, they will be presented with actions they should take. These actions include course-based activities designed especially for the course, materials from the independent learning center selected by the subject leaders, relevant library books and online courses designed by the same unit (i.e., language center). These materials and activities were all selected and suggested by the respective subject leaders to help students who receive a lower grade in certain assessment components. For example, if students achieve a “below average” grade in language, it would be recommended that they complete course-based language activities, specific grammar materials in the independent language learning center and grammar courses designed by the center. Most of these include links so that students can access these resources when they are ready.

Figure 2. Screenshot of the “diagnostic report” page

All three courses have their own CDRs (i.e., with different content in the reports). They are accessed by different URLs. The design of all pages for the CDRs of all courses is very similar. The CDR landing pages are identical except for the radio buttons available for the component grades for the respective first assignment in different courses (i.e., the components grades can be different for different assignments). Despite a similar design and layout, the content on the diagnostic report page varies for the CDRs in different courses. The averages are different across courses, and the materials recommended are specific for the course. However, the CDRs look similar across courses, and they provide similar features to students.

EVALUATION OF COURSE DIAGNOSTIC REPORT

Methodology

After designing an initial prototype with stakeholders, the CDR was subjected to evaluation by another group of major stakeholders, the students. The following describes the evaluation process of the prototype and presents the results. In general, the CDR was piloted with students taking three courses. After trying the CDR, the students were asked to complete a questionnaire and were invited to participate in a focus group interview.

Participants

A total of 569 students in their first or second year of university study in the disciplines of business, civil and environmental engineering and health sciences participated in the pilot of the CDR. These students were completing one of the courses in which the CDR was available to them. Due to the data collection arrangements, these students should have completed the first assignment for the course. Depending on the courses they are taking, their English proficiency levels vary. Within the entire sample, 59% of students attained Level 3 or below (equivalent to IELTS 5.48 or below) in English in HKDSE; 29% attained Level 4 (equivalent to IELTS 6.31–6.51); and 12% attained Level 5 or above (equivalent to IELTS 6.81 or above). The project team felt that the sample represented the actual student population.

Data Collection

The CDR was piloted in the middle of a semester when teaching was ongoing to avoid conflicts with any assessment due dates or examinations during the semester. Students taking the English courses had just received the results of their first assessment with component grades that were needed for the CDR pilot.

Procedures

In addition to the official ethical clearance obtained from the university, prior permissions from the teachers were obtained by the research team to conduct the research in their classrooms. Thirty-nine class visits were conducted in 23 days. During the class visit, the researcher(s) spent the first 5 minutes introducing the CDR project and the CDR to students. Students were then given 5 to 10 minutes to attempt to use the tool by entering their first assessment component grades, during which a member of the project team assisted students who required further clarification. Students were encouraged to input their actual grades to check their performance on the first assessment in comparison to that of the students who took the same course in previous cohorts. Students were assured that the inputted data would not be collected or stored on the server.

Next, the project team member distributed the questionnaire to all students and invited them to complete it following the CDR trial. Students were reminded that their participation in the research was completely voluntary, that it would not have any effect on their subject grades and that all data collected through the questionnaires were solely for academic research purposes and would be kept or reported anonymously. Students who did not wish to participate were instructed to return a blank questionnaire. A total of 569 completed questionnaires were collected.

To collect more information about students’ perceptions of the tool, the project team member invited one or two groups of one to four volunteers in each class to participate in a 30-minute focus group interview immediately after the completion of the questionnaires. Nine group interviews, involving 28 students, were conducted, in English. Prior to each interview, all interviewees were given further written details about the project and were required to provide signed consent to participate in the interview. Every group or individual was given several minutes of preparation time, and all interviews were conducted in English and were voice-recorded with consent.

Instruments

Questionnaire: The questionnaire contains three parts: Part A includes nine questions using a 5-point scale to indicate agreement; Part B asks for participants’ specifics, including gender, program of study, HKDSE English Language subject results, grades of the first assessment of the English course and other English exam results if available; and Part C asks for participants’ full name and signed consent.

Interview Guide: The six interview questions are about student-users’ thoughts about the usefulness and strengths and weaknesses of the CDR, the usefulness of the improvement suggestions provided in the CDR, their behavior when trying the tool, their English learning motivation after using the tool, additional functions to include and additional improvement suggestions to be included in the CDR.

Data Analysis

As a project evaluation study, the collected data were subjected to simple descriptive analysis in order to understand how students perceived the CDR. The descriptive statistics from the questionnaire were computed, including the mean and standard deviation. Simple content analysis was conducted with the interview scripts. The project team examined the interview scripts and identified themes that have been recurring in the data. This evaluation study aimed to identify the most common theme identified by students in terms of their perception of the tool and action to be taken. Other themes were considered to be less important in the current study.

Results

Generally speaking, students were satisfied with the CDR and viewed it as a useful tool for their learning. Table 2 below summarizes the results of the questionnaires distributed after students tried the CDR. They believed that the tool was useful, and all means for perception were above three (out of five), and they found the comparison of results (mean: 3.47) more useful than the recommendations provided (mean: 3.27). The students felt that other courses should use a similar tool. The small standard deviations (<1) among these questions suggest that students had similar opinions regarding their general perception of the CDR, as their ratings were clustered around the means.

Another positive finding was the generally high willingness (with a mean score of three or above for three of the recommended actions) to take action after using the tool. The students were most willing to refer to non-course-based online resources (mean: 3.27) followed by course-based online activities and online courses. There were least likely to consult suggested books, with a mean score lower than three (out of five; 2.95). These findings suggest that students not only found the tool useful but that it also caused them to refer to some resources that they might not have referred to without this tool.

Table 2. Summary of descriptive statistics

Interview data were subjected to simple content analysis by identifying recurring themes in the data in relation to the perception of the tool and action to be taken. Among the themes identified regarding the perception of the tool, students were most interested in knowing more about their standing in the course. Some students explicitly mentioned the need to know where they are with the given grades, while others asked for more visualizations so that their positions could be more precisely presented. The following quotes from students reflect these points:

The tool was useful, especially the component of the grade comparison. This student would like the grade comparison to be as thorough as to show the real position that the students achieved in each component of the assignment in the class or cohort, along with the grade range. (Student F)

As for grade comparison, a pie chart of students’ individual position in the cohort was suggested to be added. (Student K)

In terms of action to be taken, students requested the provision of more specific online materials, including a specific online language exercise or a specific page of an e-book. They felt that they had completed the required course-based activities and were bored, so they needed to have more non-course-based activities. The following quotes exemplify what students thought about the action to be taken after using the tool.

…prefer more concrete advice and at least 2 direct links to relevant resources for each writing component. (Student O)

The suggestion of doing IndiWork was meaningless because IndiWork itself felt like a piece of compulsory homework, of which the students might have already done at least 50%. (Student C)

To summarize, students generally found the tool to be useful, especially for grade comparison, but they would prefer that the comparison be presented as a visualization that shows them precisely where they stand in the course. In terms of action to take, students were willing to take action based on using the tool, but they would prefer online and specific language exercises rather than course-based materials, which they were obliged to complete. This feedback gave the research team more insight on how the tool could be improved and how dashboard research could move forward.

IMPLICATIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

The results from the pilot study offer new perspectives on dashboards. The following paragraph attempts to consolidate the lessons learned in this project and suggests how dashboards could be further developed.

Importance of Consultation/Dilemma

During the planning process, it was evident that different stakeholders perceived the needs of dashboards very differently. While the team sought to pursue innovation through algorithm development and technological advancements, stakeholders such as subject leaders were more concerned with workloads and student motivation. There has been discussion of the “right information for stakeholders” in past dashboard research, but the focus was more on how the dashboard could be designed for the use of different stakeholders so that the “right information” would be provided to meet their needs (Few 2007; Schwendimann et al., 2017). This pilot study expands this understanding and suggests that different stakeholders may need to play an influential role in ensuring the “right information” not only for themselves but also for other stakeholders. Therefore, consultation with different stakeholders (e.g., technical team and subject leaders) on the needs of other stakeholders (e.g., students) is important.

Unexpected Use

While the project team expected students to use the CDR in certain ways, the students looked for ways to enhance their experience. Some stronger students reported that they intentionally entered a poor grade so that they could see the suggestions to improve the component grades. This was unexpected because the original instructions were to enter their actual grades. Additionally, stronger students were not expected to take any major actions to improve. This finding has two implications.

First, informing student progress involves more than just students’ current progress. Verbert et al. (2013) described a dashboard as an application for collecting students’ personal information. In this project, the unexpected use suggested that students’ personal information includes their expected information or information needed for planning (i.e., recommendations for improving themselves in the future, even though they do not need to). Second, it is important to note that students will look for ways to enhance their experience, and thus flexibility should be allowed. For example, if students’ grades were retrieved directly from the learning management system and the dashboard recommendation could only be generated in this way, students would not be able to enhance their own experience. Therefore, more flexibility should be allowed in the dashboard design.

Need for Visualization/Precise Position

There is a need to personalize the visualization so that students know where they stand when comparing with other students. Students’ need for personalized feedback is not surprising and has been highlighted in the literature (Link & Li, 2015). Moreover, past studies have described the use of multiple visualizations (Mottus et al., 2015; Ramos-Soto et al., 2015). This study provides another concrete example of “personalized feedback.” In this pilot study, students were interested in knowing precisely where they stood compared to students from previous cohorts. Therefore, “personalized feedback” is not limited to what they need to do (i.e., recommendations) but also includes how they are doing. Future projects need to consider the personalized progress of students in addition to offering personalized recommendations.

From a student perspective, the need for sophisticated visualization is clear. However, this raises a question for technical specialists: How can a simple HTML dashboard provide sophisticated features? The project team are not web developers, but there seems to be no simple solution to technically cater for sophisticated visualization. In other words, there may be a need to hire developers to further explore technical options to provide sophisticated visualizations for students. Future project teams should explore this possibility and strike a balance between simple technical design and the needs of students.

Cost-Effective and Non-Specialist Solutions for All

This project showcases an effective solution for any teacher who wants to try a dashboard, and it is recommended that teachers who are interested in a dashboard start with a similarly simple solution. This project included a simple interface that was still cost-effective and easy to use. While many other past projects (e.g., Elaachak et al., 2015; Mottus et al., 2015; Ramos-Soto et al., 2015) included well-designed dashboards, they were technically sophisticated and required a developer or (in-house) computer scientist to develop. This project, based on results from a student pilot study, was as effective as those in previous studies and offered a satisfying dashboard experience to students. However, this dashboard, the CDR, only requires non-specialist computing knowledge to develop and maintain (HTML with jQuery). Accordingly, the CDR is appropriate for implementation by teachers without specialist computing knowledge, teaching development funds and/or teaching assistants. Such implementation could provide more opportunities for teachers and students.

CONCLUSION

This paper describes the design, development and evaluation of a learning dashboard, the CDR. This dashboard has an easy-to-use interface, and students and subject leaders were both satisfied with the design and features. Through an in-depth discussion of the needs of stakeholders, the challenges in development and the process of evaluation, this paper provides sufficient details for learning designers, teachers and AI researchers to explore future opportunities in their contexts and to contribute to the growing body of literature on learning dashboards. The study highlights the need for consultation with stakeholders, flexibility for unexpected use and more sophisticated visualization as well as the potential for easy implementation by teachers without technical knowledge.

This paper provides two important contributions to the field: (1) From a learning dashboard development perspective, the developed CDR is a low-cost solution (instead of a costly development project designed by a contractor), and course lecturers, without much support, can replicate the design and implement CDRs in their own courses; and (2) studies on learning dashboards in the language learning context are rare.
