Chapter 8

Theoretical Considerations and Practical Teaching Activities

  • Trinh Ngoc Thanh
    Ho Chi Minh City University of Technology and Education, Vietnam

ABSTRACT

This chapter draws attention to the implementation of corpus data in the development of learning materials for grammar teaching. The corpus-aided implementation is divided into the two main scopes of this chapter: theoretical considerations and practical teaching activities. The former scope of this chapter aims to review key theoretical concepts in grammar teaching, relevant teaching frameworks, and characteristics of corpus-aided pedagogy. Meanwhile, the latter scope of this chapter outlines practical teaching activities in corpus-aided grammar teaching in three particular stages: observation, generalization, and reproduction. Overall, corpus-aided grammar teaching can uncover patterns of language use in different varieties of English and develop the ability for grammatical interpretation through the analysis of language patterns and selected linguistic data.

INTRODUCTION

The relation between instruction and comprehension in grammar teaching was considered the key issue in the mapping of grammatical form and its underlying meanings (Batstone & Ellis, 2009). Recent debates related to the role of grammar instruction merely acknowledged the exposure to target language as the key factor in succeeding the attainment of grammatical and discourse competence yet emphasized a certain extent to which grammar teaching functions as the essential component in teaching practices (Mystkowska-Wiertelak & Pawlak, 2011). According to Nassaji and Fotos (2004), there are at least four claims in support of grammar teaching: these include the role of noticing or awareness on form (cf. Swain & Lapkin, 2001), the intervention of teaching to benefit the acquisition of grammatical knowledge (cf. Lightbown, 2000), the emphasis of accuracy in learning the target language (cf. Swain & Lapkin, 1989), and positive evidences of grammar instruction in classroom context.

Corpus linguistics was applied in language learning context via the means of corpus-aided pedagogy including the implementation of data-driven learning (Johns, 1991) and corpus-aided discovery learning (Huang, 2008). According to Johns (1991), data-driven learning commonly refers to a type of machine learning where the computer retrieves linguistic information from a large amount of text and acts an informant for the learners to facilitate their scanning of patterns and generalizations from the appearance of keyword-in-context concordance. The corpus-aided discovery learning further generates the attention to the linguistic form and knowledge through the steps of researching, discovering, and reflecting (Huang, 2008) and identifying, analyzing, and interpreting observations (Bernardini, 2000). Most importantly, at the core of grammar leaning, corpus-aided discovery learning constructs grammar description from the ground of authentic linguistic evidence and the learner engagement into the discovery learning task (Johns, 1991).

Considering the above theoretical overview on the implementation of corpus-aided pedagogy on grammar teaching on the particular aspect of connecting instruction and comprehension, three theoretical questions (TQ) for this paper are as follows:

  • 1. TQ1: What are key components and frameworks in grammar teaching?
  • 2. TQ2: In what way can corpus linguistics be integrated into grammar teaching?
  • 3. TQ3: How can corpora resources be adapted to teaching grammar following the sequence of stages?

Main sections are organized accordingly on the basis of three stated TQs. In response to TQ1, Section 1: Key components of grammar teaching firstly provides a review of key theoretical concepts in grammar teaching including comprehensible input, accuracy, fluency, and lexis. These selected four components are reviewed on the base of focus on forms/ focus on form distinction or the relationship between grammatical form and derived meaning in grammar teaching. Meanwhile, the review on theoretical frameworks of grammar teaching in Section 2: Grammar and its relevant teaching frameworks explains the mechanism of transformational generative grammar, systemic functional grammar, and lexicogrammatical approach. These three selected frameworks further elaborate the form-meaning interface into larger theoretical considerations for human cognition, pedagogy, and pragmatics.

Section 3: Corpus-aided grammar teaching responds to TQ2 by reviewing characteristics of corpus-aided pedagogy and its implementation in the context of grammar teaching. This section reviews the resources and elements which can be transferred from corpus linguistics to the practice of grammar teaching. From the review of how corpus linguistics can be integrated into teaching activities for grammar, Section 4: Practical teaching activities answers TQ3 by presenting two teaching cases studies of corpus-aided linguistic analysis along with follow-up practical teaching activities. On the chosen varieties of British and American English, the investigations of moment expressions and comparative lexes are chosen to demonstrate corpus-aided adaptation into the stages of observation, generalization, and reproduction.

SECTION 1: KEY COMPONENTS OF GRAMMAR TEACHING

This section provides a review of key components in grammar teaching. We will firstly review theoretical view of comprehensible input- the key element in Krashen’s Input Hypothesis. The follow-up review will be placed on definitions of accuracy, fluency, and lexis- three related concepts in the transformation of input in language acquisition.

Comprehensible Input

The notion of comprehensible input can be defined into two integrated components, including what sorts of element theoretically constitute input and what types of mechanism practically facilitate comprehension. According to Mystkowska-Wiertelak and Pawlak (2011), the constitution of input is often initially derived from theoretical perspective of non-interventionists as believers in the natural acquisition of the target language in the classroom: in particular, this acquisition is composed of the interrelated systems of lexis, syntax, phonology, and pragmatics and how these systems link language forms with language functions as well as language functions with language uses. Furthermore, the natural acquisition can be explained via the substantial framework of Krashen (1982)’s Monitor Model within which stating that language acquisition occurs when there is a natural pathway for the language information from the comprehensible input to interact with the existent language faculty of human being (Mystkowska-Wiertelak & Pawlak, 2011).

Among the proposed hypotheses from Monitor Model, the Input Hypothesis characterizes the notion of comprehensible input from the emphasis of understanding the message in the target language and thus language acquisition takes place when the level of input is beyond our current level of knowledge (Krashen & Terrell, 1983). In the representation of i+1 as comprehensible input, i signifies the current level of learner’s competence while +1 marks the next stage of development where learners go through the procedure of understanding the meaning rather than the form of the message in the target language-either in the spoken or written form (Krashen & Terrell, 1983; as cited in Lee, 2018).

However, the success in the transmission of an input to be comprehensible rather depends on some conditional factors. The foremost condition is the extent to which the input is finely tuned through the continuous flow of examples in the aim to promote the acquisition of the next structure at the specific stage of language development. The continuous flow of examples is illustrated as the container of unacquired grammar and the transmission of the next structure in this container would rather depend on the extra-linguistic context such as the assistance of visual aids and the reference to familiar topics (Krashen, 1985). The second condition is the role of internal language processor as the device to transform the acquisition of the provided input (Chomsky, 1975). Within this internal process, the device itself operates internal rules for the comprehension of the input and yet this operation is subject to an “affective filter”-an imaginary blockage which prevents the input to become comprehensible before being utilized for language acquisition (Krashen, 1985, p. 81).

Finally, in the matter between comprehension and skill development, comprehensible input is considered the result of two reverse directions. Following the Comprehension Hypothesis and Skill-Building Hypothesis (Krashen, 2017), the accumulation of comprehensible input is likely to facilitate skill development when the acquirers internally comprehend what they produce; meanwhile, comprehensible input is considered the result of skill development when the process of skill development initiates automaticity in spoken and written language. Related to the concern of comprehension and skill development is the extent to which a comprehensible input becomes an optimal one. According to Krashen and Mason (2020), the provision of comprehensible input is not sufficient for language acquisition because there is no guarantee for the provided input to be fully transmitted and the process of language acquisition does not require the comprehension of every part of the provided input. Furthermore, the optimal input is thought to be interesting enough for a temporary distraction instead and should be intense in terms of linguistic structure and learning opportunities so that the procedure of acquiring new aspects of language can be gradually processed (Krashen & Mason, 2020).

Accuracy, Fluency, and Lexis

Having mentioned that comprehensible input i+1 should be positioned from the current learner’s competence i into the attempt for acquiring language at the +1 level, there are certain conditions which may inhibit the flow of an input in its process of being comprehended by learners, including (a) the fine-tuning of the input with specific assistance, (b) the availability of internal language processor, and (c) the concern between comprehension and skill development. Along the way with reviewing three key terms including accuracy, fluency, and lexis, this section further explores the first two certain conditions (a) & (b) from the distinction between focus on forms and focus on form

Firstly, the concern of fine-tuning the input with specific assistance can be linked with the form and meaning interface in specific considerations of focus on forms/ focus on form. The focus on forms/focus on form refers to explicit teaching approaches which is respectively distinctive in the emphasis of grammatical forms (form with plural –s) and the emphasis on meaning-based derivation from the acquired structures (Ellis, 2006). Although there remained a concern that the connection between form and meaning may be inadequate toward the development of communicative competence, the attention to grammatical form in teaching instruction is also necessary for the acquisition of the target language in specific teaching context. Fotos (1998) stated that the necessity of explicit instruction on grammatical forms in EFL learning context as the EFL context is limited in opportunities for communicative usage in the real-world context and even in the language classroom. Therefore, the formal instruction on teaching grammatical forms should be modified in the communicative teaching framework so that the promotion of accuracy can be achieved through the interaction with the learning task design as well as the promotion of L2 learner’s understanding of the target form and L2 learner’s awareness to the correct from (Fotos, 1998).

While the promotion of accuracy is linked with the strand of focus on forms in the sense that it is the key focus in driving L2 learners into the grammatical structure and the avoidance of grammatical mistakes via corrective feedback (Ellis, 2006), the achievement of fluency connects the strand of focus on form with the notion of automaticity and the meaning-based orientation in language usage. In terms of associating with input, the notion of automaticity links fluency with the learning of formulaic sequence or sequence of lexis in a combination of words in larger structures) rather than single words (Gholami et al., 2017). According to Nation and Webb (2011), this sequence of lexis can be in the forms of collocations (e.g. make the bed; do homework), lexical bundles (e.g. as a result of ; on the other hand), and idioms (e.g. get out of hand ; pull someone’s leg). Although formulaic language considerably assists cognitive efforts in language processing and information retrieval in dual-system approach (Wray, 2002) and even provides an advantage in analyzing rule-based language (Conklin & Schmitt, 2008), there are certain challenges in the achievement of native-like fluency through the acquisition of formulaic sequence. In this case, the availability of internal language processor in the acquisition of formulaic sequence may be limited in its interaction with relevant linguistic aspects of the provided formulaic sequence (e.g. words, morphemes, letters, pronunciation) due to certain limitations. The causes of these limitations can be subject to the routine of rote-learning in language acquisition (Sinclair, 1991), the monitoring of attention to accuracy or fluency by different types of L2 learners (Krashen, 2003), and the procedure of acquitting lexical items in three stages of noticing, retrieving, and generating (Nation, 2001).

SECTION 2: GRAMMAR AND ITS RELEVANT TEACHING FRAMEWORKS

The previous section reviews the form-meaning interface in between the two strands of focus of forms and focus on form. In terms of teaching applications, the former can be instructed in the attention to grammatical forms and accuracy while the latter can be combined with the attention to meaning and fluency. In the connection between fluency and accuracy, the previous address to formulaic sequence raises particular concerns to two issues: first, whether the teaching of sequence of lexis is useful for the acquisition of the target form and second, whether the involvement of instruction in grammar teaching is necessary for the construction of language acquisition. This section further explores the stated two issues by reviewing the concept grammar and to be followed by the review of selected teaching frameworks: including generative grammar, systemic functional grammar, and lexicogrammatical approach.

In addition to lexis, grammar is also considered as a means to create language meaning and the general distinction between them is that while the former term is connected with the semantic aspect of a word and the resources of vocabulary, the latter one is in connection with the syntactic and structural aspects; and in a concrete arrangement, grammar associates an organizational pattern of which lexis fills individual items into the slots in the structured pattern (Sinclair, 2000). Larsen-Freeman (2014) proposed a three-dimensional grammar framework dividing grammar into the components of form (morphosyntactic), meaning (semantics), and use (pragmatics). The three-dimensional grammar framework shows the interrelation among these three components in how they interact with each other in the fit of grammar and communication and in the construction of an appropriate grammar teaching approach; and in comparison to the form-meaning interface in focus on forms/ focus on form, the addition of use in the three-dimensional grammar framework emphasizes the relationship between language and context in the encoding of structure (Levinson, 1983). According to Larsen-Freeman (2014), this framework expands the context into either the social context (e.g. social relationships among speakers), the discourse context (e.g. the linguistic context emerged from the relevant text or the genre and the register of the language), or the presuppositions (i.e. what is generally assumed about the context of conversation).

On the construction an appropriate grammar teaching approach for L2 learners, a foremost issue is given to the focus of which influences how instruction should be handled in grammar teaching. First, following the teaching framework of grammatical analysis, grammar teaching can be moved from analyzing eight categories for parts of speech to the learning of sub-systems (including phonology, morphology, and syntax) and the transition into short dialogues with specific language items and their functions along the way with 3P stages of presentation-practice-production (Hinkel & Fotos, 2002). Second, in an adaptation of contrastive analysis, grammar teaching can be classified into the perspectives of Chomsky’s transformational generative grammar and Halliday’s systemic functional grammar. Chomsky (1957) viewed that language is composed of innate structure inside the human brain and universal categories of word speech are identified in all languages; furthermore, as reviewed by Xiao (2017), another highlight from Chomsky (1957)’s proposal is that sentence is formed from the generative process of applying phrase structure rules (e.g. S→ NP+VP; VP→ V+NP; NP→Det+N) while the transformational part refers to the surface and deep structure of a sentence, given that the surface structure represents the performance of sentence (e.g. in phonological representation) and the deep structure is the underlying meaning and categories of the sentence (e.g. the semantic representation). The meaning side of sentence is on the other hand the key emphasis in Halliday (1973)’s systemic functional grammar, specifying language is the component of ideational, interpersonal, and textual functions. These stated functions respectively represent human experience about the world (i.e. the reality of the world or shared ideologies among communities), human interactivity with the text (i.e. the connection of speaker and writer with the text), and textual organization of the text (i.e. the construction of the text for the purpose of communication) (Halliday, 1985).

Third, on the assumption that the arrangement of words in one sentence encodes grammatical relations, it is also worth taking the teaching framework of grammatical interpretation in grammar teaching. Coming the consideration with pedagogical content knowledge and the interaction between language teacher cognition and grammar teaching context (Sanchez & Borg, 2014), the provision of grammar explanations should reflect the pedagogical and cognitive adaptation of grammatical knowledge toward the aim of transforming meaningful inputs into L2 learner’s cognition. To promote the process of interpretation in grammar teaching, there should be the creation of learning tasks which appropriately link the grammatical forms with the meanings indicated by the forms (Batstone & Ellis, 2009). Furthermore, the task of interpreting grammar can be seen the compromise between lexis and grammar in lexicogrammar approach on the understanding that the pattern language is inseparable in its meaning and structure (Sinclair, 2000).

On the premise that lexicogrammar approach is viewed as the unified system of lexis and grammar, grammatical interpretation should be studied in habit formation of textual environment including the construction of surrounding co-text (e.g. function words) and discourse functions (e.g. genres and registers) (Gledhill, 2011). This approach is particularly useful in the analysis of formulaic sequence (e.g. collocations; lexical bundles) using the knowledge of grammar and pragmatic rules and developing the interpretation task from corpora resources (Mystkowska-Wiertelak & Pawlak, 2011). Drawn on this point, the next section will review how the usage of corpora can assist grammar teaching following the teaching implications of corpus-aided grammar teaching.

SECTION 3: CORPUS-AIDED GRAMMAR TEACHING

The usage of corpus data in the context of English language teaching is subject to its usefulness in providing an access to authentic language data because it is argued that the language in use is not the same as rule-specified grammar and varied in terms of forms and degree of formality (Huang, 2008). In application of language teaching and learning materials development, corpus linguistics can be applied in the compilation of dictionaries in either the printed form (e.g. Collins COBUILD Advanced Learners’ English Dictionary; Macmillian English Dictionary; Oxford Advanced Learners’ English Dictionary) or online search (e.g. The Corpus of Contemporary American English; British National Corpus; Cambridge Dictionaries Online). Apart from dictionaries, corpus linguistics is also implemented in data-driven learning as the means for enhancing the “interpretation of form” and “realization of function” via the creation of grammar exercises and grammar learning activities from corpus data (Johns, 1990, p. 293). These grammar exercises and learning activities are distinct in the nature of consciousness-raising (Meunier, 2002) and the purpose of which is to facilitate learner’s discovery into the regular patterning of the target language in an inductive manner (Johns & King, 1991).

Our next review is given to the concern on what kinds of empirical evidence can be drawn on corpus resources to create grammar exercises and grammar learning activities. First, language teachers can utilize corpus concordance of certain lexical items (i.e. text lines with the search word in the middle and the surrounding context) as the linguistic evidence for learners to observe the usage of grammar and vocabulary and to be followed by the next stage to generalize the patterns and rules associated with the observed search word (Liu & Jiang, 2009). Observations of concordance can be conducted inductively by directing learners’ attention to on-screen computers or printed handouts and examining how lexical items are used in specific context (Nesi & Basturkmen, 2009). Second, word frequency list can be employed as the means for learners to observe the ranking of certain lexical items and to make comparisons of these items across selected language varieties and language contexts (O'Keeffe & Farr, 2003). Normally, the word frequency list is extracted in the high frequency word list starting with the minimum frequency of ten and high frequency lexical items on the list may reflect characteristics of the specialized corpora in terms of usage and emphasis (Warren, 2009).

Another consideration for corpus-aided grammar teaching is placed on languaging, the emphasis of which is how to mediate cognitive processes in the process of meaning-making to develop knowledge and experience through the usage of language (Swain, 2006). In terms of application in corpus-aided grammar teaching, languaging can be combined with the following stages in corpus-aided investigation: (1) observing the recurrences of language patterns for the chosen lexis, (2) generalizing the explanations on the identified language patterns via comparison methodology, and (3) reproducing conventional or producing creative texts (Gavioli, 2001). This concept can also be integrated in the case of contextulized grammar teaching where the teaching of grammar is among the combination of lexicogrammar approach, the implementation of corpus, and contextualization (Liu & Jiang, 2009). Contextualization from corpus data is beneficial for grammar teaching in the sense that it provides relevant context transferred from the text for learners to interpret grammar as being compared with paper-based learning materials (Charles, 2007). In particular, the embedded information from contextualization can be discourse functions, genre, speech events, and specialized language, all of which should construct relevant linguistic environment in the grammatical constructions (Perez-Llantada, 2009).

The next concern in this section is given to how teachers can implement corpus linguistics in a grammar lesson. According to McEnery et al. (2006), corpus linguistics can be viewed as “a new philosophical approach to linguistic enquiry” (p. 6) and this linguistic enquiry can be firstly conducted in the transition from bottom-up to top-down processing (Flowerdew, 2009). The search of specific lexical items in a bottom-up processing would result in the analysis and classification of specific patterns to the search items while the top-down processing would rather be handled in the analysis of how genre-specific requirements influence the emergence of frequent patterns and structures. For instance, Flowerdew (2008) illustrated how the top-down selection between passive and active voice in the lexical item focus comes from the bottom-up search of focus and its relevant pattern. The linguistic enquiry from corpus data is also designed in grammar exercises which include observation/research task and freer creative writing activities. In Johns (1990)’s article, the author illustrated the design of grammar exercises on the observation/ research task of that-clause from which learners identify the associated verbs with that-clause from concordance lines of New Scientist weekly journal-the extracted corpus, subsequently compare the two groups of cited quotations of that-clause, and finally complete the sentences with that-clause in their own creativity. The third way to conduct the linguistic inquiry is the extraction of the World Wide Web as the source of corpus data and performance of online search from there. Boulton (2012) reviewed Google as an exemplary case of concordance with which learners can extract results of linguistic data of their own needs; nevertheless, the act of Googling should consider relevant issues including the training of getting and evaluating the results and not to mention the issue of how reliable and frequent the linguistic data withdrawn from this search engine.

SECTION 4: PRACTICAL TEACHING ACTIVITIES

This section suggests teaching activities implementing corpus-aided grammar teaching as the teaching methodology. Following previous discussion on languaging, an attempt is made to introduction teaching activities on the aspect of grammatical interpretation in the following procedure: observation, generalization, and reproduction. On the chosen varieties of British and American English, the first teaching case demonstrates an exploration of the length of an action i moment expressions from the following formulaic sequences: V+ momentarily, V+ for a moment, and V+ in a moment. On a similar focus on the chosen varieties of English, the second teaching case further incorporate the function of Googling in the design of teaching activities for the comparative tasks on the following lexis: (1) coffee house-coffee shop and (2) discount-concession. Note that the abbreviation of T and Ss will respectively stand for Teacher and Students in this section.

Teaching Case 1: Moment Expressions

Data Collection

Data for this teaching case was collected from available corpora: Bank of English (sub-corpus: british and american), Corpus of Contemporary American English (COCA), and British National Corpus (BNC). I also collected data from other online resources such as Google.com (Region: United States), Google.co.uk (Pages from UK), and online dictionaries: Oxford, Longman, etc. Table 1 gives further details about the resources for moment expressions from two varieties of English:

Table 1- The available corpora for moment expression

Expressions Resources
momentarily
for a moment
in a moment		 Bank of English (sub-corpus: british & american)
Online websites
Online dictionary
COCA
BNC

Data Analysis

WordSmith 6.0 was chosen as the tool to analyze text data extracted from the corpora. The following features are of primary focus when using WordSmith 6.0: the first five words on the left (L5 to L1) and the right (R5 to R1) of the node (i.e. key word of investigation), and the cluster (i.e. the patterns of the node). Structural analysis for the text data was also conducted to explain from a lexicogrammatical approach how arrangement of words in one sentence encoded its grammatical relations. Findings from both WordSmith and structural analysis aimed to quantify the length of an action in British and American “moment” expressions: V+ momentarily, V+ for a moment, and V+ in a moment.

Teaching Procedure

On the concern of languaging, this section presents the teaching procedure of moment expressions in three phases: observation, generalization, and reproduction (Gavioli, 2001). In particular, students initially draw on what they have already known to perceive form/meaning connections; and subsequently, the teacher develop instructional activities to raise learner consciousness of the new constructed form/meaning connections. A summary of key observations will be synthesized after the completion of three stated phases.

Observation

Teacher (T) designs warm up activities to introduce to students (Ss) moment expressions: momentarily, for a moment, and in a moment. T can show some examples of the following structures V+ momentarily, V+ for a moment, and V+ in a moment from the two varieties British and American English and have Ss predict the length of the action indicated by the main verbs.

After that, T shows to Ss the list of verbs which momentarily functions as pre-modifier and draws Ss attention to comparing the frequency of verbs associated with momentarily. Following after the demonstration of L1 and R1 positions of the node momentarily, Ss discuss the significance of how momentarily is used in sentence structure. Across the two varieties of English, the main verbs following momentarily are in passive voice and past tense. It is also noted the passive form of the verb distract in past tense was a significant pattern which is associated with momentarily in British English.

Figure 1. Patterns of momentarily from Bank of English (left) and Word Smith 6.0 (right)

Generalization

The interpretation task is drawn from the significant cluster was momentarily distracted from the patterns of momentarily (Figure 1). T performs the search for examples on the selected formulaic sequence of was momentarily distracted. From these interpretations, Ss will quantify the length of action in the equivalent formulaic sequence of in a moment and for a moment.

The following examples show in a moment as a synonymous expression for momentarily in American English to because there is no delay between two actions. Meanwhile, as being identified with a delay between two subsequent actions, for a moment is considered a synonym of momentarily in British English.

(american): “A 17th century drawing by Rembrandt was snatched from a private art display at a California luxury hotel while a curator was momentarily distracted, officials said Monday” (Dawn, 2011)

(british):“ The peasant quietly came in and sat down in front of the money. When the assistant was momentarily distracted by a fit of coughing he took out his single rupee and put it on the counter opposite the mass of rupees. Then he waited for his money to attract money” (Leggett, 2000)

Reproduction

Activity 1: Defining the length of the action

Using the following quotation, T raises student awareness to 3 synonymous expressions: momentarily, for a moment, and in a moment.

An English friend of mine says that he nearly had a heart attack on a flight in the United States when the American pilot announced that the plane would be airborne “momentarily.' In British English, the language my friend speaks, “momentarily” means “for a moment,” and he thought the pilot was suggesting an imminent crash soon after takeoff. In American English, however, “momentarily” means “in a moment,” and the pilot was merely appeasing the impatient passengers (Tharoor, 2003)

Alternatively, T can ask Ss to interpret the length of the action be in the air in the two sentences. Suggested answers are in the brackets:

  • ● The plane would be in the air for a moment. (British English, short action)
  • ● The plane would be in the air in a moment. (American English, long action)
    • Activity 2: Checking dictionary

T can have Ss check online or paper dictionaries of British and American English (e.g. Oxford, Cambridge, Macmillian, Longman dictionaries, etc.) of the moment expressions: momentarily, for a moment, and in a moment. Ss interpret the definitions and examples from these resources to quantify the length of action indicated by the main verbs. Depending on the level of Ss, T can set the task of interpreting examples from other online resources such as Google search and online newspapers for Ss to explore the aspect of length of the action in these three expressions in British and American English.

Further Suggested Application

This activity shows how corpus data can be used to explore the emphasis of adverbial phrases in certain time frame. Apart from the focus of moment expression on measuring length of time for the collocated actions in verb, there are some other topics worth investigating such as the emphasis on adverbs of frequency (e.g. often, frequently, usually, occasionally) on verb collocation in the time frame of present simple. On the perfectual aspect of verb tense, T can collect data of adverbs indicating the perfect tense and guide Ss into an analysis of the aspect of recently completed action or the continuous aspect of action from past until the present. Meanwhile, for advanced Ss, the time frame of future can be analyzed on a variety of adverb usages and verb tenses (present simple, present simple continuous, be going to, and future simple).

Teaching Case 2: Comparative Lexes

Place to Buy Coffee

Data Collection

The teaching case 2.1 compares coffee shop and coffee house in British and American English. The corpus data for designing teaching activities is extracted from Bank of English (sub-corpus: british and american) and further linguistic data for exploration is collected from Google search.

Data Analysis

Similar to teaching case 1, teaching case 2.1 also implements Word Smith 6.0 and patterns of nodes (i.e. five on the left side and the other five on the right side of the key word) on the design of relevant teaching activities. Data interpretation would be drawn on the co-occurrence of patterns of words to analyze this distinction between two items.

Teaching Procedure

Teaching case 2.1 compares coffee house and coffee shop in two varieties of English. The design of grammar teaching activities on the case of coffee shop and coffee house therefore demonstrates the case where teachers can implement the analysis of word pattern from the position of nodes and the relevant descriptive information from daily usage. Teaching activities follow similar procedure on three stages of observation, generalization, and reproduction.

Observation

The purpose of the observation task is to help Ss recognize the regular existence of the two lexes: coffee house and coffee shop in British and American English. T firstly presents to Ss the extracted patterns of nodes for coffee shop and coffee house from Bank of English (sub-corpus: british and american). T can raise Ss awareness by raising their attention to the position of L1 and develop tentative analysis of how the two items are used in two language contexts.

The extracted patterns of nodes on the position of L1 demonstrate a tendency of coffee shop and coffee house being commonly referents in the United States. The non-existence of coffee shop at the center position and the feature of articles (a/the) in L1 position generates the hypothesis that drinking coffee is rather less common in the United Kingdom.

Figure 2. Patterns of coffee house and coffee shop in British and American English

Generalization

In the generalization stage, Ss perform Google search for online written articles on the topic of coffee shop and coffee house. Depending on the searched results, they generate classification of their findings on the basis of the following suggested criteria:

  • 1. Size of the place:
  • 2. Local naming:
  • 3. Regular customers:
  • 4. Interior design:

Ss share their findings with other Ss and T will assist Ss to determine which suggested criteria is likely the contributor to the usage of coffee shop and coffee house in real-life context.

Reproduction

T can conduct another comparative task of how representation of similar concept of where to buy things is varied across British and American varieties of English. Suggested comparative items include the representation of a place for car-fixing in auto body shop (American English) and repair shop (British English) and the representation of a place for buying daily-life products in grocery store (American English) and corner shop (British English).

Further Suggested Application

The comparison of lexes on place to buy coffee shows how corpus linguistics can be implemented in investigating cultural annotation of language usage across varieties of English. The pattern of language identified in a specific word context denotes the implication of how language usage reveals the reality of object in daily life and its equivalence in another word context. Further similar teaching plans using corpus data can be directed at the investigation of food and animal culture in literary works. Other topics on gender and societal differences on language usage can be directed in combination with text analysis of extracted excerpts and dialogues.

Discount and Concession

Data Collection

Teaching case 2.2 distinguishes concession and discount as the referent to the amount of reduced money from the original price. The collection for corpus data is stated in Table 2:

Table 2. The available corpora for discount and concession

Expressions Resources
discount
concession		 Bank of English (sub-corpus: british & american)
Online dictionary
COCA
BNC
Google

Data Analysis

WordSmith 6.0 and Google search are implemented for data analysis. The purpose is to combine both languaging and googling into the interpretation of language use in sociolinguistic context. In other words, grammatical analysis is expanded into the connection with demographic data and social policy in certain contexts.

Teaching Procedure

Observation

The observation stage of grammar teaching can be started with observation of frequent combination of concession and discount in British and American English from corpus data of Bank of English. Ss analyze which part of speech functions as the pre-modifier for the head nouns concession and discount in the british and american sub-corpus. Teaching activities can be further combined with dictionary definitions for explanations of the analyzed patterns.

Figure 3. Patterns of concession and discount in Britsh and American English

The extracted patterns of concession and discount in Figure 3 firstly show in American English a major pattern of adjectives being pre-modifiers for the head noun discount. The group of degree adjectives (deep, great, large, substantial) can be seen an addition of information about the degree of “reduction in the usual price of something” (Longman Dictionary of Contemporary English, n.d.). Meanwhile, concession seems to connect with the pre-modifiers embedded with the semantic value of human (adult, student) and this pattern also corresponds to the definition of “a reduction in the usual price of something, made available to students, old people, etc.” (Cambridge Dictionary, n.d.).

Generalization

Grammar teaching activities in the generalization stage can be developed with the probability of 2x2 word combination of four items: student, senior, discount, concession. T can draw a statistics of hits and search results from the selected corpora resources and Google Search Engine and assists Ss in interpreting these statistics with reference to their background and prior knowledge in other fields of study (e.g. economics, social studies, geography, history).

Figure 4. The 2x2 combination of student, senior, discount, concession

Statistics from Figure 4 predicts that the student group seem to attain the priority in United Kingdom while the senior citizen group seem to attain the priority in United States from 1993 to 2007. This interpretation can be further linked with demographic settings in these countries within or beyond this period of time.

Reproduction

Grammar teaching activities in reproduction stage are expected to combine further consolidation of previous identified findings and patterns with an intensive expansion of knowledge from interdisciplinary fields. T can link relevant knowledge from social policy to facilitate discussion about the impact of social structure of population and welfare issues on the emergence of linguistic patterns. The suggested topics include, for instance, education policy in the United Kingdom and national policy for senior citizen in the United States. The reading materials can be electronically retrieved from designated offices such as Department of Education and Office of Retirement and Disability Policy.

Further Suggested Application

Teaching case 3 demonstrates how sociolinguistics is an interdisciplinary field with corpus linguistics in distinguishing related meanings of synonyms and integrating with demographic data to explain the emergence of linguistic patterns from the collected corpus data. The investigation of synonyms can be conducted in comparative studies in different fields of study such as economics, education, and politics. A further aspect from this teaching case is the expansion of corpus data from specific group of speakers and combines the interpretation with demographic details of speakers in visualizing the significance of the emergent linguistic pattern.

CONCLUSION

The purpose of this chapter is to introduce how corpus linguistics can be integrated in the assistance of grammar teaching. The reviews of theoretical concepts, teaching frameworks, and corpus-aided pedagogy serve as the background for the outline of practical teaching activities using corpora resources as an adaptation into the cycle of observation, generalization, and reproduction stages in grammar teaching. This chapter considerably contributes to the field of corpus-aided pedagogy practical suggestions of how to teach grammar in the direction of corpus-aided discovery learning, and thus it mainly aims to bridge learner engagement into the sources of linguistic insight (Huang, 2008). Another contribution is the demonstration of how to enable learners to use grammar with accuracy, meaningfulness, and appropriateness (Larsen-Freeman, 2014). Finally, it is the consideration into teacher’s interaction for grammar learning, considering the increase of outputs when learners interact with the learning materials and the refinement of linguistic knowledge through means of hypothesis testing (Hinkel & Fotos, 2002).

There are some new dimensions worth considering in mediating the link between corpus linguistics and grammar teaching. First, it is the transition from the acquisition of grammatical knowledge into the acquisition of language skills and how corpus-aided instruction can further assist the transition from knowledge to skill. For instance, the teaching of writing skill can be adapted with the assistance of corpora resources for prior teaching of formulaic sequences toward the improvement of writing skill (Park, 2019). Within the teaching of writing skill, the particular usage of learner corpora is also useful for teachers to develop corpus-related research to diagnose learner’s difficulties in language learning and to provide suitable corrective feedback (Ying, 2018). Second, the diversity of learner’s profile is another dimension to be kept in mind before implementing corpus-aided instruction. Although corpus-aided instruction is reported to be effective in reading capacity through the teaching vocabulary in key word lists and concordances (Xu et al., 2019), teaching activities with corpora resources may be challenging for lower proficiency learners and thus more attention should be given to collaborative learning activities and the design of comprehensible materials for this particular group of learners (Hadley and Charles, 2017). Finally, teacher’s perception toward the usefulness of corpus-aided instruction is also of primary concern, considering that there are possible obstacles such as technical difficulties, time-consuming preparation, and their lack of confidence in data interpretation (Farr, 2008) as well as whether the corpus-aided instruction is likely to match the particular teaching contexts and learning outcomes (Poole, 2020).

Section 3

Chapter 9

21st Century Skills After COVID

  • Nurefsan Guneri
    University of Missouri-Kansas City, USA
  • Yusuf Ozgel
    Houston ISD, USA
  • Yetkin Yildirim
    Rice University, USA
  • Faruk Taban
    North American University, USA

ABSTRACT

In a rapidly changing world, especially amidst COVID-19, the educational goals must be refined and modified. This chapter derives from a study based on research findings and recognizes the individual aspects of 21st century skills that can pave the way for adaptation and transition utilizing applied skills and digital literacy. The 21st century skills (21CS) have also been in the spotlight about digital literacy, as the pandemic possessed the current need to continue instruction and learning despite the setback imposed. It was concluded that 21CS covers the requirements and works best when implemented into the current mode of instruction combined with STEM education and basic skills.

INTRODUCTION

Prior to the recent shift to online learning, educators understood the important role that 21st century skills played in providing students with a well-rounded education. These skills have typically been framed as the social and emotional tools that allow students to think critically about new problems, communicate effectively with others, and motivate themselves to work independently. A survey of employers conducted prior to the pandemic found that the skills most valued in job applicants were these soft skills such as communication, writing, speaking, and self-directedness (World Economic Forum, 2016). For K-12 students especially, developing these skills is a crucial part of education, at least as important as any particular course content. This is especially true in STEM fields, where the rapid pace of technological change makes it almost impossible to predict what specific problems students will face in their professional work. This has led to increased interest in project-based learning or group-led learning, which are better at preparing STEM students to be flexible problem solvers, equipped to face whatever challenges may await them in the modern workplace.

But the disruptions brought about by COVID-19 have made such 21st century skills even more crucial for students, who are now tasked with navigating their own learning in the midst of unforeseen disruptions and emotional stress. With the increased autonomy of online learning, students faced new challenges to stay motivated and engaged in their classwork. A student’s involvement in social networks has been shown to have a major impact on their academic success, and the increase in social isolation has adverse effects on student well-being and performance (Stadtfeld et al., 2019). And the new stressors associated with COVID — worries about the safety of family members, economic collapse, uncertain future of jobs and even education itself — only compound the problems of isolation. A study from Switzerland, conducted in the early weeks of the pandemic, found that students were on average more depressed, more anxious, more stressed, and more lonely than they were before lockdown (Elmer et al., 2020). While not surprising, these findings do emphasize the degree of stress that students are under and help explain why academic performance has faltered amid the shift to online learning.

A study by the Brookings Institute compared student’s academic achievement and growth during COVID to their academic achievement and growth from 2019 (Kuhfeld et al., 2020). While they noticed a decline in learning across the board, they found that math and other STEM subjects were the hardest hit — students were further behind in these classes than any others. The researchers speculated that subjects like reading or language arts were easier for parents to teach at home, while the more specialized STEM subjects suffered more from the lack of access to teachers and experts (Kuhfeld et al., 2020). It could also be the case that, in the transition to online learning, STEM education had to give up many of the pedagogical gains it made towards a more engaging, project-based curriculum and revert back to a lecture-based model that is more likely to leave students behind. Research has long suggested that STEM instruction is greatly improved by utilizing more real-life situation applications, promoting cooperation and group work, and establishing an increase in hands-on activities (Myers & Fouts, 1992). Unfortunately, these are the very things that online learning makes more difficult.

Undoubtedly, STEM students are in need of help. They are currently facing unprecedented social and emotional challenges, while at the same time the uncertainties of online and hybrid education have made it harder to deliver these students the 21st century skills that could help them cope. Many educators are barely treading water themselves, and between the difficulties of navigating new learning platforms and improvising new models for education, they have in some cases neglected to attend to the “soft” skills that are so important for student growth. Also, pedagogies that prioritize creativity, critical thinking, and collaboration may simply be harder to execute in online and hybrid class environments. 21st century skills have become more difficult to teach just when students need them most.

The goal of this chapter is to raise awareness of the continued need for 21st century skills in K-12 STEM education and propose a few practical methodologies for incorporating them into a STEM classroom, even when that classroom must exist online. If nothing else, these skills should be considered essential components of a COVID-era education because they teach students how to adapt to unfamiliar problems and communicate difficult emotions effectively (Jerald, 2009). By incorporating more discussion of social and emotional skills into their curriculum, as well as prioritizing collaborative communication in online projects, educators can show their students how to shift between different modes of learning more fluidly and can help them mitigate feelings of isolation. Beyond that, these lessons in communication, information literacy, and adaptability are still the best way to prepare students for a world that can change in an instant, as COVID-19 has so dramatically demonstrated.

WHAT ARE 21ST CENTURY SKILLS?

Ultimately, the appeal of primary and secondary education comes from its ability to prepare students for adult life and the workforce. A study by Kaufman (2019) established that high school principals predominantly believed that the role of high school education was college readiness, preparedness for the workforce, the military, and embracing a socially active life. But the same study found that many in the workforce consider incoming high school and college students to be grossly underprepared for the demands of a modern workplace (Kaufman, 2019). The consensus among most business leaders is that what is lacking are the so called “21st century skills,” which include the critical thinking, teamwork, collaboration, and communication skills unequivocally important for potential employees.

Many different researchers have articulated their own lists of essential 21st century skills, but broadly speaking the term refers to a set of competencies that allow students to navigate a rapidly changing world. These are the skills that go beyond simple course content, including such abstract processes as problem-solving, self-direction, collaboration, and adaptability. They do not entail mastery in a specific field, but rather a familiarity with a wide range of skills that are useful in almost any career or path one may choose. These skills have been the source of much discussion in education research; a comprehensive review by traced the evolving use of the term through 40 research papers (Chalkiadaki, 2018). The author concludes that 21st century skills can be generalized into four basic categories: personal skills, social skills, knowledge and information management skills, and digital literacy.

Personal skills refer to the development of a student’s individual characteristics. Of these, creativity was found to the most commonly cited 21st century skill, valued highly by both educators and employers (Chalkiadaki, 2018). In this context, creativity means the ability to innovate in a workplace as well as a sense of curiosity and imagination. Problem-solving was deemed another essential personal skill. At a time when knowledge is widely accessible, making the ability to apply that information more valuable. Self-directedness or autonomy is also considered extremely valuable, such as having the ability to self-regulate and develop. Other Personal skills such as sound reasoning, inquiry, and the ability to make informed decisions were also found to be essential.

The next category of essential skills has to do with a student’s one’s ability to communicate and get along with others. These social skills include skilled and effective oral and written communication, conflict management, global awareness, and an openness to diversity. Collaboration is also deemed as equally important. A good combination of these skills leads to less conflict and misunderstandings, and more productivity in a workplace or in one’s life. Many colleges actively look for students with strong leadership and cooperation skills in their applications (Sahin, 2014). Knowledge of a second language is also important given the increasingly globalized and heterogeneous nature of the workforce (Chalkiadaki, 2018).

Knowledge and information management skills are associated with one’s ability to absorb and retain information. In this age, learning is often based on technology and the internet, coined as e-learning (Balcaen, 2008). Students must be well versed in developing their skills in being able to absorb and manage information, especially from technology, as it becomes more common.

Digital literacy is a student’s adeptness with technology and is one of the most prominent and important 21st-century skills. A student’s ability to utilize technologies has become increasingly important as technology and the world’s reliance on it evolves. The digital age has brought a new tool that aspiring students must be confident in. Digital literacy involves a series of subskills that all have to do with one’s ability to effectively utilize media, the internet, and other technologies.

Research has found that 21st century skills are easier to teach at younger ages (Chalkiadaki, 2018). Kids are more malleable and have an easier time learning 21st century skills, provided that they are in a supportive environment. On the other hand, adults already in the workforce have a much more difficult time acquiring these skills and habits. Moreover, businesses are less inclined to actively teach adults a non-specialized skill set in a competitive economy, especially when someone with those skills can be readily hired instead. Because of this, K-12 education is the best time to teach 21st century skills. Ultimately, prioritizing the instruction of 21st century skills will create a stronger and more adaptable generation of children who are more prepared for life after high school.

In a globally competitive environment, a versatile and adaptable skillset is the most valuable resource. In the midst of COVID-19, K-12 STEM educators must feel a new urgency to help students gain these new competencies and 21st century skills. The collaboration, adaptability, and critical thinking that these skills foster may be especially useful to students in these challenging and uncertain times. They can help students develop resilient responses to the COVID-19 pandemic and help prepare them for future challenges (English & Kitsantas, 2013).

DEVELOPING THESE SKILLS IN A CLASSROOM

For decades, there have been calls for a “new model” of education that would treat these skills as equal to traditional content in importance and work to prepare students for a competitive global marketplace (Bellanca, 2010). This new model imagines more self-directed, project-based learning at the center of education. It was to be more “personalized,” giving students meaningful challenges that were well-matched to their interests and skill levels. This was meant to give students more choice, self-determination, and self-motivation. Scaffolded support would look different than the older “lecture model,” and students would have new opportunities for feedback and evaluation from their peers, teachers, and experts in the field.

For example, students at Chicago High School who have experienced this more project-based, personalized approach to education have developed a sense of responsibility and argue that school feels as though it's their second home (George Washington University, n.d.). Comfortable interaction, open discussions, and being on site for their desired career paths have made this possible. For students that are interested in agriculture, the school provides areas essential to learning how to grow plants and the students also get to dissect different types of plans to further investigate. For students who are interested in cooking and culinary arts, the school has a kitchen where the students can cook by step along with their peers with their own produce they have grown at their school. For other students that are interested in other subjects such as environmental sciences or biology, and even veterinary care, there are animals such as horses and goats that the students can look after and have responsibilities to carry out during a given time period. Some students claimed that they did not mind commuting to school for longer periods of time because they felt that it is worthwhile to put in work, see the results and share their respective experiences with their peers. They claimed to cherish the school and the opportunities being provided to them. Both the students and teachers are motivated and ready for real life applications when they get to college.

The school provides them with essential courses in addition to courses that they choose as possible electives (George Washington University, n.d.). By putting them on track, they are able to help students finish one year of college during high school, which makes their transition to college much easier. Most of these students were provided with many different opportunities including internships and getting to shadow the professionals that they desire to become in the future. This way, they build connections ahead of time. They are not only motivated and inspired, but they are also confident and comfortable going into the career pathway of their preference. Some of the students have the opportunity to witness what a day might look like in their chosen career path by partaking in classes that have almost the same, if not very similar conditions that they would have in the actual jobs. An example of this was when one of the students claimed that he wanted to pursue the field of mechanical engineering. He was able to experience various options in this field and autonomously performed well. He was measuring, cutting, and building using machines that mechanical engineers would use. This not only provides him with experience but it also builds his sense of confidence towards his desired career.

Through first-hand experience that mimics the actual experiences in real life, students are able to fully transition smoothly in addition to working with ease despite having to be independent in most of these professions. This is because they have various hours of research, interaction, collaboration, and experiences by the time they graduate high school. This also opens new doors for them in terms of scholarships and internships because colleges recognize students that have put in extra effort and dedication towards their desired path. By the time they attend college, instead of being undecided about their major or being unsure about success in their major, they can be confident about the choices they make ahead of time. They can delve deeper into the profession area that they want to pursue, and specialize in different parts for the profession instead of having an ambiguous idea about what they want to pursue, or struggle while pursing that path (George Washington University, n.d.).

However, these new models were typically designed around traditional, in-person classrooms, as online learning had not yet been made ubiquitous by the COVID-19 pandemic. Do they still serve as useful guides for student learning now that education has shifted online? What can be adapted from them to help students acquire these crucial 21st century skills today?

PROJECT-BASED LEARNING

According to the available research, students learn best in experiential, collaborative environments (Blinkoff, 2021). Assessment-based teaching that abstracts knowledge away from practical use doesn’t lead to long-term learning for most students. Furthermore, such theoretical approaches do not always promote college readiness. Students need applied skills, not just content-specific skills. And the best way to provide them these skills is to allow them to work collaboratively in heterogeneous environments (Paulus, 2018). As 21st century educators, it is essential move beyond a lecture paradigm to allow kids to teach themselves and teachers to be facilitators of this process (Bellanca, 2010). Many researchers have promoted this type of “active” learning, which entails iterative, interactive, engaging and meaningful exercises performed on the daily routine, and praised it for its ability to foster creativity and open-mindedness in students (Blinkoff, 2021). Because active learning requires the communication and collaboration of involved students, they have to actively pay attention and cooperate and bring their own ideas and solutions to the table for different settings.

Project-based learning is perhaps the most common form of active learning found in K-12 curricula. This type of instruction confronts students with a real-world problem that requires them to learn concepts, apply information, and collaborate with others in order to solve it (Marx et al., 1994). Project-based learning makes students responsible for their learning, as they “actively participate in the processes of constructing knowledge and making meaning” (Mergendoller et al., 2006). This fosters valuable 21st century skills, such as self-direction and self-regulation. Students are taught how to “set goals, plan a course of action, select appropriate strategies, self-monitor, and self-evaluate their learning” (English & Kitsantas, 2013).

Also referred to as “self-regulated learning,” this autonomy is an essential feature of project based learning, and can be nurtured by deliberate curricular planning. Some strategies include presenting students with the overall questions and the goals of the project, so that they frame the problem in terms of what they already know instead of what they must learn. This is the first phase. Next, as students start to seek possible explanations for their leading questions, they must use self-control as well as self-observation. In the third phase of the project, students come to a conclusion and make self-reflections and presentations. This allows the students to make connections between initial goals that they wanted to achieve versus the conclusions or results they acquired in the end. When students are effectively guided through these three phases, they are able to develop the autonomy and self-regulation to take on and even lead similar projects in the future. The benefits of project-based learning in teaching students how to be autonomous, self-motivated thinkers are clear. Research has shown that this kind of self-regulation in students is strongly correlated with academic success (Zimmerman, 2008; Zimmerman & Kitsantas, 1999).

Another related type of active learning is the rotational station model, in which students move in groups through the different stages of a project, getting to experience a number of different types of challenges. Moving from one group to another, the students may have the chance to write code on a tablet, for example, then move to another station where they must read a passage and find the main idea, then to another where they might use 3D models to present the findings of their research. This develops motor skills, social skills and intellectual abilities all at the same time. Students don’t just sit back and listen to an instructor lecturing; they become familiar with the topic at hand through multiple different learning modalities. Not only do the students speak with each other, they draw, write, explain, present and build their creations and take pride in the work they do which nourishes their hunger for learning and discovering the world through individual scopes of interest and hobbies. Once that association of excitement and eagerness to learn ignites within the student, it triggers a sense of autonomy and responsibility, and learning is no longer forced (Brandt & Thompson, 2020).

A systematic review of 21st Century Skills competencies supports the idea that project-based and rotational station learning methods teach collaboration as well as autonomy (Chalkiadaki, 2018). This is important, because although collaboration is often vaunted as an essential 21st century skill, it is not easy to teach. Good collaboration depends on a variety of complex social skills, including leadership, decision-making, trust-building, communication, and conflict-management (Carpenter & Pease, 2013). This is what makes project-based learning so effective: it allows educators to foster these skills indirectly, by designing experiences that support their growth. As students work together to solve problems, they learn how to work in community, give and receive criticism, and learn to how to navigate a diversity of approaches and opinions.

Project-based learning also teaches students to focus on process outcomes (English & Kitsantas, 2013). Although schools generally teach students about right or wrong answers in school, there are very few occasions in real-world situations where that is the case. in real world situations multiple paths can be taken towards a desired goal or have different answers to a problem that may not be concretely considered right or wrong. This is why teaching students to focus on the process of coming to a solution rather than the solution itself is so valuable. This is a much more transferrable skill for 21st century application, and project-based learning is ideal for emphasizing this type of student awareness. In the different phases of their projects, students benefit from peer reflection and evaluation, and they become more cognizant of the means by which they came to their final result. This ultimately makes them more proficient as a part of a team. Throughout all the phases of project-based learning, students enter into cyclical feedback loops of reflection and planning, which are essential skills with real-world application.

PROJECT-BASED LEARNING ONLINE

Clearly project-based learning is an excellent way to give students the 21st century skills that they need in the classroom, but do the secondary benefits of project-based learning still apply when instruction shifts online? There isn’t much research available yet, but early case studies suggest that it is possible to effectively adapt project-based learning to virtual environments (Miller et al., 2021). Highly motivated and experienced teachers have been able to adapt their project-based curriculum online, and they’ve viewed these projects as an essential to maintaining social connections during remote learning (Miller et al., 2021). Another study found that online project-based learning still results in a positive impact on student motivation, engagement, and self-directedness, but students themselves don’t consider online projects as valuable as in-person projects (Abuhmaid, 2020). The results of this perception on their academic performance are unclear, but educators should note that the difficulties of communicating online via social media created new barriers that made groupwork among student more difficult and frustrating. It may be that the benefits of project-based learning, while still present, are heavily mitigated by the isolation and lack of motivation inherent in online learning. So, in order to provide the kind of 21st century education that students need, online educators may need to supplement a project-based curriculum with other activities designed to promote soft skills.

TEACHING SELF-DIRECTEDNESS ONLINE

Prior to the pandemic, studies found that students utilized self-regulated learning strategies more often in online contexts than in-person (Lee & Tsai, 2011; Sletten, 2017). But a survey of 20,000 middle school and high school age students conducted in 2020 found that only 41 percent of them could motivate themselves to do their schoolwork at home (Mathewson, 2020). This is the biggest hurdle for making online learning an effective alternative to the classroom. However, these results may have a lot to do with the type of education students were receiving prior to the disruption of COVID-19. In schools that were already prioritizing 21st century skills by allowing students to work at their own pace and prioritize their own goals, instruction was not disrupted as severely by COVID-19 (Mathewson, 2020; Levine, 2020). The more students had been encouraged to take ownership and be active in their own learning, the more easily they were able to transition to productive online learning.

But for the majority of students, a problem remains. How can you teach self-directedness now, when online learning makes students less motivated to learn than ever? The Institute for Self-Directed Learning provides a number of resources for schools, parents, and students in order to promote this essential 21st century skill. They suggest that one important change that helps promote self-directedness is the shift in accountability from a top-down, teacher-student model to a more community-focused model (Institute of Self-Directed Learning, 2021). Students are most self-directed when they are being held accountable for their learning not by an authority figure, but by a set of norms or a code of conduct generated by the students themselves.

This emphasis on community, whether online or in-person, appears in other research as well. A student’s motivation is directly related to their sense of belonging in a classroom or academic community (Ryan & Deci, 2000). When students feel more investment in a learning community, typically because they feel their intelligence and effort is being acknowledged and can see the increase in their own ability over time, they are more likely to persist in their learning and practice self-directedness (Carpenter & Pease, 2013). One strategy for creating such a community in online learning is to develop community agreements for the digital classroom. Students should decide together what their learning goals are and what constitutes appropriate behavior in the digital space, as well as how they will hold each other responsible for maintaining these agreements. Then by returning to these agreements each class, educators can build a culture of accountability and foster a sense of ownership in student work (Henry, 2020). This method for cultivating a culture of trust and respect in online classrooms can help to compensate for some of the depersonalization that comes from learning online and help students to feel more emotionally secure in their learning, leading to better academic work.

TEACHING COLLABORATION ONLINE

Virtual learning makes it more difficult for teachers to create productive collaborative environments, but several successful examples do exist. Many instructors have taken advantage of the “breakout room” feature of video chat applications in order to quickly create student groups within a particular assignment. More effective and long-term collaboration is usually the result of curricular planning though. Many of the previously-mentioned methods for establishing a sense of online community will be applicable here as well. Intentional communication is important, as students are more likely to be engaged in a collaborative project if they understand the reason why they are being asked to work in a group (as opposed to individually) (Columbia Center for Teaching and Learning, n.d.). This could also mean choosing an appropriately complex problem for students to solve together, one that might require them to combine their various skillsets.

The critical thinking and problem-solving skills that students practice in a collaborative team work environment will also nurture crucial interpersonal skills, giving students more confidence. Although most platforms are online in terms of education right now, there still needs to be mode of contact students can collaborate with their teachers, educators other students, friends, and family (Bisen, 2021). This will allow students to understand the methods of communication and the culture of communication. Since most of the students have to meet through an online classroom platform, they can always utilize different resources such as breakout room features, and online groups created by those platforms. They may not be interacting in person however they still understand the culture of sharing information collaboration and teamwork. Learners get to discuss ideas and reflect on each other's work while also reflecting on their own strengths and shortcomings, which they can learn to make better from each other. In addition to having these skills, online groups can stimulate a sense of autonomy and responsibility for each and every learner. Oftentimes, one group work requires presentation of results, the peers of the group must communicate and collaborate effectively in order to be able to transfer information when they are presenting their project. To effectively complete tasks at hand, the team members divide the responsibilities evenly. This helps to create a sense of respect for each other as well as accountability and trust, therefore also nurturing relationships.

One school that has handled the transition to online learning relatively well is the South Bronx Community Charter High School (Levine, 2020). Educators there have been able to productive collaboration and class community online, and their successes present a useful case study. For one thing, school policy required that every student be given a device, even before the pandemic. This meant that, at a bare minimum, all students had access to the tools they needed to participate in online coursework, and students and teachers both were already familiar with an online learning management system chosen by the school. But more importantly, the school’s use of advisory groups prior to the pandemic provided a built-in community structure that was effective and minimizing each student’s sense of isolation (Levine, 2020). When instruction was happening in-person, a small group of students would meet regularly with their advisor to discuss their professional and emotional development. These pre-existing relationships of trust were invaluable once the pandemic hit, as they allowed the school to check in with students daily, which in turn reduced student isolation and fostered more accountability. As a result, the school saw a much lower drop in attendance when learning shifted online, and students remained engaged in their work. This type of trust and accountability is essential for creating collaborative environments (Columbia Center for Teaching and Learning, n.d.).

CONCLUSION

Despite the clear challenges of teaching and learning online during a global pandemic, the upheaval in education brought about by COVID-19 includes some reasons for optimism. If nothing else, it has brought new attention to the social and emotional needs of students. Over 83% of states around the country have given more emphasis to social and emotional learning since the start of the pandemic, and as some schools began to reopen in 2020, they did so with a renewed focus on providing students with training in soft skills (Edna Bennett Pierce Prevention Research Center, n.d.). In the near term, these skills will help students process the losses and changes brought by the pandemic, but they will also equip students to grow and develop 21st-century skills in the future. The past few decades have seen a growing consensus in education that social and emotional learning is not secondary to academic learning but actually creates the conditions for it (The Aspen Institute, n.d.). The pandemic has caused educators to confront these needs more directly than ever, which could set a helpful precedent for education moving forward.

The shift to virtual learning has also exposed the severe limitations of a one-size-fits all approach to education, forcing institutions to take much needed steps toward greater personalization. A top-down approach to education that doesn’t account for the various aptitudes or learning modalities of students has become “completely unmanageable with kids spread out across their various households working independently” (Mandel, 2020). In this way, the pandemic has accelerated a change that was already underway in education and could be of great value to students. The shift to online learning in 2020 was admittedly haphazard, and many institutions were not prepared to support their students or teachers through the transition. But it did demonstrate the potential for personalized learning that could come with more curricular planning and support. At the very least, online learning has been normalized, and as more sophisticated pedagogical tools are developed, it could become an essential part of a personalized, 21st-century education. Because the more that schools can engage the individual interests and abilities of their students, the more equipped these students will be for success in real-life situations (Brandt & Thompson, 2020).

Finally, the state of learning during COVID has all but required students to practice the valuable 21st century skill of adaptability. The entire purpose of teaching 21st century skills is to prepare students to be able to thrive in unpredictable environments, and the pandemic has certainly been that. Of course, surviving the difficulty is not in-itself enough. Students still need to be taught that change is not “bad or undesirable,” but rather that it is inevitable and creates opportunities for new ideas and solutions (Martin, 2020). Then they will always be able to reference the pandemic as not just a challenge, but as an example of their own resilience. Navigating COVID hasn’t been easy, but the flexibility and adaptability that students have had to learn will hopefully give them confidence to face whatever the future holds.
