Chapter 10

A Three-Tier Change Model

  • Seda Khadimally
    https://orcid.org/0000-0001-6374-3248
    North American University, USA

ABSTRACT

This chapter focuses on a project-management-based online learning design (PMBOLD) project planned to be executed with a three-tier change model. The PMBOLD project introduces an online instructor training project proposed to be implemented at an executive management level at today’s post-pandemic higher educational organizations. Based on the popular analysis, design, development, implementation, and evaluation (ADDIE) design framework, the project is presented as a roadmap for all change agents at universities, especially those currently left with managerial choices to make as to whether to completely transition to online learning or continue with hybrid learning post pandemic. The goal of this intervention plan is to bring about a solid organizational change in today’s higher educational institutions that implement distance learning and e-learning in their administrative operations and instructional activities. The proposed project for online instructor training will have curricular and ID-focused implications as well as substantive managerial applications.

INTRODUCTION

Before, during, and after the implementation of this planned educational intervention at the administrative level, the targets must be explicitly stressed by the change agents, long-term—rather than single, one-day workshops only (Anderson, 2010)—training must be planned and executed, and follow-ups must be conducted, so this e-learning project can yield positive outcomes. Thus, all change agents during each phase of the project will be in charge of bringing about a sustainable transformation at higher educational organizations implementing e-learning in their instructional design and curricular activities. Each change facilitator will be responsible for addressing the vitality of change from the very beginning until the end of this process with utter clarity. They will be in charge of including each organizational member in the entire process of decision-making, making them part of the whole program. This will undoubtedly be made possible with utmost attention and listening. Also, the wide repertoire of know-how and skills every change agent is required to bring to the table is expected to help them handle some of the inherent issues and challenges that might potentially emerge along the way.

TIER 1

Initiating the Project

According to Lynch and Roecker (2010) every organization and each project will have its own specific requirements, so a project manager will need to customize these requirements for that specific organizational culture and for that project’s circumstances. In this context, agile design models will comprise a solid reference for this project’s manager and the team members, all of whom seek to successfully initiate, plan, execute, control, and close this technology-based training project at a higher educational institution.

There will be three major deliverables in the initiating phase of this online teacher training new program: 1) Building a strong business case with a solid project charter, 2) stating the scope of the preliminary project, and 3) securing an effective participation of all team members that support the development of the project (Lynch & Roecker, 2007).

Regarding the first deliverable in this phase, this proposed action plan will serve as a concrete project that will provide a good pitch to all executive sponsors with whom a continuing relationship will be sustained for the duration of the project. Managing a new project in any organization takes time, resources, as well as collaborative efforts to plan, design, develop, and evaluate the outcome. Thus, a project charter will guide the first stage of initiating this project, and then allow for effective project management and development actions within any higher educational organization.

According to Dick, Carey, and Carey (2009), the planning of DE is critical and it takes an inclusion of all of the different components that play a crucial role in the successful design, delivery, and evaluation of a DE course including learners, the content itself, the course delivery method, learning environment, technological tool(s), and others. Adapting the similar approach to their systems approach to DE course design and delivery, Moore and Kearsley (2012), too, emphasized the importance of using all of the necessary components and subcomponents of the school system—both in traditional, face-to-face and online, asynchronous, virtual learning environments. Most importantly, if the instructor and designer of this online, asynchronous course manages to incorporate into the course curriculum some of the subtle elements such as culture, diversity in learning styles (Bloom, 1968), languages, educational backgrounds, learning goals, preferences, needs, interests, she will end up successfully designing, developing, and delivering effective and efficient instruction pertinent to the various topics taught.

Scope Definition

The field of applied linguistics is incredibly diverse. As an instructor with ample formal training and knowledge of pedagogy, a project charter is a project manager’s and his team’s business case; that is, it provides the project sponsors and all other important players with the reasons why that project was selected and needs to be completed (Allen & Sites, 2002). The charter of this instructor training project will clearly and comprehensively present what type of project is about to be initiated, why, and how (see Figure 1 – Project charter template to be used for this project, Lynch & Roecker, 2007).

In regards to defining the scope of this online course design project that aims at assisting all higher education class instructors with their technology-supported instructional practices, a project charter will be written by all members in the project team. The project charter will not only be written in an extensive manner, but also provide the executive sponsors and each member of the project team with their required roles and responsibilities. Costs, project-based requirements, other necessary resources, scheduled timelines, risks associated with the project, and additional related variables will be explicitly articulated from the beginning, so any problems that might potentially emerge during the initiation and development phases can be prevented in advance. Without a thorough diagnosis of needs or what and why this new training program for all online course instructors is much needed in its initiation stage, the project might end up unsustainable or fail to meet the training needs (Lynch & Roecker, 2007) of some of the online instructors at higher educational institutions. According to Lynch and Roecker (2007), a well-built project charter is prepared with the what and why of a project at its onset. Therefore, in this phase of the project, an elaborate, clear, and comprehensive instructor training project charter will be highly instrumental as the project is initiated.

The below project charter template will be used to create a comprehensive project charter for this online course instructor training program:

Figure 1. Project charter template (adapted from Lynch & Roecker, 2007)

Project Charter Template Instructions

The layout and contents of this template are recommendations and should be modified to fit organizational and/or project requirements. The fields of the template and their contents are described below:

  • Project Title: The title of the project. This may be a new online registrations system, a new course management system, a new course, etc.
  • Project Manager: The name of the project manager.
  • Description: A description of the project the clearly describes the bounds of the project. The template provides three subfields to aid in describing the project.
  • Project Need: A short paragraph that overviews the reason for this project. Examples of this field are:

A new online registration system to replace the manual telephone-based system to improve customer satisfaction and provide 24 x 7 registrations.

Project Description: A paragraph that describes the bounds of the project and may include high-level deliverables of the project. This paragraph is the preliminary project scope statement that may describe what is included in the project deliverables and what is not included in the project deliverables. This statement will be refined in the project planning phase, described in the chapter 3. However, the boundaries outlined here must be adhered during this refinement process.

  • Project Start Date: The planned start of the project – the planned project charter agreement date.
  • Projected Finish Date: The date when the project is placed into production or the course is delivered.
  • Goal: The goal of this course development. An example of this might be ‘Develop and deliver a new on-line product line - A sales course’.

Assumptions, Constraints, and Risks

Assumptions: A summary of the conditions assumed in the project. A sample of this might be: Each participant in this course will have access to Microsoft Word and Excel. In addition, the participants will have at least 56kb Internet access and access to an e-mail client.

Constraints: A summary of the constraints for this project. An example of this might be: This project will be developed using existing Internet development applications and existing resources.

Risks: An outline of the potential risks involved with this project. An example of a risk for this project this might be identifying limited information technology staff to support the design, development, and testing of this new course.

Resources

Financial: The projected budget, in whole currency, for the project. This item is one entry that is the total budget for the project.

Personnel: The total number of people required to complete this project. This value is the total of the number of full-time and full-time equivalent personnel required to complete the project.

Material: An outline of the materials required to complete the project – not operate the project deliverables in production. An example of this might be 10 copies of a course authoring application, 10 desktop personal computers with access to a development server with 120 GB of storage.

Approach: An outline of the high-level steps and milestone dates planned to complete this project. An example of this for a new online course might be:

  • Initial course prototype – dd/mm/yyyy
  • Usability study – dd/mm/yyyy
  • Refined course prototype – dd/mm/yyyy

TIER 2

Instructional Design

ADDIE, a Popular Instructional Design Model Applied to the Online Course Instructor Training

Designing this online course consistent with the principles of instruction under the ADDIE model and constructivist learning framework is the primary goal, which can greatly assist with the manner in which each online course instructor facilitates their students’ meaningful learning. The scope of the project, combined with the goal of achieving meaningful learning for all higher-education students can help to better comprehend and appreciate the epistemological, philosophical, and pedagogical underpinnings of this design project.

Teaching with cutting-edge educational technologies in this era, especially post pandemic, has become paramount, relevant, and timely. Since higher-education students in the current digital age who are enrolled in various academic courses are older adolescents and/or adults, appealing to their needs with appropriate social media tools, and helping them transform into self-directed learners (Knowles, 1970) could pose a real challenge on the part of the online course instructors. Thus, the Analysis, Design, Development, Implementation, and Evaluation (ADDIE) ID framework can provide the entire project management team with a solid foundation for helping instructors comprehensively learn how to manage and utilize social networking tools, and thereafter, create authentic, meaningful, memorizable, motivational, and engaging (Allen & Sites, 2012) activities for all learners.

Thus, in order to overcome potential challenges and risk of creating project-based, tech-savvy, collaborative, interactive, authentic, effective, and engaging (Reiser & Dempsey, 2007) activities supported with an array of social networking applications (e.g., Facebook, Twitter, Instagram, etc.), the entire online course designed for online instructors will be based on the ADDIE ID framework (see Figure 2).

Figure 2. ADDIE, a popular ID model this e-learning project (Retrieved from Gagné, Wager, Golas, & Keller, 2005, p. 13)

ADDIE Applied to the Instructor Training Online Course Design in the Initiating Phase of the Project

In this first stage of the ADDIE ID model, a thorough analysis helped the project manager create a needs analysis survey (see survey questions 1, 2, 3, 8, and 10 in Figure 3), which she designed with SurveyMonkey. In the conduct of this survey, she collected essential data from all course instructors at the school locale, by sending them an online survey link. This survey simply demonstrated the technology needs of teachers at the pertinent school locale. She then designed her weekly syllabi based upon the data she derived from this analysis. The data she collected demonstrated that some of the necessary technology tools are already utilized by teachers both inside and outside of the classroom while some others are not. Thus, the data reported by majority of online instructors showed that all teachers needed to be on board as far as their use of cutting-edge technologies in class and in terms of the school administration’s attitude towards and encouragement of their use of innovative, digital tools both inside and outside of the classroom.

Importance of Instructor Training for Effective Online Teaching

This online instructor training course is crucial for all online instructors teaching at universities, especially for those that have transitioned to teaching with online technology tools with the emergence of the pandemic. By use of social media tools, instructors can gradually build upon their newly acquired knowledge of teaching all skills in the English language. These new technology-assisted teaching skills will be highly congruent with 21st century digital literacies. The teachers’ instructional applications with social media technologies are particularly critical to support their students’ knowledge-building process, because they will initially begin to deliver the content via scaffolding their students’ learning. As these instructors’ students begin to improve their understanding and practice of the subject matter, the instructors will gradually let them learn the subject matter on their own and by use of an array of social media tools. This learning/teaching approach embedded in the initiation phase of this project directly aligns with the constructivist learning pedagogy (Vygotsky, 1978). From an ID viewpoint, another note on the importance of teaching with social networking tools is that the entire life cycle of this online course to be designed for online course instructors will be predominantly guided by the principles of ADDIE.

Gap Analysis: Identifying the Gaps with 3 Strategies

  • 1. In the process of completing an analysis of the de facto system in an organization and generating results from this analysis, the project management team can use gap analysis-based strategies. The first strategy can help establish target objectives that are specific and clear. This often occurs with a closer look into the organization’s mission statement, strategic goals, and its objectives as the organization learns, grows, and develops.
  • 2. The second strategy would be to analyze the current state of all business operations and processes across this organization. Gathering relevant pieces of data can be achieved through comprehensive data collection methods such as surveys, interviews, online questionnaires, observations within the organization, brainstorming sessions, and other documented information from within the organization’s repository. The project manager and her team will seek to collect data pertinent to the instructors’ and other staff members’ performance levels, along with the allocation of an array of resources.
  • 3. With respect to the second gap analysis strategy, it is crucial that the project management team compare the organization’s previously set target goals to those in its current state to see whether or not they were met. During the systems analysis process, the third strategy that a project management team needs to use is preparing a future-oriented and expansive action plan that must outline each particular step the team must take in order to close the gaps they identified. This comprehensive online instructor training project not only provides the project team with a list of specific steps to take for the future state at higher educational institutions, but allow them to achieve the target objectives as they bridge the gaps between this higher educational organization’s current state and future state.

Content Delivery Methods: The Unit–Module–Topic (UMT) Model for Instructor Training for an Online Course Design Project

This design of this online instructor training course was based on a supporting ID model, UMT. The entire design process led the project manager of this course design to consider an array of factors to incorporate into the design. These factors can be listed as the content, the background training of all university instructors, the process through which the students’ learning experiences will take place (i.e., methodology including the type(s) of learning environment and all of the social networking applications), as well as the methods by which to assess instructors’ training/learning experiences. “By following through with this process, the instructors will find that teaching their adult learners with social networking technologies “is an exciting and dynamic experience, one that will be welcomed by both the learners and [the instructors]” (Moore & Kearsley, 2012, p. 152).

Work Breakdown Structure (WBS)

Especially during the initiating phase of the project, it is critical to build a solid team of executive sponsors, subject matter experts (SMEs), educational designers, instructional technologists, online course instructors, and school administrators (Hirumi, 2013), all of whom are expected to be on board. Each of these team members will be experts in the learning and teaching field that this project will revolve around.

Each team member’s role and responsibility has been clearly defined for the successful execution of this project and its deliverables. In regards to this, Lynch and Roecker (2007) pontificated that instructional designers are generally “required for the planning and executing phases” (para. 2) of a new project. Nevertheless, during the continuation of the project, some of the team members might be continually needed as far as their roles, responsibilities, and general expertise on computer-assisted education (CAE), along with their ability to oversee the benefits and/or risks associated with executing and developing this project. These individuals are to include the project manager, veteran university faculty members (i.e., some of the experienced instructors with tenure), and program supervisors.

Throughout the initiating phase of this project, it will be critical to secure an effective participation of all team members that are to support its development throughout. The team members will be informed that where project manager might potentially fail to empower or enable participation of each team member as proposed in this project charter, the executive sponsor might have to intervene as a negotiator between the project manager and these team members with whom the project manager might be in conflict later during the project implementation. Resistance to change among some of the team members and possibly among some of the instructors who may not have utilized social networking tools before, ambiguity in roles and responsibilities, in addition to failure in fulfilling assigned tasks per se are anticipated to be major causes for such program intervention, which could result in significant delays in the execution, controlling, and closure stages of this project. Resistance as a potential inhibiting factor will be later addressed in detailed under the planning and executing phases. In such cases, the role of the executive sponsor is to ensure the stability, smooth development, and sustainability of the entire project.

Risk Management Plan: Strategies to Overcome Potential Resistance from Staff

As this project proceeds through its planning, development, and execution phases, a successful buy-in from potentially resistant staff will be vital for the effectiveness and duration of the project until it completes its full life cycle. Following the below strategies in order to effectively deploy this social network technology-based online instructor training project will significantly affect all change efforts, and therefore mitigate any emerging issues or challenges that may require management.

  • 1. Creating buy-in from potentially resistant staff will be an arduous, yet rewarding task at the end. In order to effectively manage this social networking-supported instructor training project and to enhance the success of all project deployment efforts, it is imperative that each change facilitator follow certain steps. Following these steps can especially allow for buy-in from the staff that might potentially resist this change process (see Figure 4).

Concerning how to deal with the resistant staff in an organization, each change agent that will be assigned a specific role and responsibility within the team, and each will be required to use the following strategies that Lawrence (1969) offered: Get the people involved to participate in making the change, get them to understand the true nature of resistance, clarify the technical aspects of new ideas, emphasize new standards of performance for staff specialists, encourage them to think in different ways, and make use of the fact that signs of resistance can serve as a practical warning signal in directing and timing technological changes.

Resistance could be a major inhibitor ahead of the change agents planning, developing, and executing this new e-training program designed for online course instructors at schools. It may not only impede the targeted transformation organization wide, but also cause resentment between the younger generation of course teachers who are competent in utilizing social networking sites in their instructional practices and those who are not. Hence, in order to create buy-in from resisting staff and successfully manage the implementation efforts, there are additional strategies that will be used during these phases of the project.

The change agents will be responsible for asking the right questions in the right manner. Most importantly, they will need to project their own feelings on the employees (Block, 2000, as cited in Anderson, 2010), and reflect on their previous experiences about change within other such educational organizations within the area or district. This will help these change agents set a good example for the online instructors and staff who can, in turn, model these behaviors in the same way. Initially recognizing employees’ resistance (Anderson, 2010), using empathy, and conversing with the resistant staff in a way that they could finally understand the reason why innovation is necessary with the implementation of this new instructor training program.

As a pragmatic project, this executive management plan can serve as a solid guide to overcome one of the aforementioned potential inhibitors, resistance, which might especially come from older employees (Yeatts, 2000). If there is resistance to an extreme degree from veteran faculty at higher educational institutions, this action plan will allow all change facilitators to effectively handle the situation at the onset of the problem. Any change in the program might be taken emotionally by these older workers, and perceived as a personal attack. They might then involve their feelings (e.g., fear of the unknown, frustration of uncertainty, anxiety of lack of knowledge, confusion due to unclear messages conveyed by the change agents) in the process.

Another strategy is to explain and clearly articulate why such an innovative e-training program, which is designed for university instructors assigned to teach online courses, is required now rather than later. In regards to this, an action should be taken immediately. This is anticipated to be an ongoing effort with revisions, and the process will continue long after the change implementation. During this time period, follow-ups will be conducted in order to measure the effectiveness of all project implementation efforts, and an ongoing assessment will be critical, which will call for an ongoing, iterative evaluation plan.

Figure 4. Risk management plan form (Lynch & Roecker, 2007)

  • 2. User Participation Modes: With respect to the success or failure of ISD project implementations, I also understand that there is a direct relationship between the project size and its impact on users’ participation in the creation of the system. Wagner and Piccoli (2007) proposed as they discussed this relationship. According to the researchers, users would be more committed to getting fully involved in the project if they perceive its effects to be positive on their work; however, before this phase and after the process starts, this level of participation tends to fall and they do not pay the same attention.

This is an interesting point to closely look at as far as the relationship between ISD implementations and risk management According to Amrit, van Hillegersberg, and van Diest (2013), user participation could vary based on these two variables: Type and depth. Lynch and Gregor (2004) provided that the degree of user participation relies on whether the users are consultative (some users within the organization willing to participate voluntarily), representative (e.g., reference groups for selected users), or consensus groups (when several users participate). Also, where depth of their participation is involved, Lynch and Gregor (2004) additionally provided a three-tier user participation framework, which consists of “stages of the process, frequency, and voice” (p. 71).

Change Management Plan

Lynch and Roecker (2010) provided that there are parameters and deliverables for a new project’s planning, development, and execution phases. The authors also noted that agile models’ variables and deliverables are clearly articulated in these particular stages of a project at its planning and preparation. According to Lynch and Roecker (2010) “these variables include cost, schedule, requirements, and risks, and as each of these is made more fully known, a go/no-go decision must be made at the end of each phase” (para. 11). Where the planning, development, and execution phases are involved, this e-learning project will draw on the project principles proposed by Lynch and Roecker (2010), who explained that a PMBOLD project “goes beyond the mere delivery of static content. Instead, it includes collaboration, both synchronous and asynchronous, as well as some type of shared learning experience with fellow students” (para. 2) (For change requests and iterative design based on ADDIE and IPECC, see Figures 5 & 6).

These aforementioned change principles will directly align with Hirumi’s (2013) work on e-learning design that focuses on a three-tier pedagogy-technology framework. Similar to this three-level instructional technology framework, this ESL instructor online training project, which will be based upon teachers’ instruction with social networking applications, should be regarded as an action plan. This plan should be considered as one that not only highlights some of the issues that might potentially arise during e-learning interactions (i.e., human-human, human-content, and human-tool interactions), but also eloquently explains the need for integrating prominent theoretical frameworks into practice of e-learning project planning, design, and development. Regarding theoretical frameworks that guide pedagogy and technology training, it is noteworthy that Vygotskian social constructivism (1978) was selected as a major learning theory framework that guided this e-learning project as it incarnated.

Organizational change is often considered to be a large-scale operation. It requires the type of leadership which is aimed to have a tremendous impact on the values with which all organizational members act, communicate, share, and even react to one another. As such, resistance to change cannot be considered as an independent phenomenon in this change process. In fact, it will be considered as a driving force and a catalyst of change in an organization.

Schedule, Budget, and Staffing

Schedule: This project is anticipated to be completed in the course of 6 months (starting from January of 2022 to the end of July of 2022) (see Figure 6 - The specific timelines, including the project procurement plan, executing, controlling, revisions, corrections, and requests for change, as well as closing).

Budget: Other than time, the budget will be another vital resource to allocate during the project. The course development fee through a popular social networking application, Facebook, will be tantamount of $10,000 per one instructor training course with an additional $2,000 fee each time the course is offered and needs to be developed and/or modified.

Staffing: The project manager, executive sponsors, subject matter experts (SMEs), educational designers, instructional technologists, course instructors, and school administrators and personnel (Hirumi, 2013) including supervisors, department chairs, assistant directors, other program coordinators and staff members that fully understand the necessity of teaching ESL with robust use of social media tools in each classroom, (from beginners to advanced levels)

Communication Management Plan

All successful e-learning projects planned, developed, executed, and evaluated are the products of a collaborative work with all stakeholders involved in each step of the entire project.

Lynch and Roecker (2010) suggested a large network of project team members to run the project starting from the analysis and initiation stages to its conclusion. With this in mind, this online ESL instructor training course design project will follow generic project management principles in all phases of the project (from initiation to planning to design to development to execution to evaluation, and to conclusion).

Besides the primary purpose of executing an effective and efficient online training for faculty at higher educational institutions, it is also the intent that each faculty member and all staff agree to get on board. However, the project managers and team members are cognizant of the fact that this might not often be the case during these particular stages of this technology-assisted instructor training project. Thus, constant, honest, and open, and communication among all stakeholders involved in this change process is the key to the successful execution and closing of this online instructor training project.

Facilitation of the Online Course Design

In order to provide all online teacher trainees with ample interaction with the content, the instructor, and with their peers, and to keep them on task, various engaging methods of teaching will be selected. These methods will help facilitate active learning and collaboration among the students and the instructor, while encouraging them to explore their own learning experiences independently, autonomously, which is critical in terms of creating a learning environment that will be student-oriented. Because this is an asynchronous learning/teaching setting, “strategies such as problem-based learning, collaboration, and student-led discussions work well” (Smaldino, Lowther, & Russell, 2011, as cited in Simonson et al., 2012, p. 204). It is imperative in a second/foreign language learning environment supported with asynchronous technologies that the instructor facilitating learning process and encourage her students to take the responsibility and ownership of their own learning processes.

The facilitation of this online course design draws on an exemplary e-learning program that was previously implemented at a research-based university in Sydney, Australia, all members of the staff and faculty learned in time to deal with the project’s extended application and planning periods, set timelines, long-term project development phase, and a learning and evaluation periods (Ward, West, Peat, & Atkinson, 2010). During these phases, faculty, staff, and students commonly experienced and enjoyed all e-learning activities delivered from within this online course design.

Procurement Plan

The following three strategies will be used by the project team to procure resources for the effective execution and successful completion of the project:

  • 1. In-house development,
  • 2. Outsourcing (from prominent social networking sites sites such as Facebook),
  • 3. and customizing the course design as an innovative instructional product.

Risks and Challenges Associated with the ISD Implementation

Other than the advantages of especially the second strategy (i.e., outsourcing) that the members of the project team will use, there might also emerge a number outsourcing-related disadvantage. Confidential information can be compromised. Also, according to Dennis et al., (2009) in-house personnel are not learning the skills needed to create the system. Thus, there may emerge a few downsides of outsourcing during new ISD implementations. It is true that with the outsourcing strategy followed, the ownership of technical skills may shift or be completely replaced. That is, the technical know-how will be different from that of the senior management, middle management, ends users, and certainly that of the team’s technical experts engaged in the new ISD implementation. It is also undeniable that budget-related issues might surface, and there is the certainly risk of jeopardizing ISD-related technical or other confidential information.

Nevertheless, outsourcing is still likely to reduce user participation to a certain degree, which is not necessarily bad. Lin and Shao (2000) pontificated that outsourcing tends to reduce user involvement and the system impact is likely to reduce user attitude. The former may not be necessarily detrimental or life-threatening to the lifecycle of a project. However, for example, the lifecycle of a software product development might certainly have been compromised due to over involvement of end-users, consumers, or the clients. Therefore, as far as the success—or the failure—of SDLC goes, it is plausible to draw the conclusion that issues potentially arising may not necessarily stem from the technical failures or lack of technical skills of the project team outsourcing to create that new system. These appear to pose inherent risks with a multilateral approach to the relationship between ISD implementation and risk management. Eventually, by outsourcing during the system implementation, some of the issues associated with ISD management may be mitigated by the project management team.

TIER 3

Evaluation Plan

Marion (2002) pointed out that organizational culture can be strengthened by Total Quality Management (TQM) which refers to a strategy to increase effective productivity at schools. Effective TQM implemented during the project controlling and closure phases of this instructor training online course design project.

The e-training program designed for all university instructors will be achieved by capitalizing on an “interpersonal dimension of an organization” (Marion, 2002, p. 245). Marion (2002) also stressed that TQM can be sustained via “tacitly acknowledging the holistic nature of an organizational culture, one that includes all elements of the production process” (p. 245) (see Figure 6).

The educational climate at today’s higher educational institutions puts emphasis on interpersonal relations, which leads each school member to believe that in the lack of a healthy organizational climate wherein communication is not sustained, obtaining productivity is unfeasible. During this project’s controlling and closure phases, the role of SMEs will not be overlooked. The substantive responsibilities of SMEs will greatly contribute to reducing any potential risks and challenges that might be associated with the project. SMEs could also alleviate challenges to this project, particularly if the project manager is not an expert in the e-learning and distant learning fields this project is executed in. The evaluation plan will help determine SMEs’ key roles and participation in the project.

One of the foci of this training instructor proposal outline is the ability to convey the message that successful organizational culture leadership will be exercised as the overall training program quality is evaluated, revisions are done, and the project comes to a conclusion. This will not be possible without the internalization of espoused moral values of universities, along with the embedded instructional skills of each online course instructor, all staff members’ habits of collective thinking, and the teaching philosophy of the educational institution (i.e., unconventional wisdom). Other values that will be respected and appreciated throughout the project quality control and closure stages will be the school culture. All will be maintained through ongoing, formative evaluations during the project implementation and through summative evaluations upon completion of the project.

FUTURE RESEARCH DIRECTIONS

For future research in the e-learning and online course design and faculty training fields with its managerial applications, it is important to stress the use of social media tools that can greatly influence teaching and learning practices provided that each online course instructor is encouraged and trained to effectively integrate use of these applications into their lesson plans and hands-on classroom practices. At the executive supervisory level, it will be imperative that future researchers, practitioners, and leaders in the pertinent field plan, design, implement, and evaluate all resources necessary for the proper conduct of scheduling, budgeting, and staffing, all of which should be allocated in the right manner and for the right reasons during the process of executing the entire online training project.

CONCLUSION

Achieving a sustainable e-learning project implementation in any higher educational organization will involve inclusion. It is imperative that all change agents continuously include the resisting employees, colleagues, or supervisors in the entire process of change. Inviting them to participate in the change process is a precursor to successfully bringing about change across the school. Furthermore, this can lead to a participatory, democratic, and peaceful work environment for all instructors, staff, and program supervisors involved in the e-training program. Regarding participatory leadership, Goleman (1995) pointed out that democratic leaders often build consensus by continued participation. There are other steps to take during these phases of the social networking technology-based training project designed for online course instructors. The project manager of this program created a strong charter with her team, by taking from the three-tier change model, formulated by Kotter (1996). This change model will be effective in overcoming potential challenges, one of which is instructors’ inherent resistance to change within the program. Kotter’s (1996) eight-step model that this proposal has been inspired by includes the following: Establishing a sense of urgency, building a powerful guiding coalition, creating a vision, communicating this vision, empowerment of others to take actions on this vision, creating short-term wins, consolidation of improvements and generating further change, as well as institutionalizing new approaches for prospective change and continued academic growth at the post-pandemic higher educational institutions.

KEY TERMS AND DEFINITIONS

ADDIE: Acronym for analysis, design, development, implementation, and evaluation. It is a popular instructional design model used in the project management, systems design, and instructional design areas.

ID: An acronym for instructional design.

PMBOLD: An acronym for project-management based online learning design.

SMEs: An acronym for subject matter experts.

TQM: An acronym for total quality management.

UMT: An acronym for the unit-module-topic instructional design model.

WBS: An acronym for work breakdown structure.

Chapter 11

  • Yetkin Yildirim
    Rice University, USA
  • Kamil Yildirim
    Rice University, USA
  • Emin Alp Arslan
    Rice University, USA
  • Ibrahim Eren Bisen
    Rice University, USA

ABSTRACT

The purpose of this chapter is to explore how COVID-19 has widened the achievement gap between students of different socioeconomic statuses. The chapter will demonstrate that students from lower socioeconomic classes received lower-quality education than students from higher socioeconomic situations who in some instances received an even better education than they had pre-pandemic face-to-face learning. These widening achievement gaps along with chronic absenteeism and lower student motivation are some of the biggest problems that the pandemic has brought to online education. This chapter goes on to explore how to remedy some of these problems. By fixing these issues, online learning and even education as a whole can emerge stronger than it was before the disruption of COVID-19.

INTRODUCTION

The coronavirus has brought on some of the biggest changes the world has seen in recent years. One of the most impacted institutions has been education. When emergency online learning took over in early 2020, many believed it to be a momentary disturbance and the proper precautions weren’t taken. When education continued exclusively online, the precautions that some institutions had ignored came back to bite them and cost many students their education. The failure of institutions to adapt to a virtual environment was a detrimental factor for many students’ education. The prevalence of online learning in 2020 caused the quality of education to suffer, and achievement gaps have started to widen even further, especially for struggling families.

There are many types of online learning, including classes that transpire entirely over the internet, video conferencing, and stand-alone educational software programs. But this chapter will use online learning to refer to learning that takes place partially or entirely over the internet through the help of an instructor. We can also distinguish online learning into two categories: learning conducted totally as a substitute or alternative to face-to-face learning and online hybrid learning that is blended with face-to-face instruction to provide learning enhancement.

Online learning does allow for flexibility, and it does make learning more accessible, at least to a point. However, the rate at which this shift has happened due to COVID-19 has been staggering, and the results of that rapid adjustment have been mixed. In fact, by April 2020, more than 1.4 billion students in over 190 countries around the world were abruptly shut out of their schools (Sheppard, 2021). This is the difference between online learning and emergency remote teaching. While online learning, if done right, can in many cases be just as fruitful as face-to-face instruction, and in some cases more fruitful, the forced shift to an online environment due to this unprecedented shutdown accomplishes the opposite. Not only is the quality of education falling, but the gap in education between those of different socioeconomic levels, disproportionately Black and Hispanic students, has also been increasing.

Effects on the Achievement Gap and Economy

There has been a persistent gap in educational achievement between Black/Hispanic students and white students. In fact, this gap has been estimated to cost the U.S. economy as much as $500 billion a year in productivity (Auguste et al., 2009). While progress has been made to narrow this gap over recent years, the disparity in academic achievement is still immense, and the emergency shift to virtual school is predicted to widen it (Stanford Center for Education Policy Analysis, n.d.). This gap not only disproportionately hurts minorities but also hurts the economy as a whole. Estimates predict that if this gap had been closed in 2009, today’s GDP could be anywhere from $426 billion to $705 billion higher (Dorn et al., 2020). Closing this gap is a crucial endeavor, but with COVID-19 looming over our heads, the near future looks bleak. The average K-12 student is predicted to lose about $70,000 of lifetime earnings because of educational losses caused by COVID-19 (Dorn et al., 2020).

While discussing the switch to online learning, it is important to remember that access to computers and the internet remains unequal. According to the Organization for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD), nearly all privileged 15-year-olds have a computer, but almost a quarter of disadvantaged students don’t. This situation reveals the urgent need to “bridge the digital divide,” in the words of the UN Conference on Trade and Development.

It must also be noted that many countries outside the US, especially developing countries, have it even worse (Vegas et al., 2020). Before the pandemic, 90 percent of children in low- income countries and 50 percent of children in middle-income countries came out of school without mastering the necessary basic secondary-level skills (Vegas et al., 2020). In comparison, only 30 percent of children in high-income countries like the US lack this level of academic achievement. But even 30 percent of children is too many, considering that this means 30 percent of the children are going to leave school without the skills needed to lead a successful life (Vegas et al., 2020). But after the pandemic, these numbers will only get worse. The children who live in poor countries who are already behind students from other countries will now face even worse conditions. In mid-April 2020, while about 90 percent of high-income countries were providing remote learning, only 25 percent of the low-income countries were providing any type of remote learning (Vegas et al., 2020). Most of the remote learning in low-income countries was provided through TV and radio, which isn’t as effective as online learning. Although these numbers will likely improve as the pandemic subsides, there is still a substantial difference between the percentage of low-income countries, where more people of lower socioeconomic status are located, and high-income countries that provide effective remote education.

The shift to emergency online learning affected girls disproportionately more than boys. In some countries, girls already lacked opportunities for education. When COVID-19 hit and education went online, even more discriminatory barriers were put in front of those girls who already had trouble getting education (Sheppard, 2021). Furthermore, when girls get out of school in these countries, they have higher chances of facing gender-based violence, and they gain more responsibilities at home, which makes it more difficult for them to ever get back into school. This increases the seriousness of the problem.

Wealthy families from around the world have the means to ensure that their children get a good education, regardless of the pandemic. Some such families have hired private tutors or teachers to teach their students at home without the dangers of catching the virus (Vegas et al., 2020). In most cases, students learn more with this type of personalized attention than they do in traditional classrooms. Some wealthy parents have also found online platforms which can give their children extra learning assistance. These online platforms are generally very expensive, so they aren’t an option for families of low income. Even though the wealthy families that do these only comprise a small percentage of the population, this still shows how the situation with COVID-19 is increasing the education gap between students of different socioeconomic status.

Negative Effects of COVID-19

By April of 2020, about 94 percent of learners throughout the world were feeling the effects of the pandemic on their education (UNSDG, 2020). But education was already in a crisis even before the disruptions brought by COVID-19. Worldwide, approximately 250 million children do not or cannot attend schools, and approximately 800 million adults are illiterate (UNSDG, 2020). The pandemic only made our pre-existing problems worse; it increased the number of children who were out of school, delayed the education that many students were receiving on a day-to-day basis, and in some cases made the education children were receiving less effective.

These effects were felt throughout the world by all socio-economic classes to different degrees. Preschoolers and elementary schoolers missed out on the opportunity to learn and practice social skills that needed to be nurtured. In low socio-economic areas, most of the children whose parents worked missed the proper food and nutrition they need, many of them missed the educational opportunities that were available, and these problems will only compound in the future. In secondary, collegiate, and graduate-level education, institutions have been more able to provide continuity through online lectures, which has made it easier for higher-level institutions to adapt to the problems that COVID-19 has brought. But many local and smaller higher-level institutions lacked the proper technological infrastructure and did not receive full support.

REMEDIES

Given the widening gap between students of different socioeconomic levels caused by COVID-19, it is now more important than ever to consider different learning paradigms that yield learning opportunities to students regardless of their status. Online education must be as easy to access as possible while not compromising its efficiency and effectiveness. In addition, many learners have been in a fragile emotional state during this pandemic, which providers of online education must take into account (Carter et al. 2020). If they do, then all learners will have an easier time adjusting to their online classes and an easier time learning. It is also important to remember that many learners are not as efficient when it comes to pacing their classwork themselves, and they tend to be less productive while working in a home environment (Carter et al. 2020).

Even though the crisis of COVID-19 is unique, much of the research done on the topic of online learning is still useful. Previous studies show that, in order to create an effective online learning environment for all types of students, teachers need to use careful instructional design and planning as well as a systematic model for design and development (Hodges, 2020). Also, creating an effective online environment for every student in a class will help lower the achievement gap between students of different socioeconomic levels. However, these two factors need to be implemented correctly to actually be able to help create an effective online learning environment. Unfortunately, this isn’t what is happening today, especially given the speed with which institutions had to transition to online learning.

Moderating Variables

Figure 1. Online education design’s moderating variables
Note. Reprinted from “The Difference Between Emergency Remote Teaching and Online Learning” by Hodges et al, 2020. Copyright 2020 by Educase Review.

In the book, Learning Online: What Research Tells Us About Whether, When and How, the authors identify nine variables that an online learning environment must consider (Means, 2014). Within each of these dimensions, there are different “options.” However, not all of these options work together well to create an effective online learning environment in every situation. For example, in a class of adults, it is generally more beneficial for the class to be asynchronous, whereas in a classroom of younger people, having synchronous classes can be more beneficial. Another thing to keep in mind is that choosing some options can change the effectiveness of other options. The student-instructor ratio, for example, can change many other factors in an online classroom. As the number of students in a class rises, it can be harder for a teacher to implement certain options in their class. For instance, the teacher of a large classroom might have to resort to using an automated source of feedback, because it is harder for them to give feedback to all of their students one by one. Therefore, to create an effective online learning environment, instructors need to choose the proper options for every class based on several factors like the age of the students. This can greatly decrease the achievement gap between students of different socioeconomic classes that has worsened during the pandemic. Using the correct options can improve the learning of the students in the class as a whole and help and get the students who are falling behind back on their feet. In fact, not only can this help close the gap in achievement among students of different socioeconomic levels, but it could also help close the gap in effectiveness between online learning and face-to-face learning.

Improving the Effectiveness of Online Learning

To make online learning environments as effective as face-to-face learning environments, two important factors have to be considered. In face-to-face learning, students have an extensive amount of support from outside sources, such as libraries and friend groups, that can only be found in a school environment. Lectures are only one part of face-to-face learning; these support factors are what help make face-to-face learning more effective and successful. When it comes to online learning, there is less support for students, especially when teachers can’t help their students face-to-face and lectures become a bigger, if not the biggest, part of the curriculum (Hodges et al., 2020). To make online learning more effective, institutions need to find resources and other factors to replace these supplements. Lectures alone aren't always enough to effectively teach students. In fact, it is especially important to find learner supports in an online environment, because that environment makes it even harder for some students to focus on lectures, including a lot of students from lower socioeconomic classes. There are many available options for online learner supports that can be used in an online learning environment. These options that the students have go a long way in helping the online learning environments and it also makes them more effective. Especially now, because of COVID-19, these learner supports are even more necessary since a very high number of students have no other choice other than online learning.

Pacing and Procrastination Prevention Skills

Furthermore, self-regulated learning, which refers to learning that is guided by metacognition, strategic action, and motivation to learn, is also very important in an online learning environment. Since students won’t be face-to-face with a teacher in a classroom, they need to be able to work and learn on their own. For example, these skills will be necessary when students are trying to use online resources to aid them with their learning, and when students work on classwork or homework assigned by their online instructor. However, these skills are not easy to acquire and demonstrate. Many students have problems with pacing and procrastination when they are on their own. Although this complication can exist in face-to-face learning too, in an online learning environment, it is much more apparent and can have more serious consequences. Pacing and procrastination have been a big problem for students during COVID-19. Many students spend long portions of their days online, which can cause the days to blend together and makes it easier for them to lose track of time. This can lead to them not completing their work when they are supposed to. Such students need to be supported with teacher-created timelines or a way that they are always able to see a clear timeline and the due dates of their assignments. Doing this can also help decrease the achievement gap between students of different socioeconomic classes by making it easier for students who have a hard time with online learning, which can be people from lower socioeconomic classes.

Technology Accessibility

Additionally, students do not all have the same access to technology. Some students might have poor internet connections and low-quality devices that make their online work take longer. Other students may not be as familiar with the online interfaces and applications used in their online learning environment. Consequently, they will have to learn to use these interfaces and applications before they can attend classes with them efficiently. The students who are under these circumstances are generally students who come from lower socioeconomic classes. We need to support students who have these issues and try to make sure that these students have adequate devices to effectively participate in online learning. On top of that, arguably the biggest factor that makes it harder for students to pace themselves, especially during COVID-19, is the lack of motivation. This lack of motivation could also be caused by the quality of the devices that a student uses. Moreover, students that aren't motivated to do their work, regardless of other factors, tend to procrastinate their work (Carter, 2020).

How Instructors Can Motivate their Students

One way that instructors can help students with pacing is by motivating the students who are not motivated. The easiest and most effective way to do this is through frequent communication with the students to ensure that they are keeping up with the class and understand what is being taught. A student who knows what he/she is doing is more likely to do what they are supposed to do. This communication can mean closely monitoring student engagement by looking at their participation and performance and giving personal assistance to those who are doing worse. Constant communication also helps the instructor evaluate a student’s access to technology. Personal assistance from the instructor will guarantee more interaction between students and their teachers. There are already many online instructional tools to monitor students and make this easier for teachers. Teachers are able to provide students with asynchronous support with helpful videos and sites. For example, teachers can provide a summarizing video after every chapter of their class. This can help the students understand the information and give a refresher over the topics learned throughout the chapter. Due dates and deadlines are other big motivators for students. These due dates have to be strict and should not be lenient. If the teachers are too lenient with due dates, which will almost certainly only hurt students in the long run, the student will try to take advantage of the teacher’s goodwill and good intentions. If students feel like they can get away by procrastinating more, it is safe to assume they will do so. Finally, progress reports on long-term assignments can help make sure that students are on track.

Student motivation plays perhaps the biggest role in self-regulation for students, especially in online environments. The effects of motivation and internalization have been studied and have been shown to increase student performance by encouraging high-quality learning and conceptual understanding (Deci et al., 1991). Even artificial intelligence technologies can help play a role in predicting student performance, a significant indicator of student motivation. One way this can be done is through the analysis of eye tracker data of online learners (Sharma et al., 2020). The AI looks for students that back-track and reread, which are closely associated with more motivated and engaged students. Another way that artificial intelligence can help facilitate this is

To help enforce due dates and monitor students, teachers can ask students’ families for their participation (this is most likely only possible for K-12 students.) Generally, families can monitor their child's progress and make sure that they do their assignments on time. This is especially helpful now that most students join schools from home. On top of that, families can provide moral and emotional support and encouragement to their children. Lastly, families can also make it easier for teachers to communicate with their students and learn about their needs so that the teachers can provide a sufficient online learning environment.

THE ECONOMIC POLICY

The Economic Policy Institute has created a plan to tackle these problems that have arisen with remote learning. The plan describes “three Rs:” relief, recovery, and rebuilding (Garcia & Weiss, 2020). The idea is to first fund schools during the pandemic to help improve the effectiveness of remote learning (relief). Next, investments in education will be made to help bring those affected by the pandemic up to speed (recovery). Finally, the idea is to build a foundation upon which later school shutdowns can be managed better and help schools understand every aspect of students’ abilities and limitations (rebuilding) (Garcia & Weiss, 2020).

Regarding the relief stage, the Economic Policy Institute has provided a checklist of the minimum that needs to be done to help students during the pandemic. They call for schools and community institutions to help feed children and provide more counseling and mental health support. Schools must come up with ways to monitor student needs to not only help those students, but also to be able to provide what students need to learn. Schools must be able to provide technical support, providing both internet access and devices to do schoolwork on to narrow the digital divide and disadvantages of lower quality devices. Care needs to be taken towards those with special-needs and English learners over the pandemic, along with any others that may need more specialized help.

The recovery stage will help affected students get back to their expected rate of academic progress from before the pandemic. Schools should waive performance assessments in favor of diagnostic and curriculum-based exams. This will help both students and schools; the data gathered from diagnostic assessments can be used to evaluate the extent to which this recovery phase needs to be done. Large number of teachers are pressured to leave schools due to the problems that the pandemic has caused, so strategies must be put in place to retain these teachers. Better technological and well-thought-out systems need to be put in place to accommodate both students’ and teachers’ emotional learning and bonds. One of the most important factors to keep in mind for schools and teachers during this recovery phase is that both the quality of learning and the quantity of learning time must be increased to make up for the academic progress lost due to this loss of quality and quantity over the pandemic.

As for the rebuilding phase, institutions should take advantage of this opportunity to restructure the education system and reimagine approaches to learning. This is a chance to provide better systems for more effective learning and help improve the accessibility of education to narrow the educational achievement gap across socioeconomic levels and between those of different backgrounds. Schools must start paying more attention to the unique skills of students and nuances of learning. By rewarding the development of cognitive and socioemotional skills, many opportunity gaps can be narrowed, not favoring any one type of student, and going forward with a whole-child approach. By accounting for the impact of poverty and racial/socioeconomic divides, schools can help take the first steps towards a whole-child approach in education.

POLICY RECOMMENDATIONS FOR EDUCATIONAL INSTITUTIONS

To prevent the COVID-19 Pandemic from becoming a multi-generational disaster in education, new policies are urgently needed. The biggest international campaign to support countries facing the educational challenges caused by COVID-19 is the Save Our Future program, powered by The LEGO Foundation. This campaign is guided by some of the biggest humanitarian organizations, such as the United Nations International Children's Emergency Fund (UNICEF), The United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization (UNESCO), The United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees (UNHCR), The World Food Programme (WFP), Association for the Development of Education in Africa (ADEA), and Education Above All. All of the policies proposed in this paper are based on the guidelines that were created by the United Nations and the education community (United Nations Sustainable Development Group, 2020). These recommended educational policies can be divided into multiple categories: mitigating the transmission of the virus while reopening the schools, precise allocation of educational funds to reduce the impact of the virus, strengthening the resilience of education for all areas while keeping the education quality the same, restructuring the classical education systems while increasing the positive change in teaching and learning.

The most important first step for reopening the schools is mitigating the transmission of the virus. To return to the state of education before the pandemic, first the spread of the virus must be limited. Institutions need to provide the necessary safety measures to ensure the physical and mental health of their students. This could include creating more handwashing and hand-sanitizer stations, providing a nurse at each school who can conduct COVID-19 testing, instituting smaller class sizes or alternating schedules to prevent the crowding, etc. Institutions need to listen to the concerns of all students no matter their age and grade. It will also be safer for institutions to go a step beyond what the local health officials are recommending instead of following the bare minimum safety recommendations.

The second set of policy recommendations address the need for districts and higher-level educational institutions to precisely allocate the funds they receive from the local/federal government and donors to reduce COVID’s impact on the quality of education. Because of the recession brought on by the pandemic and subsequent lockdowns, governments are spending more money for the safety of the general public and in some cases, especially in third-world countries that do not possess the capital to provide resources to all of its sectors at the same time. This has involuntarily led to a decrease in funding for educational institutions (United Nations Sustainable Development Group, 2020). It is not an easy task for governments to increase their taxes or their tax base, so educational institutions need to utilize the capital that is given to them carefully. The educational community has been trying to keep this funding decrease to a minimum, but if and when they cannot prevent the loss of funds, institutions could limit the funding for clubs and other activities. The education sector could use their powerful voice on a national level to raise their concerns about this issue as well.

Institutions need to strengthen the resilience of education for all areas while keeping the quality of education the same. While transitioning to online learning, one important mistake that all educational institutions made to varying degrees was the lack of preparedness for emergency situations like pandemics and natural disasters. Focusing on equality and inclusivity in online learning could help schools be more resilient in the future. If institutions want to strengthen their curriculums and class structure, they need to understand the problems of all people instead of the problems of the privileged. The needs of the learners who are under threat of losing their education must be prioritized. If schools could tackle these problems in this way, both the students and the school itself would see many benefits. The second policy that institutions could adopt is reinforcing the capacity of risk management and emergency help groups (United Nations Sustainable Development Group, 2020). Schools need to be able to create risk management and emergency help groups at every level: individual, organizational, and institutional. These groups need to be able to develop and implement contingency plans. Finally, schools must ensure that there is strong leadership, coordination, and easy consultation. Any school board can create policies and goals, but to implement the policies and goals that are created, strong leaders will have to manage and coordinate with their co-workers to meet these policy goals. Coordination will also give the inputs needed from the students, teacher, and admin.

Finally, the classical education sector will need to be restructured for positive change in teaching and learning. Since the beginning of the pandemic, the flaws of our current system have been made apparent all over the world. Closures of every type of educational institution make it clear that there can be no simple return to life before COVID-19. The report from the United Nations on this matter is clear and definite, and these are some of the policy proposals suggested by the report. School boards need to focus on mitigating the learning losses of the individuals and prevent potential student dropout, integrate programs for students to learn skills in the sector that they would like to focus on such as employability programs (United Nations Sustainable Development Group, 2020). Furthermore, educational institutions need to provide connectivity to all their students, remove the barriers for accessibility to technology, and improve data accessibility. Even if students are in self-paced learning environments, they need to be treated as students not customers. Lastly, educational institutions need to support the articulation and flexibility at every level.

WHY IS IT IMPORTANT TO MAKE SURE THAT ONLINE LEARNING IS EFFECTIVE FOR EVERY STUDENT IN THE CLASSROOM?

Sooner or later, this current situation with COVID-19 will come to an end. The world will go back to how it was, and students will finally be able to go back to school. Although a small number of students will still be learning online, a majority of the students will be back to face-to-face learning. But then, students and educators both will have to contend with the learning losses caused by the pandemic disruption. Second, and more importantly, because the current situation has obviously made it harder for schools to teach their students, if students were still able to learn in online environments during these difficult times, they could be even better students once this pandemic ends and they go back to school. Assuring that education during COVID-19 is effective will only strengthen the forms of education that emerge from this pandemic, making them more impactful (Vegas & Winthrop, 2020). As stated before, learning during the pandemic has been difficult on most students. If schools and teachers were to make sure that students learned everything they need to learn by creating the best online environments for their students and by supporting them as much as they can, students could emerge from this pandemic as better learners. On top of that, due to COVID-19, many students have had to acquire new skills through the extraordinary experiences brought by the pandemic. For example, many students, especially those from low socioeconomic status, have to stay at home either by themselves or with their siblings without their parents being there. Also, in these cases, older siblings have had to learn how to take care of their siblings at home and learn at the same time. These skills are important for learners and can help them learn in future scenarios, especially college, if they choose to attend, where they have to learn by themselves without the help and support of others. Additionally, if teachers support their students with acquiring self-regulated learning skills, these skills can also be very beneficial in almost every classroom setting. If all of the students were to learn beneficial new skills during their experiences with the pandemic and online learning, education could improve to a great extent after the pandemic (Carter et al., 2020). Furthermore, if teachers strive for parent involvement in their children's education, the parents’ involvement can continue after the pandemic which can also be very beneficial for students. This is why it is very important for schools and teachers to make sure that they can create effective online learning environments that support every student regardless of their socioeconomic status (Auguste et al., 2009).

The decreased learning time due to the pandemic will almost certainly hinder student performance, some groups more than others. Student academic advancement has been estimated to increase on average anywhere from 0.3 to 0.7 standard deviations throughout a school year (Garcia & Weiss 2020). The 2019-2020 school year was cut short due to the pandemic, and the Economic Policy Institute estimates a loss of at least 0.1 standard deviations in academic performance. This indicates that students lost at least one-third of their expected academic progression due to the pandemic in early 2020. However, this regression in learning shouldn’t necessarily be extrapolated to the 2020-2021 school year. COVID-19 came as a surprise over the spring of 2020, and emergency remote learning had to take place. Schools and teachers had much more time to plan and prepare for the next school year, so decreased learning time should be less prevalent, and its effect on education less imposing.

Student disengagement and especially chronic absenteeism are also factors that could significantly hurt students over the pandemic. There are indications that the negative relationship between absenteeism and student outcome becomes more pronounced the more days students miss school (Balfanz et al., 2020). This is worrying in that absenteeism promoted by the pandemic due to the easier aversion of consequences makes the repercussions of decreased learning time much more intense. Chronic absenteeism especially seems to be a primary indicator of lower academic performance and higher chances of dropping out. The United Nations has called the expected sharp increase in dropout rates to be a “generational catastrophe.”

It turns out that teachers need help as well. Weak support systems have failed to coherently help adapt teachers to an online environment and left them unequipped to teach over the pandemic. While just over two-thirds of public-school teachers have gotten instruction on adapting to a virtual environment (which is still concerningly low), more teachers found it to be only somewhat useful, and in some cases, not useful at all. Most teachers have never contemplated online learning before the pandemic, let alone attempted or done it. Even if technically proficient teachers have gotten over the obstacle of technical issues, many teachers were inexperienced in how to conduct teaching online and were left to come up with teaching plans and curriculum on the fly, possibly damaging the quality of education. Teachers also have to deal with the difficulty of creating emotional bonds virtually. There are emotional bonds critical to learning and the online environment makes it especially hard for the teachers to form meaningful relationships with their students.

CONCLUSION

The emergency online learning brought on by COVID-19 has brought on many unanticipated problems. From an accelerating gap in educational achievement between different socioeconomic levels to challenges posed by navigating online environments, most of the research points to the failure of emergency online learning and the disregard of schools’ emergency policies. Fortunately, there are many things teachers and schools can do to improve the quality and accessibility of remote learning. Teachers may employ varying moderating variables to fit the conditions of their classrooms and schools. Many strategies can make online learning just as effective as face-to-face learning through means such as allowing more access to external resources and through the use of learner supports. Teachers and parents can allocate some of their own time to monitor students’ skills and motivations. Procrastination can be prevented through the constant supervision of both teachers and parents and slightly stricter deadlines. Administrators can monitor the leniency of teachers and the progress of students by adjusting school policy with a top-down approach. More allocation of funds towards technology can help greatly improve the accessibility of virtual classrooms to students, especially those that are less inclined to have them in their households. By taking into account all of these remedies, both parents and teachers can help improve the efficacy and accessibility of online learning. These solutions can help improve the productivity of our economy and help provide a better future for all. As we have already mentioned, if we don’t act now, the education achievement gap may leave those already behind in the dust. Our students’ futures are at stake.

KEY TERMS AND DEFINITIONS

Achievement Gap: This refers to the gap between the academic success and advancement between students of certain socioeconomic levels.

Asynchronous Learning: This refers to learning where students engage in learning in separate times and places.

Chronic Absenteeism: This refers to missing a large number of school days (generally 10 percent of the school days or more) throughout the year.

Emergency Online Learning: This refers to the emergency shift from in person education to online education in early 2020.

Face-to-Face Learning: This refers to learning that happens in an environment where the students and the instructor/teacher are physically in the same place.

Procrastination: This refers to delaying schoolwork till its due date.

Remote Learning: This refers to learning that happens in an environment where the students and instructors/teacher are not in the same place.

Self-Regulated Learning Skills: This refers to the skills that enable someone to understand and control their own learning such as time management.

Student Motivation: This refers to students’ willingness to learn and be a part of the class.

Synchronous Learning: This refers to learning where students engage in learning together at the same time.
